✅ **Fact:** the import-substituting industrialisation strategy, based on heavy protection, was effective in deepening and widening India's industrial base and giving the economy freedom from foreign dependence. Over time, excessive protection through import restrictions began to produce inefficiency and technological backwardness.

The control regime's specific defects are worth stating individually, because a list of named mechanisms beats a general complaint about the "licence raj".

| Instrument | Intended purpose | Perverse effect recorded by the source |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Industrial licensing | Direct investment to planned priorities | Cut off domestic competition, as import control cut off external competition |
| ✅ MRTP Act | Restrain monopoly | Went against economies of scale and punished efficiency: a firm that grew through good management or research faced refusal of permission once it crossed a limit |
| ✅ Small-scale reservation | Protect employment-intensive producers | Excluded reserved areas from scale and from research and development, made the sector internationally uncompetitive and gave entrepreneurs an incentive to stay small |
| ✅ No exit route | Protect jobs | From the mid-1970s, closure or retrenchment required government permission; man-days lost rose from an index of 100 in 1961 to 891.6 in 1980; the state took over sick units, and the National Textile Corporation became a major contributor to public-sector losses |
| ✅ Public sector | Build core capacity | Political and bureaucratic pressure made most units loss-making: overstaffed, headed by political appointees, unable to enforce efficiency accountability; state electricity boards and road transport corporations were notorious for losses |

✅ **Fact:** the efficiency cost is measurable. The incremental capital-output ratio - the capital used per unit of additional output - rose from a little over **2.0 during the First Plan** to **3.6 during the Third Plan**, and by one estimate touched **5.76 between 1971 and 1976**; it began declining in the 1980s but remained around 4 in the 1990s. In the 1980s the simple average rate of financial return on employed capital in public-sector enterprises was about **2.5 per cent**, and much lower if the 14 petroleum enterprises, which accounted for 77 per cent of 1989-90 profits, are excluded.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the incremental capital-output ratio is the single most efficient fact in this lesson, because it explains an apparent paradox: investment rates rose while growth rates did not. More was being spent to obtain less. A striking illustration the source records is a 1965 study showing a public-sector heavy-electricals plant set up with a capital-output ratio of between 12 and 14 - with no questions asked and no enquiry set up.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the source's sharpest judgement is that controls adopted in the name of socialist principles and equity "ended up building a distorted, backward capitalism", because they violated the requirement of continuous expansion through innovation and efficient investment. That is a left critique of the control regime, not a market-liberal one, and citing it protects an answer from looking ideological.

### 2. Weakness two - the opening India did not take

✅ **Fact:** between the 1950s and 1970s world output of manufactures increased four times while world trade in manufactures increased **ten times**; the share of world produce exported doubled between 1965 and 1990; and the Third World's share of total industrial exports rose from about **5 per cent in 1970 to double that by 1983**.

✅ **Fact:** India's share of world exports fell from about **2.4 per cent in 1948 to 0.42 per cent in 1980**, recovering only to about **0.6 per cent by 1994**. The volume of India's manufactured exports in 1980-81 was half that of China, a third of Brazil's and a quarter of South Korea's. South Korea's manufactured exports, negligible in 1962, were four times India's by 1980; in 1990 Korea exported $41 billion of manufactures to the OECD countries against India's $9 billion.

✅ **Fact:** India did diversify exports by commodity composition - manufactures were two-thirds of exports in 1980-81, rising to three-quarters by 1989-90 - and by geography; the failure was in volume relative to world expansion.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the changes India missed were specific and datable. The character of foreign capital had altered: multinational firms had begun to source components globally and to relocate whole plants, with technology transfer and local multiplier effects, instead of creating colonial-style enclaves. India responded to that change by tightening restrictions in 1973.

### 3. Weakness three - the fiscal drift, and why it was political

✅ **Fact:** the erosion of fiscal prudence began after 1975 and particularly during the Janata government of 1977-79. Food subsidies doubled between 1975-76 and 1976-77, from Rs 2.5 billion to Rs 5 billion; the fertiliser subsidy multiplied tenfold from Rs 0.6 billion in 1976-77 to Rs 6.03 billion in 1979-80; the export subsidy multiplied about four and a half times between 1974-75 and 1978-79. Procurement prices were raised without corresponding increases in issue prices, input taxes were cut and budgetary transfers to loss-making public units increased. The economists Vijay Joshi and I.M.D. Little describe the **1979 budget** as "a watershed marking the change from previous fiscal conservatism".

✅ **Fact:** the drift continued through the 1980s, reaching the point at which the National Front government that came to power in 1989 announced a farm loan waiver estimated to cost the exchequer more than Rs 100 billion. Direct central subsidies on food, fertiliser and exports alone in 1980-81 have been estimated to exceed Rs 15 billion - equal to half of total gross capital formation in public-sector manufacturing that year.

✅ **Fact:** the consolidated fiscal deficit rose from **4.1 per cent of GDP in 1974-75** to **6.5 per cent in 1979-80**, **9.7 per cent in 1984-85** and a peak of **10.4 per cent in 1991**. The gap between public investment and public savings widened from 3.6 per cent of GDP in 1968-72 to 5.3 per cent in 1980-81 and 9 per cent by 1989-90. Government domestic debt rose from 31.8 per cent of GDP in 1974-75 to 45.7 per cent in 1984-85 and 54.6 per cent in 1989-90.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the causes the source gives are political, not technical, and they connect this lesson to the party-politics sessions without repeating them. As Congress dominance eroded, political competition became competitive populism; with declining internal party democracy, bargaining between social groups moved out of party structures and into budget allocations; and parties representing rich and middle peasants, in power in states after 1967 and influential at the Centre after 1977, secured sectional subsidies at the cost of an expenditure pattern suited to development.

### 4. The 1980s: growth that could not last

✅ **Fact:** the 1980s were genuinely a high-growth decade. Industrial growth averaged about 8 per cent a year; the so-called "Hindu rate of growth" of 3 to 3.5 per cent was broken and the economy grew at over 5.5 per cent; by one estimate average real GDP growth between 1980 and 1989 was about 6 per cent. Between 1985 and 1990 GDP grew at over 5.5 per cent a year, industry at over 7 per cent, capital goods at 10 per cent and consumer durables at 12 per cent.

✅ **Fact:** the source's verdict is nevertheless that this growth "was not a result of any step-up of savings and investment; in many ways it was a result of over-borrowing and over-spending" - debt-led, like Latin America in the 1970s, and driven by domestic budgetary expansion.

✅ **Fact:** the external position deteriorated accordingly. From a current-account surplus of $1.5 billion in 1977-78, India moved to a deficit of $2.9 billion in 1980-81, $3.5 billion in 1984-85 and $9.9 billion (3.5 per cent of GDP) in 1990-91. External debt rose from $23.5 billion in 1980-81 to $37.3 billion in 1985-86 and $83.8 billion in 1990-91; the debt-service ratio rose from a manageable 10.2 per cent in 1980-81 to a dangerous **35 per cent in 1990-91**; and the concessional share of total debt fell from over 80 per cent to about 40 per cent, meaning the debt was increasingly short-term commercial borrowing.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the source adds a point that decides many examination answers: the worsening of the late 1980s was **neither due to an external shock nor to import liberalisation**. The second half of the 1980s actually saw an improvement in the trade balance, with exports growing about 14 per cent a year in dollar terms. The deterioration came from the economy's saving-investment gap and the borrowing it required.

### 5. The crisis itself

✅ **Fact:** reserves fell from $5.85 billion in 1980-81 to $4.1 billion in 1989-90 and then by nearly half to **$2.24 billion in 1990-91** - about one month's import cover. The Iraqi invasion of Kuwait in August 1990 raised oil prices and cut Indian exports to the Gulf; India's credit rating was sharply downgraded; non-resident deposits in foreign exchange began to be withdrawn rapidly. With foreign lending virtually dried up, the government **sold 20 tonnes of gold to the Union Bank of Switzerland in March 1991**. By July 1991 reserves were down to about **two weeks of import cover** despite loans from the International Monetary Fund. The country was at the edge of default.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** 1991 was not an accident of the Gulf War. The Gulf War was the shock; the vulnerability was a decade of borrowing to finance a public saving-investment gap inside an economy that could not export its way out.

### Comparative treatment: what kind of crisis was it?

| Diagnosis | Implication | Evidence for | Evidence against |
|---|---|---|---|
| External shock | Temporary; manage and resume | Kuwait, oil prices, Gulf remittances | The deficit was already 3.5 per cent of GDP; trade balance had improved in the late 1980s |
| Liberalisation-caused | Reverse the opening | Rhetorically popular | Import liberalisation was limited; the borrowing came from the fiscal gap |
| Structural and fiscal | Requires changing instruments | Fiscal deficit series, debt composition shift, ICOR, export share | Requires accepting that domestic politics, not foreigners, caused the crisis |

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Calling the 1980s "debt-led growth" retrospectively delegitimises a decade in which poverty fell and industry grew; the crisis was triggered by events nobody controlled.

**Reply:** The growth and the poverty decline were real and the source records both. What the evidence does not support is the claim that the growth was self-financing. Public investment persistently exceeded public savings by amounts rising to 9 per cent of GDP by 1989-90, and the borrowing that filled the gap shifted from concessional to commercial. A growth path financed that way is not delegitimised by the label; it is simply dated, because the arithmetic has an end.

**Residual:** How much of the fiscal drift was avoidable under coalition-era political competition is genuinely contested. The source treats competitive populism as a systemic consequence of the erosion of one-party dominance rather than the fault of any single government, which makes the counterfactual a political question rather than an economic one.

### UPSC application

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this lesson supplies the *diagnosis* half of any question on the 1991 reforms - and diagnosis is where marks concentrate, because most candidates can describe what was done in 1991 and few can explain why it had become unavoidable. The three-weakness structure works as an answer skeleton: inefficiency without competition; a missed external opening; fiscal drift converting both into a solvency crisis. Keep the present-day reform agenda out; it belongs to Economy.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Blaming the crisis on the Gulf War; that was the trigger, not the cause.
- Blaming import liberalisation; the source explicitly rules it out for the late 1980s.
- Treating the 1980s as stagnation; growth was about 6 per cent and the "Hindu rate of growth" was broken.
- Quoting the fiscal deficit without its year - the figure moves from 4.1 to 10.4 per cent across seventeen years.
- Forgetting the composition shift in debt from concessional to commercial, which is what made the debt-service ratio of 35 per cent dangerous.
- Describing the public sector as simply loss-making without the mechanisms - political appointment, overstaffing, absent accountability, populist pricing.

### Revision notes

1. Three weakness families: inefficiency from protection and controls; the missed external opening; fiscal drift.
2. MRTP punished scale; small-scale reservation blocked scale and research; licensing blocked entry; from the mid-1970s there was no exit.
3. ICOR rose from a little over 2.0 (First Plan) to 3.6 (Third Plan) and about 5.76 in 1971-76; public-sector return on capital averaged about 2.5 per cent in the 1980s.
4. World trade in manufactures grew tenfold while output grew fourfold; India's share of world exports fell from 2.4 per cent (1948) to 0.42 per cent (1980) and 0.6 per cent (1994).
5. Manufactures rose to two-thirds of exports by 1980-81 and three-quarters by 1989-90 - diversification without volume.
6. Fiscal relaxation from about 1975; the 1979 budget described as the watershed; loan waiver of over Rs 100 billion announced in 1990.
7. Fiscal deficit: 4.1 per cent (1974-75), 6.5 per cent (1979-80), 9.7 per cent (1984-85), 10.4 per cent (1991).
8. Public investment-savings gap: 3.6 per cent (1968-72) to 9 per cent (1989-90); domestic debt 31.8 to 54.6 per cent of GDP.
9. 1980s growth about 6 per cent; but debt-led and spending-led, not savings-led.
10. External debt $23.5bn (1980-81) to $83.8bn (1990-91); debt service 10.2 to 35 per cent; concessional share above 80 per cent falling to about 40 per cent.
11. Reserves $2.24 billion in 1990-91; 20 tonnes of gold sold in March 1991; about two weeks of cover by July 1991.
12. The late-1980s deterioration was neither an external shock nor import liberalisation; the trade balance had improved.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes that the 1991 crisis was caused by the Gulf War and by import liberalisation. Correct both claims using dated evidence, and state what actually caused it.

**Model answer:** Both claims fail. The Gulf crisis of August 1990 raised oil prices, cut exports to the Gulf and triggered withdrawals of non-resident deposits, but the current-account deficit had already reached $9.9 billion, about 3.5 per cent of GDP, in 1990-91 after rising steadily from a surplus of $1.5 billion in 1977-78; a shock of a few months cannot produce a deterioration of that shape. Import liberalisation cannot be the cause either, because the second half of the 1980s saw the trade balance improve, with exports growing about 14 per cent a year in dollar terms. The actual cause was the economy's saving-investment gap and the borrowing that financed it: the public investment-savings gap widened to about 9 per cent of GDP by 1989-90, the consolidated fiscal deficit reached 10.4 per cent in 1991, external debt rose from $23.5 billion in 1980-81 to $83.8 billion in 1990-91, the concessional share of that debt fell from over 80 per cent to about 40 per cent, and the debt-service ratio rose from 10.2 to 35 per cent. Reserves of $2.24 billion in 1990-91 then fell to about two weeks of import cover by July 1991.

**Misconception to avoid:** Confusing a trigger with a cause. The test is whether the proposed cause can explain the shape and duration of the deterioration; a single external event cannot explain a decade-long widening of a structural gap.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

Explain why India's high growth in the 1980s did not prevent the crisis of 1991. (10 marks, 150 words)

#### Model answer

The 1980s growth was real but wrongly financed. GDP grew at over 5.5 per cent, industry at about 8 per cent and capital goods at 10 per cent. It was not, however, financed by a step-up in savings: public investment exceeded public savings by 5.3 per cent of GDP in 1980-81 and about 9 per cent by 1989-90, and the consolidated fiscal deficit rose from 6.5 per cent in 1979-80 to 9.7 per cent in 1984-85.

The gap was filled by borrowing whose composition worsened. External debt rose from $23.5 billion in 1980-81 to $83.8 billion in 1990-91; the concessional share fell from above 80 per cent to about 40 per cent; the debt-service ratio rose from 10.2 to 35 per cent.

Growth financed by borrowing without productivity gains - the incremental capital-output ratio remained near 4 - postpones a crisis rather than preventing it.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the growth conceded with figures 2 + the financing mechanism named and quantified 3 + debt composition shift 3 + the productivity qualification 1 + verdict distinguishing postponement from prevention 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 6:** If the model had reached its arithmetic limit, the question is what was actually changed in 1991 - and whether that change abandoned the goals of 1947 or pursued them with different instruments.

## Lesson 6 - 1991: crisis, reform, and the "correction, not repudiation" thesis

Progress: 6/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: what was reformed, what it produced by the late 1990s, and where the historian's competence ends

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the survey history's chapter on economic reforms since 1991, read in full, with the canonical advanced file for the framing of reform as correction rather than surrender
CA search: "1991 economic reforms anniversary India liberalisation debate disinvestment public sector news 2026"
CA found: Reported - anniversary commentary through 2026 records eightfold GDP expansion since 1991 alongside persistent informality and weak manufacturing employment, and argues that a further reform agenda must address public services, regulation and human capital rather than repeat the 1991 instruments
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: what changed, what did not, and what the historian may claim

```text
   THE 1991 PACKAGE                                   WHAT IT DID NOT TOUCH
   ------------------------------------------         ------------------------------
   immediate fiscal correction                        labour-market reform
   exchange rate linked to the market                 exit for loss-making enterprises
     (~20 per cent devaluation at the outset)         state utilities: power, transport
   trade liberalisation; freer access to imports      subsidies: food, fertiliser, fuel
   dismantling of industrial licensing                     (all rose after 1991)
   abolition of MRTP
   public-sector reform incl. gradual privatisation
   capital-market and financial-sector reform
   restrictions on multinationals removed;
     foreign direct investment welcomed
                    |
   GOALS UNCHANGED: growth, self-reliance, poverty removal
   INSTRUMENTS CHANGED: from control to competition
                    |
   HISTORIAN'S LIMIT: the record to about 1999-2000.
   Everything after that belongs to the Economy owner.
```

*The distinction between goals and instruments is the whole thesis of this lesson, and it is the sentence that converts a description into an argument.*

### 1. Who reformed, and why then

✅ **Fact:** the minority Congress government of Narasimha Rao took office in June 1991 with Manmohan Singh as finance minister and attempted one of the most important economic reforms since independence.

✅ **Fact:** the need had been recognised far earlier. Manmohan Singh had argued in the early 1960s that India's export pessimism was unjustified and advised more openness and less control; Jagdish Bhagwati was among the early advocates of reform. An attempt in the mid-1960s was stymied. The 1970s saw "reform by stealth", with the rupee allowed to depreciate by being pegged to a depreciating sterling rather than by outright devaluation. Indira Gandhi after 1980 brought in deregulation of industrial licensing and reduced restrictions on large enterprises. Rajiv Gandhi from 1984 attempted faster industrial deregulation, exchange-rate flexibility and partial lifting of import controls - but left the macroeconomic imbalance unattended, did not touch financial, labour or public-sector reform, and abandoned even those measures amid the political crisis of the later 1980s.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is why 1991 is a *rupture* and the 1980s measures are *preparation*. The examination point is precise: Rajiv Gandhi did not launch the reforms, and saying he did confuses partial deregulation with a change of regime. The earlier sessions teach the politics; this lesson supplies the economic distinction.

✅ **Fact:** the source's explanation for why comprehensive reform waited for 1991 is political-economic: governments in vulnerable positions avoided painful measures; vested interests in the bureaucracy and in sections of business that earned rents from controls resisted; and an ideological opposition from the orthodox left obstructed. The crisis broke through all three at the moment when both the ideological opposition and the vested interests were weakest.

### 2. The reform package, stated exactly

✅ **Fact:** the process begun in 1991 involved an immediate fiscal correction; making the exchange rate more realistically linked to the market, with the rupee undergoing about a **20 per cent devaluation** at the outset; liberalisation of trade and industrial controls including freer access to imports; considerable dismantling of industrial licensing and the **abolition of MRTP**; reform of the public sector including gradual privatisation; reform of capital markets and the financial sector; and removal of a large number of restrictions on multinational corporations and foreign investment, with foreign direct investment actively welcomed.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** read that list against Lesson 5's list of weaknesses and the correspondence is almost item for item. That correspondence is the strongest available argument that 1991 was a targeted correction of identified defects rather than an externally imposed template.

### 3. The record of the first years

| Indicator | Before | After |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ GDP growth | 0.8 per cent in the crisis year 1991-92 | 5.3 per cent (1992-93), 6.2 per cent (1993-94), then over 7.5 per cent averaged across the next three years |
| ✅ Plan average | Seventh Plan (1985-90): 6 per cent | Eighth Plan (1992-97): 6.94 per cent |
| ✅ Savings | Seventh Plan average 20.6 per cent | Over 23 per cent, 1991-97 |
| ✅ Industrial production | Under 1 per cent in 1991-92, negative in manufacturing | 2.3 per cent (1992-93), 6 per cent (1993-94), 12.8 per cent (1995-96) |
| ✅ Capital goods | Negative for some years | About 25 per cent growth in 1994-95 |
| ✅ Central fiscal deficit | 8.3 per cent of GDP in 1990-91 | About 6 per cent average, 1992-97; primary deficit down from 4.3 per cent (1990-91) to 0.6 per cent (1996-97) |
| ✅ Exports | Declined 1.5 per cent in dollar terms in 1991-92 | About 20 per cent average growth, 1993-96 |
| ✅ Current-account deficit | 3.2 per cent of GDP in 1990-91 | 0.4 per cent (1993-94), 1.6 per cent (1995-96); 1991-98 average about 1.1 per cent |
| ✅ Reserves | About two weeks of cover in July 1991 | $30.4 billion at end-January 1999, about seven months of cover |
| ✅ External debt | Peak 41 per cent of GDP in 1991-92; debt service 35.3 per cent in 1990-91 | 28.7 per cent of GDP in 1995-96; debt service 19.5 per cent in 1997-98 |
| ✅ Foreign direct investment | $129 million in 1991-92 | $2.1 billion in 1995-96; total foreign investment $102 million (1990-91) to $4.9 billion (1995-96) |

✅ **Fact:** the source's judgement is that India performed one of the fastest recoveries from a deep macroeconomic crisis, and that the structural adjustment was achieved with relatively minimal pain, without the prolonged recession, mass unemployment and deterioration of the condition of the poor that had occurred in several other economies attempting adjustment.

### 4. The poverty question, answered with its own evidence

✅ **Fact:** the poverty ratio had already fallen from **51.3 per cent in 1977-78 to 38.9 per cent in 1987-88** and stood at about **36 per cent in 1993-94**. Poverty, mainly rural, rose significantly only in **1992-93**, and the source attributes the causation mainly to a drought and fall in foodgrain output in 1991-92 that raised food prices, and only very weakly to the stabilisation programme.

✅ **Fact:** the government was nevertheless criticised, including by supporters of reform, for failing to anticipate the situation and for not protecting anti-poverty expenditure in real terms in 1991-92. By 1993-94 poverty indicators were significantly better than in 1992 - lower by nearly six percentage points and below the pre-reform average of the five years 1986-87 to 1990-91. Expenditure on social services and rural development rose from 7.8 per cent of total central expenditure in 1992-93 to nearly 10 per cent on average between 1993 and 1998. Real agricultural wages, which fell 6.2 per cent in 1991-92, grew over 5 per cent a year for the next two years and surpassed pre-reform levels by 1993-94. Additional employment averaged about 6.3 million jobs a year between 1992-93 and 1994-95, against an average annual increase of 4.8 million in the 1980s. Inflation, at 17 per cent in August 1991, was brought below 5 per cent by February 1996.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the honest formulation is narrow and defensible: on the evidence available to the source, stabilisation had little negative effect on poverty, the one bad year is better explained by drought than by adjustment, and the failure to protect rural expenditure in 1991-92 was a real and avoidable error. Nothing in that formulation requires a candidate to endorse or attack liberalisation.

### 5. What reform did not do

✅ **Fact:** the incomplete agenda is recorded as clearly as the achievement. No serious effort was made to raise public savings or cut expenditure; the public saving-investment gap averaged 7.1 per cent of GDP between 1992 and 1996; the food subsidy rose from Rs 28.5 billion in 1991-92 to Rs 61.14 billion in 1996-97 and the fertiliser subsidy from Rs 32.01 billion in 1988-89 to Rs 62.35 billion in 1995-96 - the agricultural economist C.H. Hanumantha Rao noting in 1992 that the fertiliser subsidy alone nearly equalled the annual central and state outlay on agriculture. Subsidies on diesel, kerosene and cooking gas came to Rs 93.6 billion in 1995-96, and the oil-pool deficit in 1996-97 was Rs 98 billion, with a cumulative deficit of about Rs 155 billion. State utilities were not reformed; labour-market reform and exit possibilities were not created.

✅ **Fact:** momentum slowed from 1997: GDP growth decelerated to 5 per cent in 1997-98 from 7.8 per cent in 1996-97; export growth slowed for a third successive year and turned negative in April-December 1998-99; the primary deficit more than doubled from 0.6 per cent of GDP in 1996-97 to 1.3 per cent in 1997-98; selective acceptance of the Fifth Pay Commission's recommendations in 1997 raised salary expenditure without the compensating savings the Commission had proposed; the East Asian crisis reduced world trade growth and capital flows; and the economic sanctions following the 1998 nuclear tests had a dampening effect.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the pattern is the same one this topic keeps finding. Reforms that required the state to stop doing something it controlled - licensing, MRTP approvals, import restrictions - were accomplished. Reforms that required the state to withdraw a benefit from an organised constituency - subsidies, free or under-priced power, protected employment - were not. The political-geometry thesis of Lesson 1 applies to liberalisation exactly as it applies to land reform.

### 6. Correction or repudiation? The thesis, stated carefully

✅ **Fact:** the standard reading of this period presents the 1991 reforms **not as surrender** but as a corrective to excessive dirigisme, retaining the goals of growth, self-reliance and poverty removal while changing the instruments. The source adds that a broad consensus in favour of continuing reform emerged across parties from right to left, "reminiscent of the one around the Nehruvian programme at independence", and notes that many who had created or subscribed to the earlier strategy came to accept the need for reform.

✅ **Fact:** the source states the historical defence of the earlier strategy in the same breath: it "gave the Indian economy a certain depth and spread, increased its bargaining power and independence", and the shift was required because negative features had developed and world conditions had changed - if self-reliance in the 1950s required import substitution, by the 1990s it required capital and technology flows and competitive productivity.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the strongest way to use this in an answer is as a test rather than a slogan. If 1991 were a repudiation, the declared objectives would have changed; they did not. If it were a surrender to external agencies, the reforming coalition would have been narrow and externally sponsored; instead it eventually included the Communist-supported common minimum programme's own target for foreign direct investment. What did change was the theory of how the objectives were to be reached.

### Comparative treatment: three readings of 1991

| Reading | Claim | Its best evidence | Its difficulty |
|---|---|---|---|
| Correction | Instruments changed, goals retained | Continuity of declared objectives; cross-party consensus; the 1980s preparation | Must explain why the correction took thirteen years longer than China's 1978 turn |
| Surrender | Reform imposed by external agencies | The crisis timing and the Fund's role | Reform proposals long predate the crisis and came from Indian economists; foreign capital remained modest |
| Deferred inevitability | The 1966 attempt was the real turning point and 1991 completed it | The conditionality episode; "reform by stealth" in the 1970s | Understates how much more comprehensive 1991 was |

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Describing 1991 as a correction understates the discontinuity. Abolishing the MRTP Act and welcoming multinationals reversed, not corrected, the 1969-73 settlement.

**Reply:** The objection is right about the instruments and wrong about the frame. The 1969-73 measures were themselves instruments adopted to secure autonomy in specific conditions - hostile conditionality, marginal export capacity, a foreign sector that had to be contained. When Indian industry supplied over 90 per cent of its own equipment needs and foreign firms accounted for only about a tenth of factory-sector value added, the same objective no longer required those instruments. A means abandoned once its purpose is secured is not a reversal of the purpose.

**Residual:** Whether the goals genuinely survived is testable only over a longer period than this session covers, and the test involves data after 2000 that belong to the Economy owner. The historian's defensible claim stops at the record to the end of the 1990s, and should say so.

### UPSC application

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the boundary is itself examinable. A history answer on 1991 should deliver: the accumulated causes; the crisis sequence; the package; the measured first-decade results; the unfinished agenda; and the correction-versus-repudiation judgement. It should not deliver current disinvestment policy, current fiscal rules or present-day trade agreements. A candidate who observes the boundary explicitly - "the subsequent record belongs to contemporary economic policy" - demonstrates command rather than ignorance.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Crediting the 1991 reforms to Rajiv Gandhi; his measures prepared, and the rupture was Rao's.
- Describing 1991 as the abandonment of self-reliance; the goal was retained and redefined as competitiveness.
- Forgetting that the Rao government was a **minority** government, which is what makes the reform politically remarkable.
- Claiming poverty rose because of reform; the 1992-93 rise is attributed mainly to the 1991-92 drought, and the 1993-94 level was below the pre-reform five-year average.
- Presenting reform as complete; subsidies rose, utilities were untouched and labour reform did not occur.
- Carrying the account past the late 1990s into present policy.

### Revision notes

1. Reform began in 1991 under the Rao minority government with Manmohan Singh as finance minister.
2. Antecedents: mid-1960s attempt stymied; 1970s "reform by stealth" via a depreciating peg; Indira Gandhi's deregulation after 1980; Rajiv Gandhi's partial measures, abandoned in the political crisis.
3. Package: fiscal correction; market-linked exchange rate with about 20 per cent devaluation; trade liberalisation; delicensing; abolition of MRTP; public-sector reform; capital-market and financial reform; opening to foreign investment.
4. GDP: 0.8 per cent (1991-92) to 5.3, 6.2 and then over 7.5 per cent; Eighth Plan average 6.94 per cent against the Seventh's 6 per cent.
5. Industrial production peaked at 12.8 per cent in 1995-96; capital goods about 25 per cent in 1994-95.
6. Reserves $30.4 billion by January 1999, about seven months of cover; debt-service ratio 35.3 per cent (1990-91) to 19.5 per cent (1997-98).
7. Foreign direct investment $129 million (1991-92) to $2.1 billion (1995-96).
8. Poverty: 51.3 per cent (1977-78), 38.9 per cent (1987-88), about 36 per cent (1993-94); a rise in 1992-93 attributed mainly to drought.
9. Real agricultural wages fell 6.2 per cent in 1991-92, then grew over 5 per cent a year and passed pre-reform levels by 1993-94.
10. Unfinished: public savings, subsidies, state utilities, labour reform, exit.
11. Slowdown from 1997; Fifth Pay Commission selective acceptance; East Asian crisis; post-1998 sanctions.
12. The established framing: a correction of excessive dirigisme, not a repudiation - goals retained, instruments changed.

### Concept check

**Question:** State the strongest evidence for and against the proposition that 1991 represented continuity of purpose with 1947, and give your qualified verdict.

**Model answer:** For continuity: the declared objectives - growth, self-reliance and the removal of poverty - were not replaced, and the reforming consensus eventually stretched across parties, with even the Communist-supported common minimum programme adopting a target for foreign direct investment; the package also mapped item by item onto defects the earlier model had itself generated, which is the signature of a correction rather than of a substitution; and much of the reform agenda had been argued by Indian economists since the early 1960s, long before any external conditionality. Against continuity: the instruments were reversed rather than adjusted - the MRTP Act was abolished, licensing dismantled and the restrictions on foreign capital built in 1973 were lifted, so the operational content of "self-reliance" changed from insulation to competitiveness. The qualified verdict is that purpose persisted while the theory of how to achieve it changed: by the 1990s India met over ninety per cent of its own equipment needs and foreign firms accounted for only about a tenth of factory-sector value added, so the instruments of insulation had already delivered what they were built for and their retention was no longer serving the goal.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating a change of instruments as automatic evidence of a change of goals. The test is whether the objective was abandoned or redefined; here it was redefined, and the redefinition followed a demonstrated failure of the previous instruments rather than an external instruction.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

"The economic reforms of 1991 corrected the Nehruvian model rather than repudiating it." Critically examine. (20 marks, 250 words)

#### Model answer

The proposition is largely defensible, with one important reservation.

It is defensible first because the goals did not change. Growth, self-reliance and poverty removal remained the declared objectives; what changed was the theory of how to reach them, with self-reliance redefined from insulation to competitive productivity.

Second, the package corresponded to defects the model itself had produced. Licensing had killed domestic competition and import restrictions external competition; the MRTP Act penalised scale; small-scale reservation blocked research and scale; there was no exit; public-sector returns averaged about 2.5 per cent in the 1980s. The 1991 measures - delicensing, abolition of MRTP, trade liberalisation, a market-linked exchange rate with about 20 per cent devaluation, financial and capital-market reform and an opening to foreign direct investment - addressed those defects item by item.

Third, insulation had already achieved its purpose: imported equipment had fallen to 9 per cent of fixed investment in equipment by 1974.

Fourth, the results were rapid: growth from 0.8 per cent in 1991-92 to over 7.5 per cent averaged in the mid-1990s; the debt-service ratio down from 35.3 to 19.5 per cent; reserves of $30.4 billion by January 1999.

The reservation is that reform was selective. Subsidies rose after 1991, state utilities were untouched, and labour reform and exit did not occur - the state withdrew where it controlled rules and did not withdraw where an organised constituency benefited.

Reform therefore corrected the model where correction was politically costless and left untouched what was not.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = goals-versus-instruments distinction argued, not asserted 4 + point-by-point correspondence between defects and measures 5 + at least four dated outcome figures 4 + the autonomy-already-achieved argument 3 + the selectivity reservation with subsidy or utility evidence 3 + qualified verdict 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 7:** The economic strand has now run from design to correction. The land strand runs on a different logic entirely, and its first component is the only large redistribution independent India ever completed.

## Lesson 7 - Land reform I: why zamindari abolition succeeded

Progress: 7/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: constitutional protection, the abolition Acts, compensation, the loopholes, and who actually gained

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the survey history's chapter on land reforms covering zamindari abolition and tenancy reform, read in full, with the canonical basic file's disaggregated land bank and its factual-risk cautions
CA search: "SVAMITVA scheme land records digitisation India 2026 progress property cards"
CA found: Reported - by 2026 drone survey under the SVAMITVA programme covered roughly 3.29 lakh of a targeted 3.44 lakh villages with about 3.10 crore property cards prepared and around 2.65-2.72 crore distributed across 31 states and union territories, an example of the record-creation problem that defeated land reform being addressed seven decades later
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the two phases and the four components

```text
 PHASE ONE - INSTITUTIONAL REFORM (from 1947 to the early 1960s)
   1. abolition of intermediaries: zamindars, jagirdars               -> LARGELY SUCCEEDED
   2. tenancy reform: security of tenure, rent reduction, ownership   -> PARTLY FAILED
   3. ceilings on landholdings                                        -> LARGELY FAILED
   4. cooperativisation and community development                     -> JOINT FARMING FAILED
                                                                          SERVICE COOPERATIVES SUCCEEDED
 PHASE TWO - TECHNOLOGICAL REFORM (from the mid- or late 1960s)
   the Green Revolution package
   -- the two phases overlap and are complementary, not sequential compartments --

 THE EXPLANATORY VARIABLE
   component 1 expropriated a class ISOLATED during the national movement  -> it went through
   components 2 and 3 targeted the UPPER PEASANTRY, part of the movement
        and of every party's rural base                                     -> they did not
```

*One programme, four components, and a single variable - the political weight of the class being acted against - that predicts the outcome of each.*

### 1. The problem in ordinary language

A zamindar was not usually a farmer. In much of northern and eastern India he held a revenue-collecting right that had hardened into proprietorship under colonial settlements, and he lived on rent extracted from cultivators. Removing that layer does two things at once: it ends an extraction, and it hands the cultivating tenant beneath a direct relationship with the state. It does not, by itself, redistribute land to the landless, because the tenant who becomes an owner may already be a substantial cultivator with tenants of his own.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** everything difficult about Indian land reform is contained in that last clause. Abolition made the top layer disappear. It also created several million new landowners who then became the constituency that blocked the next stage.

### 2. The legislative and constitutional sequence

✅ **Fact:** within a year or two of independence - by 1949 - zamindari abolition bills or land-tenure legislation were introduced in provinces including the United Provinces, Madhya Pradesh, Bihar, Madras, Assam and Bombay, with the report of the **U.P. Zamindari Abolition Committee, chaired by G.B. Pant**, acting as the initial model for many others.

✅ **Fact:** in the Constituent Assembly there was widespread apprehension, including among leaders deeply committed to abolition such as Nehru, Pant and Patel, that zamindars would use the courts to block acquisition on grounds of the right to property or the inadequacy of compensation. The relevant provisions were framed so that compensation recommended by state legislatures was **non-justiciable**, requiring only presidential assent. There was a wide consensus on giving legislatures that authority, while acquisition of commercial or industrial property continued to require an entirely different set of principles.

✅ **Fact:** the expectation failed. Zamindars challenged the constitutionality of abolition laws, and courts such as the Patna High Court upheld the landlords' suit. The Congress government responded with the **First Amendment in 1951** and the **Fourth Amendment in 1955**, strengthening state legislatures' hands by making the question of violation of any fundamental right, or insufficiency of compensation, impermissible in the courts. Appeals continued, but the back of the resistance was broken by the mid-1950s.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the source draws a conclusion that is worth carrying into any answer on the property-rights amendments: the framers of the Constitution, including its so-called right wing, were not designing a scheme to stymie land reform but were trying to complete it within a democratic framework. The amendments were a response to judicial obstruction, not an assault on judicial review.

### 3. The resistance and the delay

| Arena | Method of resistance | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Legislature | Prolonged debates, references to select committees, repeated amendments | Several years passed between the introduction of bills and enactment in states such as U.P. and Bihar |
| ✅ Judiciary | Challenges to constitutionality up to the Supreme Court | Delay; answered by the First and Fourth Amendments |
| ✅ Administration | In Bihar, refusal to hand over land records even after losing twice in the Supreme Court | Forced the government into lengthy reconstruction of records |
| ✅ Local administration | Collusion between landlords and lower revenue officials, many of whom had been rent-collecting agents of the zamindars | Implementation skewed in the zamindar's favour |

✅ **Fact:** the Congress repeatedly reiterated its resolve in All India Congress Committee resolutions, in a 1950 conference of chief ministers and provincial Congress presidents, in the First Plan and above all in its election manifestos. ⚠️ **Analytical inference:** adult franchise cut both ways and the source states the mechanism plainly - democracy reduced the political weight of the zamindars while increasing the urgency of meeting the peasantry's long-standing demands, which the Congress had itself mobilised over decades.

### 4. Scale, compensation and completion

✅ **Fact:** abolition Acts were passed in most provinces **by 1956**; certainly by the end of the 1950s, though essentially by 1956, the abolition of intermediaries - the zamindars of British India and the jagirdars of the princely states now merged into India - can be said to have been completed, with the exception of certain pockets of Bihar. A major difficulty in implementation was the **absence of adequate land records**.

✅ **Fact:** the abolition of zamindari meant that **about twenty million erstwhile tenants became landowners**.

✅ **Fact:** compensation was generally small and varied by state according to the strength of the peasant movement, the class balance and the ideological composition of the legislature. In Kashmir no compensation was paid. In Punjab, occupancy tenants of Patiala paid nothing and inferior tenants were given a negligible amount. Most states followed a variant of the U.P. model, in which compensation was **inversely related to the size of the holding**: small zamindars paying land revenue up to Rs 25 received about twenty times their net annual income, while big zamindars paying Rs 2,000 to Rs 10,000 received merely two to four times theirs. Payment was stretched over long periods, in some cases forty years. It is estimated that big zamindars who received compensation found their income from alienated land reduced to about one-fortieth of their earlier income.

✅ **Fact:** out of a total due of **Rs 6,700 million**, compensation actually paid until 1961 was **Rs 1,642 million** - a small figure when set against, by one estimate, more than six times that amount, Rs 10,000 million, spent on food imports between 1946 and 1953 alone.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the inverse compensation schedule is the most elegant evidence in the whole land-reform story that the reform was designed to be progressive, and the payment schedule of up to forty years is the evidence that it was designed to be affordable. Together they show a state acting purposefully - which is exactly what makes the failure of the next two components a matter of political will rather than of capacity.

### 5. The loophole that mattered: "personal cultivation"

✅ **Fact:** in U.P. zamindars were permitted to retain lands declared to be under their **"personal cultivation"**, and the term was loosely defined - making it possible for those who supervised land personally, or through a relative, or who provided capital and credit, to call themselves cultivators. In states such as U.P., Bihar and Madras there was initially **no limit** on the size of land that could be declared under personal cultivation, until ceiling laws were introduced.

✅ **Fact:** this contradicted the **Congress Agrarian Reforms Committee (Kumarappa Committee)** report of 1949, which stipulated that only those putting in a minimum of physical labour and participating in actual agricultural operations could be said to perform personal cultivation, and which envisaged a limit on how much land could be resumed, never reducing a tenant's holding below the economic level.

✅ **Fact:** the practical result was twofold. Absentee landowners could retain large tracts; and to maximise the area declared under personal cultivation, zamindars resorted to **large-scale eviction of tenants, mainly the less secure small tenants** - the first of the eviction rounds that the source calls a major blot on India's land-reform record. At the same time, many former rent-receiving zamindars did begin to manage the retained land, invest in it and move towards capitalist farming, which was one of the objectives of the reform.

### 6. Who actually gained

✅ **Fact:** the big landlords, who lost the bulk of their lands, were the chief losers; the main beneficiaries were the **occupancy or upper tenants**, who had direct leases from the zamindar and now became landowners. Such tenants were generally middle or rich peasants who sometimes had subleases to lower tenants with few rights, often called "tenants at will".

✅ **Fact:** the source states the political consequence explicitly: reforms that threatened the interests of sections of the upper peasantry, who had been very much part of the national movement and had considerable societal support, were far more difficult and sometimes impossible to achieve.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** abolition succeeded because the class it removed had already been isolated socially and politically during the national movement, and it created in its place a class that no subsequent reform could dislodge.

### Comparative treatment: abolition judged against alternatives

| Path to agrarian restructuring | Instrument | Human cost | Applicable in India? |
|---|---|---|---|
| ✅ Soviet | Forced collectivisation | Millions of lives | No - incompatible with civil liberties and adult franchise |
| ✅ Chinese | Forcible expropriation and communes | Millions of lives | No |
| ✅ Japanese | Land reform under an army of occupation among a defeated people | Externally imposed | Not available |
| ✅ Indian | Legislation, constitutional amendment, compensation, litigation | Substantial eviction; no large-scale violence | The path actually taken |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the comparison is not self-congratulation; it is the explanation. India's method guaranteed that reform would stop at the point where the losing class could organise politically, because every instrument available - legislation, amendment, litigation, administration - was one the losing class could also use.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Abolition was a transfer within the propertied classes. Twenty million upper tenants became owners; the landless gained nothing; describing this as a success is a class-blind judgement.

**Reply:** The distributional criticism is correct and the source records it - the beneficiaries were generally middle or rich peasants, and several million tenants-at-will beneath them remained unprotected. But "success" here is measured against what the component set out to do, which was to remove a rentier layer, end illegal cesses and forced labour and establish a direct relationship between cultivator and state. On that test it succeeded, and it also did something the criticism understates: it removed the class that had been the Raj's rural political base, which had consequences far beyond land.

**Residual:** Whether abolition could have been designed to reach the tenants-at-will directly remains genuinely arguable. The evidence that it might have is Kashmir and, later, Kerala and West Bengal - where political mobilisation of the intended beneficiaries changed the outcome. The evidence that it could not is that nowhere in India did such mobilisation exist in 1949-52 at the scale abolition required.

### UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the single directly owned objective demand of this whole topic is **UPSC Civil Services (Preliminary) Examination 2019, General Studies Paper I, Question 2**, recorded in the verified routing ledger as "Land reforms legislation in post-independence India", with the official key unavailable locally. It is displayed neutrally in the final question index; no answer is stated, and no option is eliminated here.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the preparation this demand actually requires is exactly this lesson plus the next two: which component was legislated when, what the First and Fourth Amendments did, which component succeeded, and what the Ninth Schedule protection of 1974 covered. A candidate who has disaggregated land reform can answer statement-based questions on it from understanding rather than recall.

Cross-owned Mains routes are heavier. ✅ **Fact:** **UPSC Civil Services (Main) Examination 2024, General Studies Paper III, Question 3**, verified from the locally held official paper: "What were the factors responsible for the successful implementation of land reforms in some parts of the country? Elaborate." (10 marks, 150 words), with the directive *elaborate*. Its primary owner is the Economy file on land reforms; this lesson and the next supply the historical explanation - political mobilisation of beneficiaries, record creation, and the political weight of the class being expropriated.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Dating abolition to 1947-48; bills came from 1949 and the Acts were passed in most provinces by 1956.
- Attributing the First and Fourth Amendments to hostility towards judicial review rather than to the specific obstruction of abolition laws.
- Saying compensation was generous; it was small, inversely graded by holding size and paid over as long as forty years.
- Treating "twenty million" as landless beneficiaries; they were superior or occupancy tenants who became owners.
- Ignoring the "personal cultivation" loophole and the eviction it caused.
- Calling the whole land-reform programme a success because this component succeeded.

### Revision notes

1. Two phases: institutional reform to the early 1960s, technological reform from the mid- or late 1960s, overlapping rather than sequential.
2. Four institutional components: abolition of intermediaries; tenancy reform; ceilings; cooperativisation.
3. Bills from 1949; the U.P. Zamindari Abolition Committee under G.B. Pant was the model.
4. Compensation made non-justiciable in the Constitution; judicial challenge followed; First Amendment 1951 and Fourth Amendment 1955 answered it.
5. Acts passed in most provinces by 1956; abolition essentially complete by then, except pockets of Bihar; absence of land records was the main administrative difficulty.
6. About twenty million erstwhile tenants became landowners.
7. Compensation was inversely related to holding size - about twenty times net annual income for the smallest, two to four times for the largest - and paid over up to forty years; Kashmir paid none.
8. Of Rs 6,700 million due, Rs 1,642 million had been paid by 1961.
9. The "personal cultivation" loophole, contrary to the Kumarappa Committee of 1949, allowed retention of large tracts and triggered mass eviction of small tenants.
10. Beneficiaries were occupancy or upper tenants - middle and rich peasants, often with subtenants of their own.
11. Resistance operated in legislature, courts and local administration, including Bihar's refusal to surrender records.
12. The success condition: the expropriated class had been isolated during the national movement.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain, using the design of the compensation scheme and the personal-cultivation clause, why zamindari abolition is best described as a determined reform with a built-in escape route.

**Model answer:** The compensation design shows determination. Compensation was made non-justiciable in the Constitution and then protected by the First Amendment of 1951 and the Fourth of 1955 after courts such as the Patna High Court upheld landlords' suits; it was set inversely to holding size, so the smallest zamindars received about twenty times their net annual income while the largest received two to four times, and it was spread over periods as long as forty years, reducing big zamindars' income from alienated land to roughly a fortieth of its former level. Of Rs 6,700 million due, only Rs 1,642 million had been paid by 1961. The escape route lay in the same statutes. Land under "personal cultivation" could be retained, the term was loosely defined to include supervision through a relative or the provision of capital, and in U.P., Bihar and Madras there was initially no limit on the area so retained - contrary to the Kumarappa Committee's 1949 stipulation of actual physical participation and a resumption limit. Zamindars therefore evicted small tenants on a large scale to maximise retained land.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that a reform is either sincere or fraudulent. Indian land legislation was characteristically both: strong where it expropriated a politically weak class, and permissive where enforcement would have required action against cultivating proprietors whose support the same governments needed.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

"Zamindari abolition was the only component of Indian land reform that achieved its objective." Examine. (15 marks, 250 words)

#### Model answer

The statement is substantially correct, provided "objective" is defined as the component's own aim.

Abolition aimed to remove intermediary tenures, and it did. Bills were introduced from 1949 on the model of the U.P. committee under G.B. Pant; compensation was made non-justiciable and, when courts obstructed, the First Amendment of 1951 and the Fourth of 1955 secured the statutes; Acts were passed in most provinces by 1956 and abolition was essentially complete by then, except in pockets of Bihar. About twenty million erstwhile tenants became landowners, and compensation - inversely graded by holding size and paid over up to forty years, with Rs 1,642 million of Rs 6,700 million paid by 1961 - was kept small enough to be fiscally bearable.

The other components did not meet their aims. Tenancy legislation sought security, fair rent and ownership, yet the 1961 Census estimated 82 per cent of tenancies insecure, market rents ran near half the gross produce, and eviction followed the right of resumption. Ceilings released about two per cent of cultivated area. Bhoodan's distribution stalled.

Two qualifications are necessary. Abolition's benefit accrued to occupancy tenants - middle and rich peasants - not to the landless, and the personal-cultivation clause allowed retention and triggered eviction. And a substantial minority of tenants did gain permanent occupancy rights, so tenancy reform was not a uniform failure.

Abolition therefore succeeded on its own terms because the class it removed was politically isolated, while every later component confronted the class on which governments depended.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the component's own objective stated before it is judged 3 + four dated abolition facts 4 + the contrast with tenancy and ceilings using at least two figures 4 + both qualifications 2 + the political-isolation explanation as verdict 2.**

**Bridge to Lesson 8:** If abolition created a new class of owners with subtenants beneath them, the next question is what happened to those subtenants when the state tried to protect them.

## Lesson 8 - Land reform II: tenancy, and the anatomy of a legislated failure

Progress: 8/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: security of tenure, fair rent, ownership purchase, eviction and concealment, and the two states that partly broke the pattern

CA search: "SVAMITVA scheme land records digitisation India 2026 progress property cards"
CA found: Reported - the 2026 progress of drone-based rural property mapping is described as producing legal proof of ownership, reducing disputes and enabling credit, with several states adding women as co-owners; the historical parallel is exact, since the absence of a written record is what made tenancy rights unenforceable after 1950
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the three objectives and what defeated each

```text
 OBJECTIVE 1: SECURITY OF TENURE
   law: continuous cultivation for a fixed number of years (varying by region) -> protection
   defeat: right of RESUMPTION for "personal cultivation" + loose definition
           -> legal eviction, illegal eviction, "voluntary surrender"
   partial success: a substantial minority did obtain permanent occupancy rights

 OBJECTIVE 2: FAIR RENT
   law: maximum rent generally 20-25% of gross produce (First and Second Plan norms);
        some states 33.3-40%
   reality: market rents near 50% of gross produce; up to 70% in parts of Punjab after
            the Green Revolution; the tenant often bore input costs as well
   defeat: fair rent is enforceable only by a tenant who cannot be removed
           -- so failure on objective 1 guarantees failure on objective 2

 OBJECTIVE 3: OWNERSHIP PURCHASE
   law: purchase at a multiple of annual rent, e.g. 8 years' rent in parts of Andhra Pradesh
        ~ 40% of market price
   partial success: Gujarat about 0.77 of 1.3 million tenants; Maharashtra about 1.1 of 2.6 million
   limit: secure occupancy tenants with reduced rents had little incentive to buy

 THE COMMON SOLVENT: NO WRITTEN RECORD
   most tenancies were ORAL and unrecorded -> the beneficiary could not prove he existed
```

*Three objectives, one structural solvent. A right that cannot be evidenced is not a right; it is an aspiration with a statute number.*

### 1. Why tenancy survived abolition at all

✅ **Fact:** tenancy continued after abolition in two places - on the lands of former zamindars now said to be under their personal cultivation, and on lands sub-leased by former occupancy tenants who had become owners. Moreover, at independence only about **half** the area was under zamindari tenure; the other half was under **ryotwari**, where landlordism and an insecure, rack-rented tenantry were also rampant.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is why "abolition solved the tenancy problem" is false as a matter of arithmetic before it is false as a matter of policy. Half the country was never covered by abolition at all.

### 2. The three objectives, and the balance the law tried to strike

✅ **Fact:** tenancy reform had three basic objectives: to guarantee security of tenure to tenants who had cultivated a piece of land continuously for a fixed number of years, varying by region; to reduce rents to a "fair" level, generally considered to be between one-fourth and one-sixth of the value of gross produce; and to give the tenant the right to acquire ownership of the land he cultivated, subject to restrictions, at a price well below market - generally a multiple of annual rent, such as eight or ten years' rent. In parts of Andhra Pradesh the price was eight years' rent, roughly **40 per cent** of the market price.

✅ **Fact:** the legislation sought to balance the interests of landowner and tenant, especially where the landowner was small, through a system of **floors and ceilings**. The landowner's right of resumption was limited so that his total holding after resumption did not exceed a prescribed limit - the First Plan suggested **three times the "family holding"**, a family holding being defined as a single plough unit. While resuming, the landowner could not deprive the tenant of all his land: in Kerala, Orissa, Gujarat, Himachal Pradesh, Maharashtra, Karnataka and Tamil Nadu the tenant had to be left with at least **half his holding**; in Bihar the floor was half the tenant's holding or a minimum of five acres, whichever was less, and in West Bengal two and a half acres. Conversely, the tenant's right to acquire was restricted so that the owner was not deprived of all his land and the tenant's post-acquisition holding did not exceed the state ceiling.

✅ **Fact:** the Second Plan's reasoning was that "the economic circumstances of small owners are not so different from those of tenants that tenancy legislation should operate to their disadvantage", so very small owners could resume their entire holding for self-cultivation.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the protection written for the small owner became the instrument of the large one. **P.S. Appu**, who headed the Planning Commission's Task Force on Agrarian Relations reporting in **1973**, recorded that these provisions were misused by larger landlords with the active connivance of revenue officials, and the Third Plan noted the same abuse - large owners transferring land into the names of relatives to enter the "small landowner" category and then evicting tenants under the small owner's right of resumption.

### 3. Eviction, measured

✅ **Fact:** eviction began in **anticipation** of the legislation; inordinate delays in enacting and implementing the laws, engineered by vested interests, allowed potential beneficiaries to be removed before the law came into force. Initially only Manipur and Tripura made personal labour by the landowner a condition of resumption.

| Evidence | Figure | Source position |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Bombay State, 1948-51 | Protected tenants fell from 1.7 million to 1.3 million, more than 23 per cent | Planning Commission's Panel on Land Reforms, 1956 |
| ✅ Hyderabad State, 1951-55 | Number of protected tenants fell by about 57 per cent | Same panel |
| ✅ Hyderabad detailed study, of every 100 protected tenants created in 1951, by 1954 | 45.4 per cent retained status; 12.4 per cent became owners; 2.6 per cent legally evicted; 22.1 per cent illegally evicted; 17.5 per cent "voluntarily" surrendered | The source notes that voluntary surrender was a euphemism for illegal eviction, since tenants were "persuaded" under threat |

✅ **Fact:** the practice was so common that the **Fourth Plan** recommended that all surrenders should be made only in favour of the government, which could then allot the land to eligible persons. Only a handful of states acted on the recommendation.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the same Hyderabad study also proves the other half of the argument, and an answer that uses only one half is incomplete. **About 67.8 per cent** of tenants brought under the legislation - the 45.4 per cent who remained protected plus the 12.4 per cent who became owners - no longer suffered insecurity. That is a substantial achievement with real consequences for investment and productivity on those holdings.

### 4. Concealment, and the record problem

✅ **Fact:** where legislation could not be evaded by eviction, it was evaded by redescription. Tenants were renamed "farm servants" while continuing in exactly the same status. In the early years tenants were often converted into **sharecroppers**, because in some states, such as U.P., sharecroppers were not treated as tenants and so were unprotected: only cash-rent payers counted, not those paying fixed produce rents or a proportion of total produce. In West Bengal, sharecroppers known as **bargadars** received no protection until the West Bengal Land Reforms Act was amended in **July 1970** to give them limited protection. A spurt in sharecropping immediately after 1951 is partly explained by this.

✅ **Fact:** most tenancies were **oral and informal**, hence unrecorded, so tenants could not benefit from legislation in their favour. The **1961 Census estimated that 82 per cent of tenancies in the country were insecure**. Going only by recorded tenancies, the **1971 Census** reached absurd conclusions - that 91.1 per cent of cultivated area in India was owner-operated, and that Bihar had the highest proportion of owner cultivation of any state at 99.6 per cent, with tenancies constituting only 0.22 per cent of operational holdings - when Bihar was commonly accepted to have a very high proportion of tenancy and the 1961 Census had given a figure of 36.65 per cent.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the 1961-1971 Census discrepancy is the most useful single fact in this lesson, because it is simultaneously a datum and a demonstration. It shows what the tenancy data were worth, and it shows why tenancy area figures must always be quoted with a warning. This is exactly the point at which the standing caution applies: tenancy-area estimates are rough because oral and concealed tenancies were poorly recorded, and the decline from about 42 per cent in 1950-51 to between 20 and 25 per cent by the early 1960s cannot be read simply as success, because part of that decline came through **eviction**.

✅ **Fact:** the remedy, where it was attempted, was record creation. In U.P., **Charan Singh** as revenue minister launched a massive drive to get a few million records corrected or newly inscribed. In the late 1960s Kerala undertook a large programme of conferring titles on hutment dwellers and tenants, launched with the active participation of peasant organisations.

### 5. Operation Barga: what mobilisation could and could not do

✅ **Fact:** the Left Front government that came to power in West Bengal in June 1977 launched **Operation Barga in July 1978**, to register sharecroppers within a time-bound period so that their legal rights - permanent and heritable occupancy and a crop division of **1:3** between landowner and sharecropper - could be secured. Of an estimated **2.4 million bargadars**, only **0.4 million** had been recorded up to June 1978; after the launch the number rose from **0.7 million by October 1979** to about **1.4 million by November 1990**.

✅ **Fact:** the distinctive method was mobilisation of the intended beneficiaries and orientation camps in which "30 to 40 agricultural workers and sharecroppers and a dozen and a half officers of Land Reform and other related departments were made to stay together, eat together and discuss together in the same premises in distant rural areas" - neutralising the lower revenue officials who had obstructed implementation elsewhere.

✅ **Fact:** the programme nevertheless reached a stalemate after somewhat more than half the sharecroppers had been covered, for two reasons the source states carefully. First, it was found politically unviable and ethically indefensible to proceed against "landlords" who were themselves cultivators with holdings only marginally larger than those of the sharecroppers and entitled to only a quarter of the produce - "the 'class enemy' had dissolved into a sea of small holdings". Second, the land-man ratio allowed a landlord to rotate leased land among two or more bargadars, so registering one would permanently oust another, and registering all would push holdings below viable size.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the most important qualification in the entire land-reform chapter and the one most often missed. The limit on redistribution in West Bengal was not the absence of political will, which was present in unusual measure; it was the **objective structure of landholding**. When the average adversary is a small cultivator, further redistribution stops being redistribution and becomes the division of poverty.

### 6. The three objectives, assessed

✅ **Fact:** the first objective met only limited success: a substantial proportion of tenants acquired security and many became owners, but large numbers remained unprotected, and unsecured oral tenancy continued on a large scale despite the partial successes of Kerala and West Bengal.

✅ **Fact:** the second objective was almost impossible to achieve because of the first. Legislation fixed maximum rents at First and Second Plan levels of **20 to 25 per cent** of gross produce, with some states - Punjab, Haryana, Tamil Nadu and coastal Andhra Pradesh - fixing higher maxima between **33.3 and 40 per cent**. In practice, market rents in almost all parts of the country ran at about **50 per cent of gross produce**, the tenant often bearing input costs as well; and the Green Revolution aggravated the problem, with land values and rentals rising to as high as **70 per cent** in parts of Punjab. Only the upper stratum of tenants with occupancy rights - often indistinguishable from landowners - could enforce legal rent.

✅ **Fact:** the third objective was partly achieved. In Gujarat, out of about 1.3 million tenants, more than half - about **0.77 million** - purchased ownership rights; in Maharashtra, out of 2.6 million tenants, about **1.1 million** did so. One reason more did not is that tenants with permanent occupancy rights and reduced rents had little motivation to raise capital and face legal complications when they were already virtual owners.

✅ **Fact:** the cumulative judgement the source offers is Daniel Thorner's, from 1968: despite all the evasions, leakages and loopholes, "many millions of cultivators who had previously been weak tenants or tenants-at-will were enabled to become superior tenants or virtual owners."

> 🔑 **Answer line:** tenancy reform failed as redistribution and partly succeeded as security, and both halves of that sentence have to be in the answer.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** If a majority of covered tenants in the Hyderabad study ended secure or owning, calling tenancy reform a failure is tendentious.

**Reply:** The distinction is between those *brought under the legislation* and those *left outside it*. Among the covered, outcomes were genuinely good. But the 1961 Census estimate that 82 per cent of tenancies were insecure describes the population as a whole, most of which was oral, unrecorded and therefore never covered. A law can have a high success rate among its beneficiaries and a low coverage rate, and land reform's characteristic failure is the second.

**Residual:** How large the uncovered tenancy actually was cannot be settled. The 1961 and 1971 Census figures are mutually incompatible, and the source itself treats the 1971 figures as absurd. Any precise national claim about tenancy area in this period is therefore beyond the evidence.

### UPSC application

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three General Studies Paper III demands between 2021 and 2024 - on the impact of land reforms on small and marginal farmers, on the objectives and measures of land reform including ceilings, and on the factors behind successful implementation in some parts of the country - all route primarily to the Economy owner. Each needs this lesson's causal material. For the 2024 demand, the "some parts of the country" are identifiable and explicable: Jammu and Kashmir, where ceilings were fully implemented without compensation; Kerala, where titles were conferred with peasant-organisation participation; and West Bengal, where registration was achieved by mobilising beneficiaries and neutralising local revenue officials. The common factor is that beneficiaries were organised and records were created.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Treating the decline in tenancy area from about 42 to 20-25 per cent as proof of success; part of it was eviction, and the data are unreliable.
- Quoting the 1971 Census owner-operation figures as fact; the source calls the conclusions absurd.
- Saying sharecroppers were tenants everywhere; in states such as U.P. they were not, and West Bengal's bargadars gained limited protection only from July 1970.
- Describing Operation Barga as complete; it stalled after covering somewhat more than half of an estimated 2.4 million bargadars.
- Explaining that stall by lack of political will; the binding constraints were small holdings and the rotation of leased land.
- Forgetting that fair-rent laws cannot work without security of tenure.

### Revision notes

1. Only about half the area was under zamindari at independence; the ryotwari half had its own landlordism and insecure tenantry.
2. Three objectives: security of tenure, fair rent of one-fourth to one-sixth of gross produce, and ownership purchase at a multiple of annual rent.
3. Resumption was capped - the First Plan suggested three times the family holding - and floors protected tenants, at half the holding in several states, or five acres in Bihar and two and a half in West Bengal.
4. The small-owner protection was captured by large owners through transfers into relatives' names; noted by P.S. Appu's 1973 Task Force and by the Third Plan.
5. Bombay protected tenants fell from 1.7 to 1.3 million, 1948-51; Hyderabad numbers fell about 57 per cent, 1951-55.
6. Hyderabad study of 1951 cohort by 1954: 45.4 per cent protected, 12.4 per cent owners, 2.6 per cent legally evicted, 22.1 per cent illegally evicted, 17.5 per cent "voluntary" surrender.
7. The Fourth Plan recommended that surrenders be made only to government; few states complied.
8. Concealment: "farm servants"; conversion to sharecropping where sharecroppers were unprotected; West Bengal bargadars protected only from July 1970.
9. 1961 Census: 82 per cent of tenancies insecure. 1971 Census: 91.1 per cent owner-operated, Bihar 99.6 per cent - against Bihar's 36.65 per cent in 1961.
10. Charan Singh's record drive in U.P.; Kerala's late-1960s conferment of titles with peasant participation.
11. Operation Barga from July 1978: 0.4 million recorded before it, 0.7 million by October 1979, about 1.4 million by November 1990, of an estimated 2.4 million; crop division 1:3.
12. Legal maximum rents 20-25 per cent, some states 33.3-40 per cent; market rents about 50 per cent and up to 70 per cent in parts of Punjab after the Green Revolution.
13. Ownership purchase: Gujarat about 0.77 of 1.3 million; Maharashtra about 1.1 of 2.6 million tenants.
14. Thorner's 1968 judgement: many millions moved from weak tenant to superior tenant or virtual owner.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why did the legal ceiling on rent fail almost everywhere, and what does that failure reveal about the relationship between the three objectives of tenancy legislation?

**Model answer:** It failed because rent control is enforceable only by a tenant who cannot be removed for demanding it. Legal maxima were fixed at the First and Second Plan norms of 20 to 25 per cent of gross produce, with some states allowing 33.3 to 40 per cent, while market rents ran at about 50 per cent of gross produce and reached as high as 70 per cent in parts of Punjab after the Green Revolution raised land values, with the tenant frequently bearing input costs as well. Only the upper stratum of tenants who already held occupancy rights - and were often indistinguishable from owners - could actually enforce the legal rate. The relationship this reveals is that the three objectives were not parallel but sequential: security of tenure is the precondition for fair rent, and fair rent plus security jointly determine whether the ownership-purchase right is used, since a secure tenant paying a reduced rent had little incentive to raise capital and litigate for a title he effectively possessed. Failure on the first objective therefore propagated through the whole scheme.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading a statutory entitlement as a description of reality. In this period the gap between law and outcome was produced by evidence and enforcement - oral tenancy, missing records and local official collusion - and not by any defect in the drafting of the rent provisions.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

Analyse why tenancy reform in post-independence India succeeded in providing security to some cultivators while failing to reach the majority. (15 marks, 250 words)

#### Model answer

Tenancy reform divided its own beneficiary population into the recorded and the unrecorded, and its record follows that division exactly.

Where tenants were recorded and organised, the law worked. A detailed Hyderabad study found that of every hundred protected tenants created in 1951, by 1954 about 45 per cent remained protected and 12 per cent had become owners - roughly two-thirds secure.
