Kerala's conferment of titles in the late 1960s was conducted with peasant organisations; West Bengal's Operation Barga, launched in July 1978, raised recorded bargadars from 0.4 million to about 1.4 million by November 1990, using orientation camps that neutralised obstructive revenue officials. Ownership purchase reached about 0.77 million of 1.3 million tenants in Gujarat and about 1.1 million of 2.6 million in Maharashtra.

Where tenancy was oral, the law could not reach. The 1961 Census estimated 82 per cent of tenancies insecure, and the 1971 Census produced figures - 91.1 per cent of area owner-operated, Bihar 99.6 per cent - that the record makes untenable. Resumption for "personal cultivation" produced eviction: Bombay's protected tenants fell from 1.7 to 1.3 million between 1948 and 1951, and in the Hyderabad cohort 22 per cent were illegally evicted and 17.5 per cent made "voluntary" surrenders.

Two structural limits remained even where will existed. In West Bengal the residual landlords were themselves small cultivators, and land rotation among bargadars meant registering one would oust another.

Security therefore followed evidence and organisation; its absence, not the statutes, explains the majority's exclusion.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the recorded-versus-unrecorded division stated as the organising claim 4 + success evidence from at least three states with figures 4 + eviction and concealment evidence with figures 3 + the Census unreliability handled explicitly 2 + the structural limit in West Bengal 2.**

**Bridge to Lesson 9:** Security for some tenants still left the distribution of ownership untouched. The instrument designed to change that distribution was the ceiling - and it produced less land than any other measure in this chapter.

## Lesson 9 - Land reform III: ceilings, and the Bhoodan alternative

Progress: 9/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Nagpur and the backlash, the defects of the first ceiling laws, the 1972 guidelines, the measured result, and the voluntary movement that could not substitute

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the survey history's chapter on ceilings and the Bhoodan movement, read in full for the Nagpur sequence, the state-by-state ceiling defects, the 1972 guidelines and the distribution figures to 1992
CA search: "MSP legal guarantee farmers demand India 2026 news agricultural marketing reform"
CA found: Reported - the 2026 debate records that procurement is concentrated in a few states and that universal statutory procurement faces a fiscal objection; the structural point is the same one ceilings met - a redistributive promise is limited by what the state can enforce against an organised rural constituency
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: fourteen years from proposal to statute, and what was left to redistribute

```text
 1946  All India Kisan Sabha: maximum 25 acres per landholder
 Nov 1947  AICC committee under Nehru: fix a maximum; surplus to village cooperatives
 Jul 1949  Kumarappa Committee: ceiling = three times an economic holding
 1951-56   First Plan: accepts the principle; leaves the limit to each state;
           expects two to three years merely to survey and build machinery
 1953      AICC at Agra urges immediate steps
 1957      NDC standing committee: complete ceilings where legislated by 1960;
           others to legislate by 1958-59
 Jan 1959  NAGPUR CONGRESS: ceilings legislated in all states by end-1959;
           surplus to vest in panchayats, managed through cooperatives of landless
 Dec 1958 / Feb 1959  Ranga's letter signed by 100 Congress MPs; Ranga resigns
 Jun 1959  SWATANTRA PARTY founded - Ranga president, with Rajagopalachari and Masani
 End-1961  most states finally pass enabling legislation  <-- FOURTEEN YEARS AFTER 1947
                     |
       by then: holdings sold, partitioned, transferred benami, tenants evicted
                     |
       RESULT to 1970: 2.4 million acres declared surplus; about half distributed
                       = about 0.3 per cent of India's cultivated land
```

*The delay was not administrative sloth. It was the interval during which the intended losers rearranged their property.*

### 1. The idea, and why consensus failed here

✅ **Fact:** on ceilings, societal consensus was **weak, if not non-existent**, and that is reflected in the extreme difficulty of implementing the programme with even reasonable success. The All India Kisan Sabha had supported a maximum of 25 acres per landholder in 1946; the Congress introduced the notion officially soon after independence, when an AICC committee headed by Nehru in **November 1947** recommended that "the maximum size of holdings should be fixed", with surplus land acquired and placed at the disposal of village cooperatives; the **Kumarappa Committee** of July 1949 recommended a ceiling of three times an economic holding - an economic holding being one giving a reasonable standard of living and full employment to a normal family and at least a pair of bullocks.

✅ **Fact:** the First Plan expressed itself in favour of an upper limit but left the exact limit to each state "having regard to its own agrarian history and its present problems", anticipated that the 1953 census of landholding would supply the data, and expected that two to three years would be necessary merely to survey and set up enforcement machinery.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** compare this with abolition, where bills were introduced within two years and the Constitution was amended twice to protect them. The difference in urgency is the difference in the political identity of the loser. Abolition removed intermediaries; ceilings threatened cultivating proprietors, including the very tenants abolition had just enfranchised.

### 2. Nagpur and the backlash

✅ **Fact:** opposition to ceilings built up in the press, in Parliament, in state legislatures and within the Congress. Matters came to a head at the **Nagpur session of the Congress in January 1959**, which resolved that ceilings should be fixed on existing and future holdings, with legislation completed in all states by the end of 1959, and that surplus land should vest in panchayats and be managed through cooperatives of landless labourers.

✅ **Fact:** the reaction was immediate. N.G. Ranga, secretary of the Congress parliamentary party, who had already in December 1958 sent Nehru a letter signed by a hundred Congress members of parliament criticising ceilings, resigned in February 1959. Ranga and C. Rajagopalachari, alarmed at ceilings and at the threat of compulsory cooperativisation, joined Minoo Masani of the Forum for Free Enterprise to form the **Swatantra Party in June 1959**, with Ranga as president. The campaigners and beneficiaries of zamindari abolition - tenants who had become landowners - ranged themselves against the next stage of land reform.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is one of the clearest instances in Indian political history of a policy creating its own opposition party, and it belongs in any answer about the limits of reform inside a democracy. Notice the composition: urban free-enterprise opinion, conservative Congress leaders, and the new owner-cultivators created by the previous land reform. The state had manufactured its own veto.

✅ **Fact:** the opponents' real victory came at state level, because states had to formulate and implement the legislation. Legislatures meeting shortly after Nagpur showed no haste, and most states passed enabling legislation only by the end of **1961** - nearly fourteen years after the idea was officially mooted.

### 3. Why the first ceiling laws released almost nothing

| Defect | Evidence |
|---|---|
| ✅ Ceilings set far too high | More than 70 per cent of holdings were under five acres, yet ceilings ranged from 27 to 312 acres in Andhra Pradesh by class of land, 50 acres in Assam, 15 to 37.5 in Kerala, 30 to 60 in Punjab, 25 in West Bengal and 18 to 126 in Maharashtra |
| ✅ Individual, not family, unit | Landowners divided holdings notionally among relatives |
| ✅ Family-size escalators | The ceiling could rise by 67 per cent in Kerala, 90 per cent in Madhya Pradesh, 100 per cent in Bihar, Madras and Maharashtra and 140 per cent in Tripura where family size exceeded five; Andhra Pradesh had no limit, allowing 6 to 72 acres per extra member. Only states where few holdings exceeded the ceiling - Jammu and Kashmir, West Bengal, Himachal Pradesh, Punjab - made no family-size allowance |
| ✅ Wide exemptions | Following Second Plan recommendations: tea, coffee and rubber plantations, orchards, specialised cattle-breeding, dairying and wool farms, sugarcane farms operated by sugar factories, and "efficiently managed farms" carrying heavy investment. Tamil Nadu reportedly permitted 26 kinds of exemption; "efficiently managed" was vague enough for large numbers to have themselves declared efficient; land held by cooperatives could be transferred to bogus cooperatives |
| ✅ Delay | Large landowners had time to sell, make malafide transfers to relatives, make benami transfers and evict tenants while resuming land up to the ceiling, often falsely claiming a shift to progressive farming |

✅ **Fact:** the measured result to 1970 is stark. Until the end of 1970 **not a single acre** was declared surplus in large states including Bihar, Mysore, Kerala, Orissa and Rajasthan. In Andhra Pradesh a mere 1,400 acres were declared surplus and none distributed. Only in **Jammu and Kashmir** were ceiling laws fully implemented, about **230,000 acres** of surplus land being handed to tenants and landless labourers by the middle of 1955, without compensation. Taking India as a whole, only **2.4 million acres** were declared surplus by the end of 1970, of which about half was distributed - **about 0.3 per cent** of India's total cultivated land.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Jammu and Kashmir exception is the most economical proof available that the failure was political rather than technical. The same union, the same decade, the same administrative technology - and full implementation where the political settlement permitted expropriation without compensation.

### 4. The second wave: 1970-72 and what changed

✅ **Fact:** the context of the second attempt was the agrarian radicalism of the late 1960s - the Naxalite movement peaking in West Bengal and parts of Andhra Pradesh, Orissa and Bihar, and a widespread **land-grab movement in 1970** led by Communist and Socialist parties, with disturbances reported in 1969-70 from Assam, Andhra Pradesh, Bihar, Gujarat, Punjab, Rajasthan, Tamil Nadu, U.P. and West Bengal. The land seized was mostly government wasteland, land taken over but not distributed, and some homestead land; the movement was effectively suppressed and about **20,000 political activists were arrested**, but it drew national attention dramatically to the agrarian question.

✅ **Fact:** the Land Reform Implementation Committee of the National Development Council met in June 1964 and pressed chief ministers to plug loopholes. At a land-reform conference of chief ministers called by Indira Gandhi in **September 1970**, she argued that "the land reform measures implemented have failed to match the legitimate expectations which were first fostered among millions of cultivators during the national movement", and that India had "yet to create institutional conditions which would enable small farmers, tenants, and landless labourers to share in the agricultural New Deal". Most chief ministers rejected a reduction in ceiling limits outright.

✅ **Fact:** the Central Land Reforms Committee recommended in **August 1971** a substantial reduction in ceilings, withdrawal of exemptions such as those for "efficient" or mechanised farms, and application of ceilings to the family rather than the individual. Strengthened by the 1971 and 1972 election victories, the Congress secured approval of new national guidelines at the chief ministers' conference of **July 1972**:

1. ✅ ceiling for double-cropped perennially irrigated land within the range of **ten to eighteen acres**; **twenty-seven acres** for single-cropped land; **fifty-four acres** for inferior dry land;
2. ✅ ceiling applicable to a **family of five** - husband, wife and three minor children - with additional land for larger families up to a maximum of double the five-member ceiling;
3. ✅ priority in distribution of surplus land to landless agricultural workers, particularly Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes;
4. ✅ compensation for surplus land fixed well below market price, within the capacity of new allottees.

✅ **Fact:** resistance shifted to the courts, with hundreds of thousands of ceiling cases filed - one estimate mentions five hundred thousand pending in Andhra Pradesh alone. The government responded with the **Thirty-fourth Amendment in August 1974**, placing most revised ceiling laws in the **Ninth Schedule** so that they could not be challenged on constitutional grounds.

### 5. The measured outcome of both waves

| Date | Declared surplus | Distributed | Beneficiaries |
|---|---|---|---|
| ✅ End 1970 | 2.4 million acres | About 1.2 million acres | - |
| ✅ Early 1980s | - | Additional 2.27 million acres, against an estimated **32.25 million acres wilfully dispersed** to avoid ceilings | - |
| ✅ March 1985 | 7.2 million acres | 4.3 million acres | About 3.3 million, of whom 54.6 per cent were Scheduled Caste or Scheduled Tribe, receiving about 43.6 per cent of the area |
| ✅ Mid-1992 | 7.3 million acres | About 5 million acres | About 4.7 million |

✅ **Fact:** nearly 1.6 million acres of land declared surplus but undistributed was under litigation. There was wide regional variation: **West Bengal, with less than 3 per cent of India's cultivated area, contributed about a quarter of all land declared surplus**. Between 1985 and 1992 beneficiaries rose by 1.4 million while distributed area rose by only 0.1 million acres, which suggests new beneficiaries received only tiny plots or homestead land. The total area distributed amounted to only about **2 per cent of cultivated area**.

✅ **Fact:** the source records one genuinely important positive effect, quoting the agricultural economist C.H. Hanumantha Rao: the ceiling law "discouraged concentration of landownership beyond the ceiling level and thus prevented the possible dispossession of numerous small and marginal holders which would probably have occurred through a competitive process in the land market in the absence of a ceiling". In other words, the ceiling failed to redistribute and succeeded in preventing de-peasantisation.

✅ **Fact:** demography then completed what legislation had not. High population growth and repeated subdivision across generations - in the absence of primogeniture - left little land above ceiling limits. By one estimate, by 1976-77 nearly **97 per cent** of operated holdings were below 25 acres and **87 per cent** below 10 acres; very large semi-feudal holdings had become rare. Inequality among landowners was no longer the key issue; the problem of the landless or near-landless, estimated at nearly half the agricultural population, was.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the source's political conclusion is blunt and useful: further redistribution through lower ceilings is neither politically feasible nor economically viable, given that nearly 67 per cent of the workforce was engaged in agriculture in 1991 and that lower ceilings would multiply unviable holdings while uniting the landowning classes, by then mobilised under the farmers' movements, against any government attempting it. The remaining viable programme is homestead land or home sites, enforcement of minimum wages, and security and fair rents for sharecroppers, alongside off-farm rural employment.

### 6. Bhoodan: the alternative that was not a substitute

✅ **Fact:** Bhoodan attempted land reform through a **movement rather than legislation**, drawing on Gandhian constructive work and trusteeship. Acharya Vinoba Bhave organised an all-India federation of constructive workers, the Sarvodaya Samaj, and he and his followers walked village to village persuading larger landowners to donate at least **one-sixth** of their land for distribution among the landless. The target was **50 million acres**, one-sixth of India's 300 million acres of cultivable land - the idea being that each average family of five should give up one-sixth of its land, accepting the landless man as a member of the family. The movement was independent of government but supported by the Congress, and **Jayaprakash Narayan withdrew from active politics to join it in 1953**.

✅ **Fact:** Vinoba received the first donation on **18 April 1951 at Pochampalli** in the Telangana region of Andhra Pradesh, where the reverberations of the Communist-led armed peasant revolt were still being felt; within less than three months he had covered about 200 villages in the region and received 12,200 acres. The movement spread north, particularly to Bihar and Uttar Pradesh, receiving over **four million acres** by March 1956, after which it lost momentum.

✅ **Fact:** the distribution record is where the movement's claim breaks. A substantial part of the donated land was unfit for cultivation or under litigation. Out of nearly four and a half million acres available, only about **654,000 acres** had actually been distributed among **200,000 families** by the end of 1957; by early 1961 about **872,000 acres** had been distributed. The programme dragged on: in **June 1999** the Bihar government decided to dissolve the State Bhoodan Committee for its inability to distribute even half the Bhoodan land available over the previous 38 years.

✅ **Fact:** from the end of 1955 the movement took the new form of **Gramdan**, donation of the village, in which all land was declared owned collectively rather than by individuals. It started in Orissa and was most successful there: by the end of 1960 there were more than four and a half thousand Gramdan villages, of which 1,946 were in Orissa, 603 in Maharashtra, 543 in Kerala, 483 in Andhra Pradesh and about 250 in Madras. It has been argued that it succeeded mainly in villages where class differentiation had not emerged and there was little disparity in ownership, such as those inhabited by certain tribal communities.

### 7. Assessing Bhoodan fairly

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the source explicitly resists the easy dismissal, and a candidate who reproduces only the dismissal loses the more interesting answer. Three points deserve statement.

1. ✅ It was one of the very few attempts after independence to bring about land reform **through a movement** rather than through legislation from above.
2. ✅ Its potential rested on trusteeship: if landlords failed to behave as trustees, a satyagraha could be launched against them - which is precisely what Tamil Nadu Sarvodaya leaders proposed in 1961 against landlords who went back on Gramdan promises. The Sarvodaya Samaj as a whole, however, failed to make that transition to large-scale mass action.
3. ✅ It created a moral ambience favourable to the landless. The source cites the Communist leader E.M.S. Namboodiripad, through Kotovsky, acknowledging that the movement "to a certain extent stimulated political and other activity by the peasant masses and has created a favourable atmosphere for political propaganda and agitation for redistribution of the land" - which the source calls, ironically, perhaps the best appreciation of Bhoodan coming from its major critics.

✅ **Fact:** the standard criticism, that its purpose was to "serve as a brake on the revolutionary struggle of the peasants", is recorded and answered by noting that far more successful Gandhian movements have been characterised in the same way.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** Bhoodan could not substitute for structural reform because voluntary donation cannot reallocate land at scale, but it is misread when treated as merely reactionary; its failure was the failure to convert trusteeship into mass action.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** If ceilings released only about two per cent of cultivated area, the entire programme was a waste of four decades of political energy.

**Reply:** Two consequences survive the low yield. First, distribution was targeted: by March 1985, 54.6 per cent of beneficiaries were Scheduled Caste or Scheduled Tribe, receiving about 43.6 per cent of the area distributed, which made the programme a significant instrument of social redistribution even at small scale. Second, and more important, the ceiling suppressed the land market's tendency towards concentration and so prevented the dispossession of small and marginal holders that a free market in land would probably have produced. A policy can fail at its stated objective and still change the structure it operated on.

**Residual:** Whether a ceiling enacted in 1950 rather than 1961 would have released substantially more land cannot be established. The Jammu and Kashmir case suggests it might have; the absence of survey data before 1953, which the First Plan itself flagged, suggests the machinery did not then exist.

### UPSC application

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** ceiling questions reward exactness about three things - the two waves and their dates, the specific defects of the first wave, and the measured outcome. The Ninth Schedule protection of 1974 is a natural bridge to Polity, and should be crossed only far enough to note that ceiling laws were placed there to stop constitutional challenge; the doctrine of judicial review of Ninth Schedule entries belongs to the Polity owner.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Treating ceilings as a single law rather than two waves separated by the 1972 guidelines.
- Giving a national ceiling figure; limits were fixed by states and varied enormously.
- Forgetting the family-size escalators and the exemption lists, which are where the land actually escaped.
- Saying no land was distributed; about 5 million acres reached about 4.7 million beneficiaries by mid-1992.
- Dating Bhoodan's first donation to a year other than 1951 or a place other than Pochampalli.
- Claiming Bhoodan distributed most donated land; by early 1961 about 872,000 acres had been distributed of over four million donated.
- Missing the ceiling's unintended success - preventing concentration through the land market.

### Revision notes

1. Kisan Sabha 25-acre demand 1946; AICC committee under Nehru November 1947; Kumarappa Committee July 1949 at three times an economic holding.
2. First Plan left the limit to states and expected two to three years merely for survey and machinery.
3. Nagpur Congress, January 1959: ceilings in all states by end-1959, surplus to vest in panchayats.
4. Backlash: Ranga's hundred-MP letter of December 1958, resignation February 1959, Swatantra Party June 1959 with Rajagopalachari and Masani.
5. Most states legislated only by end-1961, fourteen years after the idea was mooted.
6. First-wave defects: very high ceilings; individual rather than family unit; family-size escalators up to 140 per cent, unlimited in Andhra Pradesh; wide exemptions including "efficiently managed farms"; delay enabling sale, benami transfer and eviction.
7. To end-1970: not an acre declared surplus in Bihar, Mysore, Kerala, Orissa and Rajasthan; 1,400 acres declared in Andhra Pradesh with none distributed; Jammu and Kashmir alone fully implemented, about 230,000 acres by mid-1955 without compensation; 2.4 million acres nationally, about 0.3 per cent of cultivated land.
8. Second wave: NDC committee June 1964; land-grab movement 1970 with about 20,000 arrests; Indira Gandhi's September 1970 conference; Central Land Reforms Committee August 1971; July 1972 guidelines.
9. 1972 guidelines: 10-18 acres double-cropped irrigated, 27 acres single-cropped, 54 acres inferior dry; family of five; SC/ST priority; below-market compensation.
10. Thirty-fourth Amendment, August 1974, placed revised ceiling laws in the Ninth Schedule.
11. Outcome: 7.2 million acres surplus and 4.3 million distributed to about 3.3 million beneficiaries by March 1985, 54.6 per cent SC/ST; about 5 million acres and 4.7 million beneficiaries by mid-1992; about 2 per cent of cultivated area; 32.25 million acres wilfully dispersed to evade.
12. West Bengal: under 3 per cent of cultivated area, about a quarter of all surplus declared.
13. Hanumantha Rao: ceilings prevented concentration and de-peasantisation through the land market.
14. Bhoodan: Vinoba Bhave; Sarvodaya Samaj; target 50 million of 300 million acres; first donation 18 April 1951 at Pochampalli; over 4 million acres by March 1956; about 654,000 acres to 200,000 families by end-1957 and about 872,000 acres by early 1961; Gramdan from end-1955, strongest in Orissa; JP joined 1953; Bihar dissolved its State Bhoodan Committee in June 1999.

### Concept check

**Question:** Ceiling legislation released about two per cent of cultivated area. Identify the one substantial structural effect it nevertheless had, and explain why that effect matters for the history of Indian agriculture.

**Model answer:** It killed the land market at the top and so prevented concentration. As C.H. Hanumantha Rao put it, the law discouraged accumulation beyond the ceiling and thereby prevented the dispossession of numerous small and marginal holders that a competitive land market would probably have produced. That matters because the standard trajectory of commercialising agriculture elsewhere has been the consolidation of holdings and the conversion of small cultivators into labourers, and India did not follow it: by 1976-77 about 97 per cent of operated holdings were under 25 acres and 87 per cent under 10 acres, and the share of holdings and area controlled by large owners kept declining. It also explains why the Green Revolution did not produce the general de-peasantisation predicted for it - the small cultivator who adopted the new technology was not being squeezed out of the land market simultaneously. The ceiling therefore failed at redistribution and succeeded at preservation, which is an unintended outcome but a structural one.

**Misconception to avoid:** Judging a policy only against its stated aim. A law that fails to transfer assets may still alter the rules of the market in which those assets move, and in this case the preventive effect was larger and more durable than the redistributive one.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

"Ceiling legislation failed because it was designed to fail." Critically examine this claim about Indian land reform. (15 marks, 250 words)

#### Model answer

The claim is too strong as a statement of intention but broadly accurate as a description of effect.

The design defects are undeniable. Ceilings were fixed far above prevailing holdings - 27 to 312 acres in Andhra Pradesh, 18 to 126 in Maharashtra, 50 in Assam - when over 70 per cent of holdings were under five acres. They applied to individuals rather than families and permitted escalators of 67 to 140 per cent for larger families, unlimited in Andhra Pradesh. Exemptions covered plantations, orchards, specialised livestock farms, sugar-factory farms and vaguely defined "efficiently managed" farms. The fourteen-year delay from the 1947 proposal to legislation by end-1961 allowed sale, partition, benami transfer and eviction; about 32.25 million acres were wilfully dispersed.

Yet "designed to fail" understates the contest. The Nagpur resolution of January 1959 was a genuine attempt, defeated by an opposition that produced the Swatantra Party in June 1959 and included the owner-cultivators created by abolition. The 1972 guidelines lowered ceilings to 10-18 acres for double-cropped irrigated land, made the family the unit and prioritised Scheduled Caste and Tribe allottees; the Thirty-fourth Amendment of 1974 shielded them in the Ninth Schedule. By March 1985, 4.3 million acres had reached about 3.3 million beneficiaries, 54.6 per cent of them SC or ST.

Jammu and Kashmir, which implemented fully by 1955, proves the constraint was political, not technical.

Ceilings were therefore not designed to fail; they were enacted after their intended targets had acquired the power to defeat them.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = at least four specific design defects with state figures 5 + the delay quantified and its consequence named 3 + the 1972-74 corrective sequence 3 + the outcome with the SC/ST share 2 + the Jammu and Kashmir counter-case as the decisive test of the claim 2.**

**Bridge to Lesson 10:** If land could not be redistributed, the founders' other institutional hope was that cultivators would pool it voluntarily. That hope produced India's most complete policy failure and, alongside it, its most complete cooperative success.

## Lesson 10 - Cooperatives: the failed joint farm and the successful dairy

Progress: 10/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Nagpur's cooperative resolution, the retreat, service cooperatives and credit, and the Anand pattern

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the survey history's chapter on cooperatives and the land-reform overview, read for the Nagpur sequence, the Thorner and Ladejinsky findings, the credit series and the full Anand and Operation Flood account
CA search: "White Revolution 2.0 dairy cooperatives Ministry of Cooperation India 2026 news"
CA found: Reported - an expansion programme under the Ministry of Cooperation aims to raise cooperative milk procurement by about half over five years to 2028-29 and to create 75,000 new dairy cooperative societies while strengthening 46,422 existing ones, with about 24,097 new societies formed and 31,394 strengthened as reported in 2026, and an explicit emphasis on women's participation
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: two cooperative models, opposite results

```text
 COOPERATIVE JOINT FARMING                      SERVICE COOPERATIVES (credit, inputs, marketing)
 pool the land; cultivate together              keep the land; pool the transaction
   |                                              |
 requires: surrender of managerial control      requires: a transaction the member repeats
           trust among unequal landholders                 and can verify
           a gain from scale that is visible
   |                                              |
 RESULT: negligible outside government pilot    RESULT: credit share 3.3% (1951-52) -> ~30% (1981)
         projects and bogus cooperatives                institutional credit 7.3% -> 63%+ of needs
   |                                              |
 WHY: no consensus; land is identity as well    THE EXTREME CASE - ANAND / OPERATION FLOOD
      as asset; the "gain" was asserted,        a daily transaction, daily payment, one member
      not demonstrated                          one vote, professional management hired by producers
```

*The difference is not ideology. It is whether the cooperative asks a member to surrender control of an asset or to gain from repeating a transaction.*

### 1. The proposal and the collapse of consensus

✅ **Fact:** Gandhi, Nehru, the Socialists and the Communists agreed that cooperativisation would improve Indian agriculture and particularly benefit the poor, and cooperativisation was an important element of the institutional agenda. But, as with ceilings, there was **no general consensus among the peasantry**. At independence the Congress proposed only pilot schemes on government unoccupied but cultivable land, clarifying that any move towards cooperativisation was to be by persuasion, with no force or compulsion.

✅ **Fact:** the Kumarappa Committee of July 1949 went further, recommending that the state be empowered to enforce varying degrees of cooperation for different types of farming, with below-basic holders required to cultivate jointly. The First Plan was more judicious, recommending encouragement rather than enforcement, though it envisaged that if a majority of owners and occupancy tenants owning at least half a village's land wished to enter cooperative management, their decision should bind the village.

✅ **Fact:** the Second Plan declared that its main task was to lay foundations so that "over a period of ten years or so a substantial proportion of agricultural lands are cultivated on cooperative lines". Expectations then rose further on the strength of reports of dramatic Chinese agricultural gains through cooperativisation. Two Indian delegations were sent to China in mid-1956 - one from the Planning Commission and one from the Union Ministry of Food and Agriculture - and both recommended a bold programme of extending cooperative farming.

✅ **Fact:** the Chinese comparison was later shown to be false. By one estimate, China's agricultural growth rate between 1954 and 1974 was only **2 per cent**, actually lower than India's **2.5 per cent** - a fact established only years after Mao's death in 1976.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is a rare and valuable example of policy driven by a factual error about another country, and it is worth stating in an answer because it disciplines the common claim that India simply lacked the will China had. The model India was urged to emulate was not delivering what it was reported to deliver.

✅ **Fact:** the **Nagpur Resolution of January 1959** stated that the future agrarian pattern should be cooperative joint farming, with land pooled for joint cultivation while farmers retained property rights and received a share of net produce in proportion to their land, and those who worked on the land receiving a share in proportion to work; and that service cooperatives should be organised throughout the country as a first step, **within three years**.

✅ **Fact:** the reaction was fierce. Critics in the press and Parliament argued that it was the first step to ending private property and would lead to forced collectivisation on the Soviet or Chinese pattern; within the Congress, Rajagopalachari, Ranga and Charan Singh mobilised against it. Facing serious division, Nehru struck a conciliatory note in **February 1959**, assuring Parliament that no coercion would be used and no new law passed, and reiterating only his personal conviction.

✅ **Fact:** the retreat was completed by external events. Chinese repression in Tibet in March 1959 and Chinese encroachments on the Indian border a few months later made any plan resembling the China model publicly indefensible. The Congress settled for service cooperatives over three years and left cooperative farms vague. Even that implied establishing **6,000 new cooperatives every month for three years**; the AICC training programme for the purpose was ignored by provincial leaders and abandoned after June 1959. The Third Plan accepted a modest target of ten pilot projects per district with a caveat the source calls "a wishful platitude, not a plan of action".

### 2. Why joint farming failed on the ground

✅ **Fact:** Daniel Thorner surveyed 117 of the "best" cooperatives across India between December 1958 and May 1959, and Wolf Ladejinsky made similar observations for the 1960s and 1970s. Two types of joint-farming cooperative were found:

- ✅ **Bogus cooperatives** formed by well-to-do influential families that enrolled agricultural labourers or ex-tenants as fictitious members, to evade ceiling or tenancy laws and to capture state subsidies and priority access to scarce inputs such as fertilisers, improved seeds and tractors.
- ✅ **State-sponsored pilot farms**, generally on poor, previously uncultivated land, made available to the landless, Harijans and displaced persons, run like government projects rather than genuine joint efforts, and generally expensive and unsuccessful, with none of the expected gains from scale.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the pattern is the recurring one of this topic. An instrument intended to help the weak became an instrument used by the strong, because the strong could satisfy its formal requirements and the weak could not.

### 3. Service cooperatives: elite capture and real achievement

✅ **Fact:** the service cooperatives reflected and reinforced the iniquitous structure of the countryside. Leadership - president, secretary, treasurer - typically consisted of the leading families of the village, which also controlled trade and moneylending, and which cornered scarce inputs including credit; low-interest agricultural credit was at times used for non-agricultural business, consumption and even moneylending. The movement also became "like a huge overstaffed government department" replicated at block, district, division and state levels.

✅ **Fact:** the exclusion of the landless was structural and documented. The Reserve Bank of India recommended in **1954** that rural credit cooperatives lend to the cultivator as the producer of a crop rather than as the owner of land; the recommendation was constantly refused, and insistence on land as security excluded the landless. In **1969** the Reserve Bank observed that tenant cultivators, agricultural labourers and others secured only **4 to 6 per cent** of total credit disbursed; the All-India Credit Review Committee of 1969 and the National Commission on Agriculture's interim report of 1971 confirmed the virtual exclusion of the landless and the disadvantage of small and marginal farmers.

✅ **Fact:** and yet the quantitative achievement is large. Primary Agricultural Credit Societies advanced loans worth about **Rs 23 crore in 1951-52**; in 1960-61 about 212,000 such societies disbursed nearly **Rs 200 crore**; by 1992-93 they were lending about **Rs 4,900 crore**. Cooperatives supplied only **3.3 per cent** of the cultivator's credit requirements in 1951-52 and nearly **30 per cent** by 1981. In 1951 the cultivator depended on non-institutional and generally rapacious sources - moneylender, trader, landlord - for **92.7 per cent** of credit; by 1981 institutional credit met over **63 per cent**, about 30 per cent from cooperatives and about 30 per cent from commercial banks, which after nationalisation in July 1969 were prevailed upon to lend to agriculture on a priority basis.

✅ **Fact:** the targeting improved. In 1979-80 about 34 per cent of short-term scheduled-commercial-bank loans went to households holding less than 2.5 acres, which were 33 per cent of households, and 57 per cent to households owning up to 5 acres, which were 49 per cent of households. Among cooperatives, those with holdings up to 5 acres received 34 per cent of credit. The landless, however, still received only about **4 per cent**.

✅ **Fact:** the structural weakness was repayment. Overdues of primary societies rose from 20 to 38 per cent of credit disbursed between 1960 and 1970, to an all-India average of 45 per cent by the mid-1970s, with provinces such as Bihar reaching 77 per cent - and defaulters were more often the well-to-do than the poor. Populist write-offs, such as the National Front government's 1990 decision to write off rural debts up to Rs 10,000, further eroded the viability of rural credit institutions.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this section is the strongest available rebuttal to a purely negative verdict on cooperatives, and the source gives it in Ladejinsky's words from a 1972 note to the World Bank: "Millions of farmers have benefited from them (cooperatives) and rural India without this landmark is hard to visualise." The credit transformation is also a precondition for Lesson 11; a technology package requiring purchased inputs is unusable by cultivators who can borrow only from a moneylender at his terms.

### 4. Anand and Operation Flood: the cooperative that worked

✅ **Fact:** the origin was a grievance. Peasants of Kaira (Kheda) district in Gujarat, supplying milk to Bombay, felt cheated by milk traders and approached Sardar Patel, who came from the district. At the initiative of Patel and Morarji Desai the farmers organised a union and pressed the Bombay government, with the help of a "milk strike", to buy milk from them. The **Kaira District Cooperative Milk Producers' Union Ltd was formally registered in December 1946** and began at Anand supplying **250 litres of milk a day**.

✅ **Fact:** the Gandhian freedom fighter **Tribhuvandas K. Patel**, who walked the villages persuading farmers to form milk cooperatives, became the union's first chairman in January 1947 and was re-elected for over twenty-five years. **Dr Verghese Kurien**, an engineer from Kerala, was chief executive from 1950 to 1973 and remained associated thereafter. By 2000 the union had grown from two village societies with under a hundred members each to **1,015 societies with 574,000 members**, handling nearly **one million litres a day** with an annual turnover of about **Rs 487 crore**.

✅ **Fact:** diversification followed: a milk-powder and butter factory in **1955**, partly to absorb higher winter yields, and the adoption of the brand name **Amul** in the same year; a factory in **1960** designed for 600 tonnes of cheese and 2,500 tonnes of baby food a year, the first in the world to make these on a large commercial scale from buffalo milk; a cattle-feed plant in **1964**, later using computing to optimise the feed mix; artificial insemination services - about 670,000 inseminations through 827 centres in 1994-95; a twenty-four-hour mobile veterinary service with twenty-nine radio-equipped vehicles; cattle insurance; fodder seed; vaccine manufacture; a newsletter; and the **Institute of Rural Management, Anand** for training rural-development managers. The **Gujarat Cooperative Milk Marketing Federation** was formed at Anand in **1974** as the apex marketing body.

✅ **Fact:** the structure is the explanation. The only condition of membership was being a genuine primary milk producer regularly supplying the cooperative; entry cost a nominal Rs 1 fee and the purchase of at least one Rs 10 share; members elected a managing committee by secret ballot, **one member one vote irrespective of shares held**; the committee elected an honorary chairman and employed paid staff for routine work; the chairman and a third of the committee retired annually by rotation, with elections contested at polling levels reaching 99 per cent. At district level a twelve-member board, six of them elected from village-society chairmen, appointed a managing director who hired professionals. By one estimate **one-third of the milk producers were landless**, and in recent years nearly **48 per cent** of rural household income in Kaira district came from dairying.

✅ **Fact:** replication followed. In **1964** Prime Minister Lal Bahadur Shastri wrote to chief ministers about a large programme of dairies on the Anand pattern, and the **National Dairy Development Board was created in 1965** at his initiative, located at Anand rather than Delhi at Kurien's insistence. The NDDB launched **Operation Flood**; by 1995 there were **69,875 village dairy cooperatives** across **170 milksheds** with a total membership of **8.9 million farmers** - though by one estimate this represented only **6.3 per cent of total milk production and 22 per cent of marketed milk**.

✅ **Fact:** a World Bank evaluation recorded the results: national milk production grew at 0.7 per cent a year until 1969 and at more than **4 per cent** annually since Operation Flood began; annual payments by the cooperative system to dairy farmers rose, in constant 1995 rupees, from **Rs 2.1 billion in 1972 to Rs 34 billion in 1995**; about **60 per cent of beneficiaries were marginal or small farmers and landless**; an indigenous dairy-equipment industry emerged, with only about 7 per cent of dairy equipment imported; and about **6,000 women's dairy cooperative societies** were established in cooperation with organisations including SEWA, with women-only membership and management committees, generally more efficiently run than male-dominated societies.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the World Bank evaluation's stated "lesson" is the most transferable idea in this lesson: by focusing a project on a predominant activity of the poor, **self-selection** results in a major portion of beneficiaries being poor, reaching target groups that are otherwise elusive - and doing so without the backlash that explicit group targeting provokes. The source draws the same parallel with the Employment Guarantee Scheme first launched in rural Maharashtra in the mid-1970s, whose chief beneficiaries were landless workers predominantly from Scheduled Castes and Tribes, again by self-selection rather than exclusive targeting.

✅ **Fact:** the Anand pattern spread beyond milk, to cooperatives of fruit and vegetable producers, oilseed cultivators, small-scale salt makers and tree growers at NDDB's initiative, sometimes against violent resistance from established traders - with the **Dhara** brand of vegetable oils as the best-known result.

### 5. The overview verdict on the institutional phase

✅ **Fact:** the source's summary of the whole institutional phase deserves to be held whole. India implemented wide-ranging land reforms within a modern democratic structure without violence or authoritarian force - no forced collectivisation as in the Soviet Union, no forcible expropriation and communes as in China, no external army of occupation as in Japan. Large semi-feudal landlords rack-renting the peasantry and extracting illegal cesses and *begar* largely became a thing of the past. State demand on the peasant virtually disappeared: many states scrapped land revenue and elsewhere its real value collapsed as prices rose while rates stayed fixed, falling from often half the net income from agriculture in the colonial period to below one per cent. Institutional credit weakened the moneylender's grip, rising from Rs 0.23 billion in 1950-51 to Rs 3.65 billion in 1965-66 and Rs 7.75 billion in 1972-73.

✅ **Fact:** Daniel Thorner's judgement on the same period: "It is sometimes said that the (initial) five-year plans neglected agriculture. This charge cannot be taken seriously. The facts are that in India's first twenty-one years of independence more has been done to foster change in agriculture and more change has actually taken place than in the preceding two hundred years."

✅ **Fact:** the residual failure is stated with equal clarity: the effort at cooperative joint farming failed as a way of solving rural poverty, inequity and landlessness; other efforts aimed at the underprivileged were often appropriated by the better off; and Indian agriculture did not move in the direction of socialism despite Avadi, the Directive Principles and Nagpur. Thorner again: "To the extent that the Government of India ever intended to (introduce) socialism in the countryside, we may say that it has been no more successful in that direction than was the British regime in introducing capitalism."

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Anand is a single district's success inflated into a national model; at 6.3 per cent of milk production it proves very little.

**Reply:** The share understates the structural effect, and the source supplies the measure that matters: the growth rate of national milk production rose from 0.7 per cent a year before 1969 to over 4 per cent a year afterwards, and 22 per cent of *marketed* milk is a different and more meaningful denominator than total production, most of which never enters a market. The model's importance is also institutional rather than volumetric - it demonstrated that professional management could be hired and controlled by small producers, which is the proposition that cooperative failure elsewhere appeared to refute.

**Residual:** Why the Anand structure proved so hard to replicate at the same quality elsewhere remains a live question. Self-selection through a daily transaction is part of the answer; continuity of leadership over decades is another; and neither is easy to legislate.

### UPSC application

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this lesson is the historical foundation for cross-owned questions on cooperatives and rural credit, which are owned by the Economy and Governance files. Its distinctive contribution is the contrast that no contemporary account supplies - joint farming failed while service and dairy cooperatives succeeded - and the reason, which is the difference between asking a member to surrender control of an asset and asking him to repeat a transaction he can verify.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Treating "cooperatives" as one undifferentiated programme; joint farming and service cooperatives had opposite fates.
- Forgetting that the Nagpur cooperative proposal was withdrawn in substance by February 1959 and killed by the China reversal.
- Dating the Kaira union to the Operation Flood period; it was registered in December 1946.
- Attributing the NDDB to Nehru; it was created in 1965 at Shastri's initiative.
- Claiming Operation Flood dominated Indian milk production; it accounted for about 6.3 per cent of production and 22 per cent of marketed milk by 1995.
- Presenting cooperative credit as a failure; its share rose from 3.3 per cent in 1951-52 to nearly 30 per cent by 1981, though the landless remained at about 4 per cent.

### Revision notes

1. Cooperativisation had elite consensus and no peasant consensus.
2. Kumarappa Committee 1949 proposed compulsion for below-basic holders; the First Plan preferred encouragement.
3. Two delegations went to China in mid-1956; the Chinese agricultural record was later found to be 2 per cent a year for 1954-74 against India's 2.5 per cent.
4. Nagpur, January 1959: cooperative joint farming as the future pattern; service cooperatives everywhere within three years.
5. Opposition from Rajagopalachari, Ranga and Charan Singh; Nehru's assurance in February 1959; Tibet and the border made the China model indefensible; the AICC training programme was abandoned after June 1959.
6. Service-cooperative target implied 6,000 new societies a month for three years.
7. Thorner surveyed 117 of the best cooperatives, December 1958 to May 1959; two types found - bogus cooperatives evading land laws and unsuccessful state pilot farms.
8. RBI 1954 crop-loan recommendation refused; RBI 1969 found tenants and labourers received only 4-6 per cent of credit.
9. PACS lending: about Rs 23 crore (1951-52), nearly Rs 200 crore through 212,000 societies (1960-61), about Rs 4,900 crore (1992-93).
10. Cooperative share of cultivator credit 3.3 per cent (1951-52) to nearly 30 per cent (1981); non-institutional sources 92.7 per cent (1951) to under 37 per cent (1981); the landless still about 4 per cent.
11. Overdues 20 to 38 per cent (1960-70), 45 per cent by the mid-1970s, Bihar 77 per cent; the 1990 write-off up to Rs 10,000 worsened viability.
12. Kaira union registered December 1946 at 250 litres a day; Tribhuvandas Patel chairman from January 1947; Kurien chief executive 1950-73; Amul brand 1955; cheese and baby-food plant 1960; cattle feed 1964; GCMMF 1974; IRMA founded at Anand.
13. NDDB created 1965 at Shastri's initiative, located at Anand; Operation Flood reached 69,875 societies, 170 milksheds and 8.9 million members by 1995 - 6.3 per cent of production, 22 per cent of marketed milk.
14. Milk production growth rose from 0.7 per cent a year before 1969 to over 4 per cent; payments to farmers rose from Rs 2.1 billion (1972) to Rs 34 billion (1995) in constant 1995 rupees; about 60 per cent of beneficiaries small, marginal or landless; about 6,000 women's societies.
15. One member one vote; annual retirement by rotation; polling up to 99 per cent; about one-third of producers landless.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using the contrast between cooperative joint farming and the Anand dairy cooperative, state the condition under which cooperation succeeds in Indian agriculture, and support it with evidence from both cases.

**Model answer:** Cooperation succeeds when it asks members to pool a repeated transaction they can verify, and fails when it asks them to surrender managerial control of land. Joint farming required pooling of land for common cultivation with produce shared in proportion to land and work, as the Nagpur Resolution of January 1959 proposed; it produced almost nothing outside two perverse forms that Daniel Thorner's survey of 117 of the best cooperatives identified - bogus societies formed by influential families to evade ceiling and tenancy laws and to capture subsidies and scarce inputs, and expensive state pilot farms on poor land that generated none of the expected gains from scale. The Anand model asked nothing of the member's land. It asked for milk, daily, and paid for it at the next collection, with membership open to any genuine producer for a Rs 1 fee and one Rs 10 share, one member one vote, annual retirement by rotation and professional management hired by producer-controlled boards; about a third of producers were landless. The result was national milk-production growth rising from 0.7 per cent a year before 1969 to over 4 per cent, and 69,875 village societies with 8.9 million members by 1995.

**Misconception to avoid:** Explaining the difference by ideology or by leadership alone. Leadership mattered, but the decisive variable is the nature of what is pooled: a daily divisible transaction can be verified and exited, whereas pooled land cannot be withdrawn if the arrangement disappoints.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

Evaluate the record of cooperatives in post-independence Indian agriculture. (10 marks, 150 words)

#### Model answer

The record splits by type. Cooperative joint farming failed: the Nagpur Resolution of January 1959 proposed it and was withdrawn within weeks; Thorner's survey of 117 leading cooperatives found mainly bogus societies and unsuccessful state pilot farms.

Service cooperatives succeeded substantially. Primary agricultural credit societies lent about Rs 23 crore in 1951-52 and about Rs 4,900 crore by 1992-93; cooperatives' share of cultivator credit rose from 3.3 per cent in 1951-52 to nearly 30 per cent by 1981, and institutional credit overall from 7.3 to over 63 per cent, breaking the moneylender's grip. Elite capture persisted; the landless still received about 4 per cent.

The dairy cooperative was the outstanding success. From the Kaira union of 1946 and the NDDB of 1965, Operation Flood reached 8.9 million members by 1995, and milk output growth rose from 0.7 to over 4 per cent a year.

Cooperation worked on transactions, not land.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the three-way split stated as the structure 2 + joint-farming failure evidenced 2 + credit transformation with two figures 3 + dairy success with two figures 2 + the transactions-not-land verdict 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 11:** With redistribution blocked and cooperation confined to transactions, the state's remaining instrument for raising rural output was technology - and it worked spectacularly, which is why its consequences are still argued about.

## Lesson 11 - The Green Revolution: technology instead of redistribution

Progress: 11/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the conjuncture, the package, diffusion in three phases, the distribution debate and the ecological cost

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the survey history's chapter on agricultural growth and the Green Revolution, read in full, together with the canonical basic and advanced files, which require the diffusion argument and the inequality critique to be stated together
CA search: "Punjab groundwater depletion paddy Green Revolution crop diversification news 2026"
CA found: Reported - in 2026 Punjab's groundwater extraction is recorded at about 152 per cent of annual recharge with roughly 72 per cent of blocks over-exploited, and the state has expanded a maize-promotion scheme to sixteen districts with a subsidy reported at Rs 17,500 per hectare for shifting out of paddy, while assured procurement and subsidised power continue to make paddy the rational private choice
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: why technology, and what it did

```text
   CONSTRAINTS CONVERGING BY THE MID-1960S
   area expansion nearing its limit  +  institutional reform stalled after abolition
   population growth ~2.2% a year    +  rising per capita income and planned industrialisation
   food prices rising from the mid-1950s; PL-480 imports from 1956
   1962 and 1965 wars; 1965 and 1966 droughts; aid used as leverage
                                  |
   THE NEW AGRICULTURAL STRATEGY (built on Nehru-era foundations)
   HYV seeds (Mexican dwarf wheat) + fertiliser + pesticide + assured irrigation
   + machinery + soil testing + extension + institutional credit
   concentrated on ~32 million acres, about 10% of cultivated area, with natural advantages
                                  |
   OUTPUT          DIFFUSION              DISTRIBUTION            ECOLOGY
   foodgrain +35%  phase 1 wheat, north-  scale-neutral?          groundwater,
   1967-68 to      west; phase 2 rice,    small farmers' input    fertiliser and
   1970-71         south and east;        intensity higher;       pesticide use,
   imports 10.3mt  phase 3 eastern India  insecure tenants lose;  yield plateau
   (1966) -> 3.6mt  and the south lead     inequality rises but    in long-cultivated
   (1970)                                  the poor gain in        areas
                                           absolute terms
```

*Every one of those four columns is examinable, and only the first is uncontested.*

### 1. Continuity, not rupture, with the Nehru era

✅ **Fact:** the view that agriculture was neglected in the Nehru years, or that the focus was only institutional, has increasingly been abandoned. Plan outlays on agriculture and irrigation were **31 per cent of the total in the First Plan** and **between 20 and 24 per cent** in every subsequent Plan, irrespective of changes of regime. Massive irrigation and power projects such as Bhakra-Nangal, agricultural universities, research laboratories and fertiliser plants took their place alongside steel plants in the Nehruvian vision.

✅ **Fact:** the precursor of the strategy was launched in Nehru's lifetime: the **Intensive Agricultural Districts Programme**, the package approach applied to selected districts with natural advantages, was begun in **fifteen districts, one per state**, on an experimental basis during the Third Plan. The scholar G.S. Bhalla's formulation is quoted directly: the qualitative technological transformation "came about not during his lifetime but soon after his death. But the foundations for the technological development were laid during Nehru's time."

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this matters for two examination purposes. It refutes the neglect thesis with outlay data, and it explains why the strategy could be scaled so rapidly after 1965 - the research system, irrigation capacity and extension machinery already existed.

### 2. The conjuncture of the mid-1960s

✅ **Fact:** despite creditable growth of about 3 per cent a year between 1949 and 1965, India faced food shortages from the mid-1950s and a crisis by the mid-1960s, with agricultural growth beginning to stagnate in the early 1960s. Population growth had jumped to about **2.2 per cent a year** from about 1 per cent in the previous half-century; per capita income was rising; planned industrialisation added demand. PL-480 imports began in **1956** with nearly three million tonnes in the first year, rising to more than four and a half million tonnes by 1963. Then came the wars of 1962 and 1965 and the droughts of 1965-66, an output fall of 17 per cent with food output down 20 per cent, food prices rising nearly 20 per cent a year between 1965 and 1968, and imports of more than **ten million tonnes** in 1966, with famine conditions emerging in parts of Bihar and U.P.

✅ **Fact:** the alternative to imports was large-scale forced procurement from the countryside at huge human cost - the path adopted by Russia and China, which lacked democracy as a safeguard, and which India found unacceptable.

✅ **Fact:** the strategy was implemented in earnest under Prime Minister **Lal Bahadur Shastri**, Food Minister **C. Subramaniam**, and **Indira Gandhi** after 1966. The World Bank's Bell Mission recommended the transition and the United States pressed for it, but they appear to have been "leaning on open doors", since a considerable domestic consensus for the change had already emerged.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the "open doors" formulation is the one to carry into an answer, because it disposes of two opposite errors at once - that the Green Revolution was an American imposition, and that external pressure played no part.

### 3. The package, and the investment behind it

✅ **Fact:** critical inputs - **high-yielding variety seeds**, with the suitability of high-yielding Mexican dwarf wheat to Indian conditions proving an extremely timely scientific breakthrough, together with chemical fertilisers and pesticides, agricultural machinery including tractors and pump sets, soil-testing facilities, agricultural education and institutional credit - were concentrated on areas with assured irrigation and other natural and institutional advantages. Some **32 million acres, about 10 per cent of the total cultivated area**, were initially chosen for the package on top priority.

| Input | Change |
|---|---|
| ✅ Institutional finance to agriculture | Doubled between 1968 and 1973 |
| ✅ Agricultural Prices Commission | Set up in **1965**, with efforts to assure the farmer a market at sustained remunerative prices |
| ✅ Gross irrigated area | Growth rose from about 1 million hectares a year before the Green Revolution to about 2.5 million hectares a year during the 1970s |
| ✅ Electric and diesel pump sets | 421,000 (1960-61) to 2.4 million (1970-71) |
| ✅ Tube wells | 90,000 to 460,000 over the same decade |
| ✅ Tractors | 31,000 to 140,000 |
| ✅ Chemical fertiliser consumption | 306,000 metric tonnes (1960-61) to 2,350,000 (1970-71), mostly in the second half of the decade |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** public investment, institutional credit, remunerative prices and cheap new technology together raised the profitability of *private* investment by farmers, which is why total gross capital formation in agriculture began to grow faster. The state did not replace the cultivator's investment; it changed the return on it.

### 4. Output and the marketable surplus

✅ **Fact:** foodgrain production rose by **35 per cent between 1967-68 and 1970-71**; aggregate food production rose from **89 to 112 million tonnes between 1964-65 and 1971-72**, calculated as a 10 per cent per capita increase. Net food imports fell from 10.3 million tonnes in 1966 to 3.6 million in 1970, while food availability rose from 73.5 to 99.5 million tonnes over the same period, then to 110.25 million tonnes in 1978 and 128.8 million in 1984. By the 1980s India held buffer stocks of over 30 million tonnes and was exporting food to repay earlier loans or as loans to food-deficit countries. It has been estimated that without the new strategy India would have had to import a minimum of about **8 to 10 million tonnes of wheat yearly at a cost of $600 to 800 million**.

✅ **Fact:** the structural mechanism was a change in the *source* of growth. Between 1949-50 and 1964-65, about 51 per cent of output growth came from area expansion, growing at 1.61 per cent a year, and 49 per cent from yield, growing at 1.5 per cent. Between **1967-68 and 1989-90, about 80 per cent** of growth came from yield increases, growing at 2.5 per cent a year, while acreage grew only 0.26 per cent and explained 20 per cent.

✅ **Fact:** the most under-stated effect is the **marketable surplus**. The initial breakthrough occurred in relatively developed regions where consumption levels were already high, so a large proportion of additional output was marketed; labour use per unit of output tended to decline, reducing the share of output set aside for labour consumption; and because growth came from yields rather than acreage, less foodgrain was needed as seed per unit of output. It was the marketed surplus - not any unprecedented rise in the aggregate growth rate - that made internal procurement and large public food stocks possible, ending dependence on PL-480 for industrial and urban food needs.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the sentence that turns an agricultural fact into a political one, and it links directly to the sovereignty argument of Lesson 4. Food self-sufficiency was not simply more food; it was the removal of an instrument of leverage over Indian foreign policy.

### 5. Diffusion: the regional question, answered with phases

✅ **Fact:** the persistent early criticism was that concentrating resources on already-advantaged regions would accentuate regional inequality. The research of scholars such as G.S. Bhalla shows diffusion in three phases:

| Phase | All-India compound growth | Where growth concentrated |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ 1962-65 to 1970-73 | 2.08 per cent a year | Wheat in Punjab, Haryana and western U.P.; Punjab at a "stupendous" **6.63 per cent** |
| ✅ 1970-73 to 1980-83 | 2.38 per cent a year | HYV technology extended from wheat to rice: eastern U.P., coastal Andhra Pradesh, parts of Karnataka and Tamil Nadu; Maharashtra, Gujarat and Andhra Pradesh grew faster than the all-India rate |
| ✅ 1980-83 to 1992-95 | 3.4 per cent a year, unprecedented | Eastern region - West Bengal at **5.39 per cent**, with Bihar, Assam and Orissa; the southern region and Madhya Pradesh and Rajasthan grew rapidly, and for the first time the south grew faster than the north-west |

✅ **Fact:** by the end of the third phase the coefficient of variation of output growth and per-hectare yield levels **between states had fallen substantially** compared with earlier decades, so the period saw both faster aggregate growth and a more diversified growth pattern that reduced regional inequality.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the honest version of the regional argument is therefore chronological. The criticism was correct for the first phase and became progressively less correct; the solution to early concentration was always to spread the technology rather than to oppose it. An answer that states only the first phase is describing 1970 and calling it a verdict.

### 6. The distribution debate, stated as a genuine dispute

This is the one place in the topic where a careful answer must hold two positions simultaneously, and they are stated here with their evidence, not blended.

**Position A - the diffusion and scale-neutrality argument.**

✅ **Fact:** early predictions of general class polarisation - that rich peasants and capitalist farmers would be strengthened at the expense of small peasants and tenants, that mechanisation would displace labour and depress wages, that a process of de-peasantisation would create conditions for agrarian revolt, summarised in the slogan that "the Green Revolution will lead to the Red Revolution" - are held by the source to have been largely unfounded. The evidence offered:

- ✅ Targeted programmes accompanied the strategy from the start: the Rural Works Programme, the Small Farmers Development Agency, the Marginal Farmers and Agricultural Labourers Scheme, the Crash Scheme for Rural Employment and the Employment Guarantee Scheme in Maharashtra. SFDA and MFAL identified more than a million small farmers and over half a million marginal farmers for loans, with investment subsidies of 25 and 33.3 per cent respectively.
- ✅ The economist **Raj Krishna reported in 1979** that "small farmers, as a class, command more productive assets and inputs per unit of land than large farmers": small farmers with holdings of five acres or less cultivated only **21 per cent** of cultivated area but accounted for **31.4 per cent** of net irrigated area, **32 per cent** of fertiliser use, **33 per cent** of agricultural credit and **26 per cent** of the value of output.
- ✅ The technology was not only scale-neutral but appears to have produced an inverse relationship between scale and productivity; the small farmer became relatively more viable and did not have to sell out in distress. In 1980-81, cultivators operating 25 acres or less were nearly **98 per cent** of operational holdings, cultivating 77.2 per cent of the area.
- ✅ On employment, Wolf Ladejinsky reported from Punjab in **February 1969** that "the demand for casual labour has increased and so have wages and the landless laborer is somewhat better off than in the past"; the source's formulation is that "the 'victims' of tractorization were bullocks not labour". In Punjab the number of agricultural labourers is said to have **trebled between 1961 and 1981** while landless agricultural households declined, with additional demand met by large-scale migration from eastern U.P. and Bihar. Punjab saw a striking increase of about **50 per cent in urban employment between 1971 and 1981**.
- ✅ Non-agricultural employment expanded through agro-industries, trade and warehousing, transport, manufacture and repair of implements and machinery, and rising rural demand for construction trades and consumer durables.

**Position B - the inequality, access and ecology critique.**

✅ **Fact:** critical scholarship stresses unequal access to irrigation and credit, regional lag, labour displacement in some settings and ecological costs, and this must be recorded as a live position rather than a refuted one.

✅ **Fact:** the source itself concedes substantial ground to it:

- ✅ Tenants and sharecroppers without security of tenure were "perhaps the only losers": rents and land values rose sharply where the Green Revolution spread, reaching as high as 70 per cent of gross produce in parts of Punjab, and owners tended to remove unprotected tenants to resume self-cultivation with hired labour and modern equipment.
- ✅ The rate of increase in agricultural employment slackened in later phases - the employment elasticity of output growth declined - though the source's characterisation is a failure to generate *sufficient additional* employment rather than displacement.
- ✅ "The Green Revolution did however contribute to increase in inequality in the countryside. But the poor too benefited in absolute terms though their well-to-do neighbours did relatively far better."
- ✅ Ecological cost is recorded without qualification: the negative impact of excessive fertiliser and pesticide use, the plateauing of growth rates in long-cultivated areas such as Punjab, and unsustainable groundwater withdrawal without adequate recharge.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the defensible examination position states the output achievement as settled, the early regional concentration as historically accurate, the diffusion as documented, and the distributive verdict as **contested** - naming the diffusion-and-scale-neutrality argument and the unequal-access critique as two positions with different evidence bases. Attributing "the Green Revolution widened inequality" to the source is a misattribution; attributing "it had no inequality effect" is equally wrong, since the source concedes relative inequality explicitly.

### 7. The ecological problem, and why there is no easy answer

✅ **Fact:** the source refuses the simple environmentalist conclusion on empirical grounds. Ecological degradation in India arises mainly from extension of cultivation to marginal and sub-marginal dryland and from deforestation, and "across different states in India, the extension of areas under cultivation and the denudation of forests seems to be high where the progress of yield-increasing technology is slow", because the poor are then forced onto marginal lands, commons and forests. **M.S. Swaminathan** estimated that producing the then current level of foodgrains at pre-Green Revolution yields of wheat and rice would require an additional **80 million hectares** - an impossible increase of about **66 per cent** in cultivable area.

✅ **Fact:** the prescription recorded is education of farmers against excessive and improper fertiliser and pesticide use and wasteful irrigation, retention of biodiversity, learning from traditional methods of ecological balance while using modern technology, and priority to research in biotechnology.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the structure of that argument - yield-increasing technology as forest-saving - is a genuinely counter-intuitive point and it is high-value in an answer, because it converts the ecology paragraph from a list of complaints into a trade-off analysis.

### 8. What the Green Revolution did to politics

✅ **Fact:** the Green Revolution "has not spawned any 'Red Revolution' in the countryside". Peasant protest and militancy rose, but not as movements of the lowest strata demanding systemic overthrow - rather as movements of small, medium and large peasants who were beneficiaries of the system and wanted more from it through higher output prices and lower input costs via subsidy. Over time the political clout of these sections increased and governments felt compelled to make concessions that were often not economically viable, such as power supplied far below cost, and free in some states.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** the Green Revolution solved the food problem, created a new rural political class, and transferred the agrarian question from the distribution of land to the terms of trade for output.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Treating the distributive question as "contested" is evasion; the source clearly favours one side.

**Reply:** It does favour one side, and the honest response is to say so and to mark the concessions it makes, which are substantial: relative inequality increased; unprotected tenants lost; rents rose to as much as 70 per cent of gross produce in parts of Punjab; employment elasticity declined; ecological costs are real. A candidate who presents the diffusion argument as the source's position, cites the critical literature as the alternative position, and notes the conceded points is not evading anything - that is what a live historiographical dispute looks like when reported accurately.

**Residual:** The counterfactual remains unresolvable. Nobody can show what distributional outcome a differently sequenced strategy - say, ceilings enforced first and technology introduced afterwards - would have produced, because no Indian state attempted that sequence at scale.

### UPSC application

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** questions on India's transition from food deficit to food security route primarily to Geography and Economy owners; what this lesson supplies is the historical explanation - the pre-1965 foundations, the crisis that forced the choice, the package, the phases of diffusion and the political consequence. Use the phase table when the question asks about regional inequality, and use Raj Krishna's input-intensity figures when the question asks about small farmers. Keep present-day agricultural schemes out of a history answer.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Saying agriculture was neglected before 1965; outlays were 20-31 per cent of Plan expenditure and the IADP ran in fifteen districts in the Third Plan.
- Treating the Green Revolution as purely a seed; it was a package including irrigation, fertiliser, credit, price assurance and extension.
- Describing it as an American imposition; the Bell Mission and the United States were "leaning on open doors".
- Presenting regional concentration as permanent; three phases are documented, and inter-state variation fell by the third.
- Attributing "widened inequality" to the source, which argues diffusion and scale-neutrality while conceding relative inequality.
- Claiming labour displacement; Punjab's agricultural labourers trebled between 1961 and 1981, with in-migration from eastern U.P. and Bihar.
- Omitting the losers who are actually identified - insecure tenants and sharecroppers.

### Revision notes

1. Plan outlays on agriculture and irrigation: 31 per cent in the First Plan and 20-24 per cent thereafter; IADP in fifteen districts during the Third Plan.
2. Population growth of about 2.2 per cent, stagnating yields and PL-480 imports from 1956 created the pre-crisis constraint.
3. Wars of 1962 and 1965, droughts of 1965-66, food output down 20 per cent, imports over ten million tonnes in 1966.
4. The package: HYV seeds including Mexican dwarf wheat, fertiliser, pesticides, machinery, soil testing, extension and credit, concentrated on about 32 million acres, some 10 per cent of cultivated area.
5. Agricultural Prices Commission 1965; institutional finance doubled 1968-73; irrigated-area growth from about 1 to about 2.5 million hectares a year.
6. Pump sets 421,000 to 2.4 million; tube wells 90,000 to 460,000; tractors 31,000 to 140,000; fertiliser 306,000 to 2,350,000 tonnes, 1960-61 to 1970-71.
7. Foodgrain output up 35 per cent, 1967-68 to 1970-71; food production 89 to 112 million tonnes, 1964-65 to 1971-72; stocks over 30 million tonnes by the 1980s.
8. Source of growth shifted: area 51 per cent and yield 49 per cent before 1964-65; yield about 80 per cent from 1967-68 to 1989-90.
9. Marketable surplus, not the aggregate growth rate, ended PL-480 dependence.
10. Three diffusion phases: 2.08 per cent with Punjab at 6.63; 2.38 per cent with rice spreading south and east; 3.4 per cent with West Bengal at 5.39 and the south overtaking the north-west; inter-state variation fell.
11. Raj Krishna, 1979: small farmers on 21 per cent of area held 31.4 per cent of net irrigated area, 32 per cent of fertiliser, 33 per cent of credit and produced 26 per cent of output value.
12. In 1980-81 holdings of 25 acres or less were about 98 per cent of operational holdings on 77.2 per cent of area.
13. Ladejinsky, February 1969: casual-labour demand and wages rose; Punjab's agricultural labourers trebled 1961-81; Punjab urban employment up about 50 per cent 1971-81.
14. Losers: tenants and sharecroppers without security; rents up to 70 per cent of gross produce in parts of Punjab.
15. Poverty programmes: Rural Works Programme, SFDA, MFAL, Crash Scheme for Rural Employment, Maharashtra's Employment Guarantee Scheme; employment generated rose from about 20 million person-days in the mid-1960s to 850 million in 1988-89.
16. Ecology: fertiliser and pesticide damage, groundwater depletion, yield plateaus; but Swaminathan's estimate that pre-Green Revolution yields would need 80 million extra hectares, about 66 per cent more cultivable area.
17. Politics: no "Red Revolution"; instead a beneficiary peasantry demanding higher prices and cheaper inputs.

### Concept check

**Question:** State the settled and the contested components of the Green Revolution verdict, and show how a "critically examine" answer should handle each.

**Model answer:** Settled: the output achievement and its mechanism. Foodgrain production rose 35 per cent between 1967-68 and 1970-71; net imports fell from 10.3 million tonnes in 1966 to 3.6 million in 1970; buffer stocks passed 30 million tonnes in the 1980s; and after 1967-68 roughly 80 per cent of output growth came from yield rather than acreage, which also generated the marketed surplus that ended PL-480 dependence. Also settled is the early regional concentration in wheat in Punjab, Haryana and western Uttar Pradesh, and its subsequent spread to rice regions and then to eastern India, with inter-state variation in yields falling by the 1980s and 1990s. Contested: the distributive verdict.
