One position holds that the technology was broadly scale-neutral, citing Raj Krishna's 1979 finding that farmers with five acres or less cultivated 21 per cent of area but used 32 per cent of fertiliser and 33 per cent of credit and produced 26 per cent of output value, and that agricultural labour demand rose rather than fell. The other stresses unequal access to irrigation and credit, regional lag and ecological cost. A "critically examine" answer states the settled findings as fact, presents both distributive positions with their evidence, records the conceded points - relative inequality rose, insecure tenants lost as rents climbed towards 70 per cent of gross produce in parts of Punjab, employment elasticity fell, groundwater and soil suffered - and returns a graded verdict rather than choosing a side.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assigning the inequality thesis to the survey source. It argues diffusion and scale-neutrality; the inequality thesis comes from critical agrarian and environmental scholarship, and confusing the two produces a misattributed citation that an examiner can detect.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

Assess the social consequences of the Green Revolution in India. (20 marks, 250 words)

#### Model answer

The social consequences must be assessed separately from the output record, which is not in dispute: foodgrain production rose 35 per cent between 1967-68 and 1970-71, imports fell from 10.3 to 3.6 million tonnes between 1966 and 1970 and stocks crossed 30 million tonnes by the 1980s.

Four social consequences are reasonably established. First, rural class structure changed: an owner-cultivator and rich-peasant stratum consolidated and, in 1980-81, holdings of 25 acres or less still formed about 98 per cent of operational holdings on 77.2 per cent of the area, so concentration did not occur. Second, the small farmer became more viable rather than less; Raj Krishna found in 1979 that holdings of five acres or less used 32 per cent of fertiliser and 33 per cent of credit on 21 per cent of area. Third, labour demand rose in Green Revolution regions, with Punjab's agricultural labourers trebling between 1961 and 1981. Fourth, insecure tenants and sharecroppers lost, as land values and rents rose, reaching 70 per cent of gross produce in parts of Punjab, and owners resumed cultivation with hired labour.

Two consequences are contested. Relative inequality increased even as the poor gained absolutely, and critical scholarship stresses unequal access to irrigation and credit and regional lag, against the documented three-phase diffusion.

Politically the change was decisive: agitation shifted from land to prices and subsidies, producing the farmers' movements of the 1980s.

The verdict is therefore an absolute improvement with a widened relative gap and a transformed rural politics.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = output record stated briefly and not allowed to dominate 3 + four established social consequences with named evidence 8 + the contested element presented as contested, not decided 4 + the political transformation identified 3 + graded verdict distinguishing absolute from relative 2.**

**Bridge to Lesson 12:** If the beneficiaries of the new technology became a political class, the history of rural protest should show a reversal - and it does, from the landless and the tenant to the surplus-producing farmer.

## Lesson 12 - Agrarian struggles, 1946-1990: the reversal of class content

Progress: 12/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Telangana, the PEPSU muzaras, Naxalbari, Srikakulam and the "new" farmers' movements

Book context: queried - the survey history's chapter on agrarian struggles since independence, read in full for Telangana, PEPSU, Naxalbari, Srikakulam and the farmers' movements, with the canonical basic file's caution against merging the two families of movement
CA search: "Naxalism Left Wing Extremism India 2026 government claims end deadline affected districts"
CA found: Reported - after a deadline of 31 March 2026 the Union home ministry informed states that no district now falls in the left-wing-extremism-affected category, with legacy districts in Chhattisgarh and Jharkhand retained for monitoring, against about 126 affected districts around 2013-14
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the reversal, in one column

```text
 1946-51  TELANGANA          landless, tenants, forced labour    against jagirdars/deshmukhs
          PEPSU MUZARA       occupancy tenants                   against biswedars
                 |
                 |   land reform legislation; abolition; partial tenancy security
                 |
 1967-71  NAXALBARI          sharecroppers, tribal labour        against jotedars
          SRIKAKULAM         Jatapu and Savara tribals           against moneylenders/landlords
                 |
                 |   Green Revolution; surplus producers; procurement and input costs
                 |
 1980-86  NASIK 1980         surplus-producing farmers           against THE STATE'S PRICES
          SISAULI 1986       landowning peasantry                against electricity charges
                 |
 DEMAND CHANGES: land -> security -> PRICES, SUBSIDIES, WRITE-OFFS
 ADVERSARY CHANGES: landlord -> landlord -> the government itself
 CLASS CHANGES: landless and tenant -> sharecropper and tribal -> owner-cultivator
```

*The caution here is absolute: do not merge these two families. They represent opposed rural interests, and an answer that treats "peasant movements after 1947" as one phenomenon has missed the entire point.*

### 1. Telangana, 1946-1951

✅ **Fact:** the Telugu-speaking area of Hyderabad State under the Nizam had seen political opposition from the late 1930s through the State Congress and the Andhra Mahasabha. Communists became a major force after the ban on the party was lifted in 1942 and took control of the Andhra Mahasabha. Peasants suffered extreme feudal-type oppression from **jagirdars and deshmukhs**, some owning thousands of acres; the organising issues were the hated forced grain levy and ***veth begar***, forced labour extracted by landlords and officials. The movement spread rapidly from 1945.

✅ **Fact:** the decisive phase was **August 1947 to September 1948**, when Communists rode the anti-Nizam, pro-integration wave, establishing a firm base in Nalgonda, Warangal and Khammam districts as landlords and officials fled to the towns. Peasants were organised into *sabhas* and guerrilla bands (*dalams*), armed mostly with slings, sticks, stones and later crude country guns; control was claimed over a large number of villages, the number mentioned being **3,000**. Land relations were reordered: land taken by landlords during the Depression in lieu of debt was returned; government waste and forest land was distributed; agricultural wages were raised; women's issues such as wife-beating were taken up; and "ceilings" on landlords' land were declared, first at **500 acres** and then at **100**, with the surplus distributed.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** one finding from that episode deserves to be carried into any answer about land redistribution in India. The source records that enthusiasm was greatest for recovering land lost to landlords in living memory, next for occupying government waste and forest land, and least for occupying the landlord's surplus land - even when offered in place of land that could not be restored. Peasants believed strongly in their claim to ancestral land and to uncultivated land, and felt little claim to a landlord's surplus; and they calculated correctly, because after the movement declined they retained the first two categories and not the third.

✅ **Fact:** the Indian Army moved into Hyderabad on **13 September 1948** and the Nizam surrendered within days; the army was greeted as a liberating force, including in rural areas. The Communists, however, decided to keep their arms and fight what they called a liberation war against the "pro-imperialist, bourgeois-landlord" government, attacking the Indian Army as they had the Razakars. The source's judgement is unambiguous: an unnecessary and tragic conflict ensued, activists were flushed out within months at great cost to thousands of peasants; the movement was officially withdrawn only in **1951**, after the party changed its line; perhaps around **500 had died and about 10,000 were in jail**.

✅ **Fact:** the state's response was rapid. The **Jagirdari Abolition Regulation** was laid down in **1949** and the **Hyderabad Tenancy and Agricultural Lands Act** passed in **1950**; over **600,000 tenants covering over one-quarter of the cultivated area** were declared protected with a right to purchase on easy terms; ceilings were introduced in the mid-1950s; and land reforms were much better implemented because of the peasants' high political consciousness. Returning landlords could not restore the old ways - they often sold land cheaply, faced pressure for higher wages, and did not try hard to recover peasants' own or waste lands.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the causal claim to make is precise: the back of landlordism in Telangana was broken during the **anti-Nizam, pro-integration** phase, when the Communists' position as leaders of a popular upsurge let them articulate radical peasant demands. The armed phase against the Indian state afterwards was not dictated by the peasant movement's needs. This also explains, without repeating the integration session, why land reform implementation in Telangana outperformed neighbouring regions.

### 2. PEPSU: the muzara movement

✅ **Fact:** in Patiala, the largest princely state in Punjab, **biswedars** who had originally held only revenue-collecting or *mafi* rights had used administrative influence to claim proprietary status - imitating the British Indian pattern in which revenue collectors became landowners - and reduced the cultivating proprietors of roughly **800 villages, one-sixth of the state's area**, to occupancy tenants and tenants-at-will. The tenants regarded these landlords as parvenus with no legitimate claim, rather than as an old established gentry whose rights had acquired social legitimacy through antiquity.

✅ **Fact:** a powerful movement emerged from 1939 and escalated into open confrontation from 1945 over non-payment of *botai* (rent in kind) and possession of land; the Praja Mandal, under the influence of **Brish Bhan**, extended support. After independence the Maharaja launched severe repression, which decreased after the formation of **PEPSU in July 1948**.

✅ **Fact:** with the state unable to assert authority, and some landlords using armed gangs, the movement organised its own armed volunteer corps through the **Lal Communist Party**, formed in 1948 by Teja Singh Swatantar and a breakaway group of Punjab Communists from the Kirti tradition. Contrary to popular and Communist mythology, the armed force never exceeded **30 or 40 people**, the largest estimate being 100, and it was not meant to confront the state - as the **Kishangarh incident of January 1949** showed, when the leadership sent away the main body in anticipation of a government assault, four members died, and those arrested were acquitted after a defence organised by left-wing Congressmen led by Brish Bhan.

✅ **Fact:** resolution came through legislation. A Congress ministry in 1951 with Brish Bhan as deputy chief minister set up an Agrarian Reforms Enquiry Committee, and the **PEPSU Tenancy (Temporary Provision) Act** of January 1952 protected tenants against eviction. After the general elections and a period of political instability, President's Rule brought the qualitative change: the **PEPSU Occupancy Tenants (Vesting of Proprietary Rights) Act, 1953**, allowed occupancy tenants to become owners on payment of compensation amounting to **twelve times the land revenue** - a modest sum given wartime and post-war inflation and revenue still assessed at pre-war rates. No further resistance was reported.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the source's political conclusion is worth stating because it recurs across this period: the Communists continued to condemn the settlement as inadequate because the biswedars were not expropriated without compensation, and were isolated from the peasants as a result. The movement's beneficiaries accepted a legislated compromise; the party that had led them rejected it and lost them. A movement's objective and its leadership's programme are not the same thing.

### 3. Naxalbari and Srikakulam

✅ **Fact:** the first non-Congress United Front government in West Bengal was sworn in on **2 March 1967**, and its land revenue minister **Harekrishna Konar** announced quick distribution of surplus land and an end to eviction of sharecroppers, calling for peasants' initiative and organised force to assist implementation. Distribution proved slow - much land was under litigation, and verification, rival claims and the grant of *pattas* took time.

✅ **Fact:** in **Naxalbari** in Darjeeling district, Communists had organised sharecroppers and tea-estate labour, mostly Santhal, Oraon and Rajbanshi, since the early 1950s. Sharecroppers worked under the *adhiar* system, in which the *jotedar* supplied ploughs, bullocks and seeds for a share of the crop; disputes over shares and evictions increased with the United Front's arrival, because of fear that sharecroppers would be given land. **Charu Mazumdar**, with **Kanu Sanyal** and the tribal leader **Jangal Santhal**, organised a peasants' conference under the Siliguri sub-division of the party only **sixteen days** after the United Front took office, calling for an end to landlords' monopoly of land, distribution through peasant committees and armed resistance to landlords and to both governments.

✅ **Fact:** between April and May 1967 villages were organised; some claims put **15,000 to 20,000 peasants** as full-time activists; peasant committees became nuclei of armed guards who occupied land, burnt land records, declared debts cancelled, delivered death sentences on hated landowners and set up a parallel administration, using bows, arrows and spears supplemented by seized guns. Reported strongholds were Hatigisha, Buraganj and Chowpukhuria under the Naxalbari, Kharibari and Phansidewa police stations.

✅ **Fact:** the movement was over by **July 1967**, with most activists and leaders including Jangal Santhal in jail. The party could not remain in government and sanction the action; after conciliation failed it expelled the dissident leaders, which triggered the coming together of extreme-left forces and eventually the formation of **CPI(ML)** in 1969. The Naxalite movement then survived mainly in towns with students as its main force, characterised by street warfare between armed groups.

✅ **Fact:** **Srikakulam**, the northernmost and among the least developed districts of Andhra Pradesh, had seen Communists organising **Jatapu and Savara** tribals into Girijan Sanghams and Mahila Sanghams from the early 1950s; between 1957-58 and 1967 the movement secured restoration of land illegally taken by non-tribal moneylenders and landlords, wage increases, better prices for forest produce, debt reduction and free access to forests for timber. There is no evidence of any push from within the tribal population towards militancy or violence.

✅ **Fact:** dissident leaders shifted to armed struggle from **November 1967**, reaching an intense mass phase between **November 1968 and February 1969**; from April 1969, with the decision to form CPI(ML), the emphasis moved from a mass line to guerrilla action and the annihilation of individual "class enemies". According to government sources about **forty-eight people** were annihilated, the rebels claiming roughly double; repression intensified from November 1969; by January thirteen leaders had been killed; by mid-June 1970 a massive police operation had arrested **1,400**; and on **10 July 1970** the two major leaders, V. Satyanarayana and Adibhatla Kailasam, were killed, ending the movement.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the source's verdict on both episodes is severe and specific: a leadership that had achieved real gains through mass organisation abandoned that method for an armed strategy the peasants had not demanded, against a modern state, with bows and arrows. It also notes the sequence - Srikakulam's mass phase began well after Naxalbari had been suppressed, and its tribal participants had no way of knowing what had happened there.

### 4. The "new" farmers' movements of the 1980s

✅ **Fact:** the farmers' movements burst onto the national stage in **1980** with the road and rail *roko* agitation at **Nasik** in Maharashtra led by **Sharad Joshi's Shetkari Sangathana**: **two lakh farmers** blockaded the Bombay-Calcutta and Bombay-Delhi routes on **10 November**, demanding higher prices for onions and sugarcane; thousands were arrested, two were killed in police firing, and prices were enhanced. Joshi, a former United Nations official, articulated the movement's ideology as **India versus Bharat** - urban industrial India against rural agricultural Bharat.

✅ **Fact:** in **1986**, at **Sisauli** village in Muzaffarnagar district of U.P., **Mahendra Singh Tikait** - a middle-school-educated, medium-size peasant, Jat by caste, and head of the Jat *khap* panchayat - presided over a gathering of lakhs before which the chief minister of U.P. appeared in person to announce acceptance of the demand for reduction of electricity charges to the old level.

✅ **Fact:** the movement was wider than these two moments, led by the Vivasayigal Sangam in Tamil Nadu, the Rajya Ryothu Sangha in Karnataka, the Bharatiya Kisan Union in Punjab and U.P., the Khedut Samaj and Kisan Sangh in Gujarat and the Shetkari Sangathana in Maharashtra. Methods included blocking highways and rail routes, withholding supplies from cities, indefinite *dharnas*, *gheraos* of officials, preventing political leaders from entering villages at election time, refusing to pay enhanced electricity charges, interest and irrigation costs, resisting confiscation proceedings and de-grabbing confiscated goods and land.

✅ **Fact:** the underlying claim was that the government keeps agricultural prices artificially low to supply cheap food and raw materials to urban areas, so that farmers pay high prices for industrial inputs and receive low returns - making them victims of "internal colonialism" and, on their own account, net creditors who need not repay loans or infrastructure charges.

✅ **Fact:** the source's assessment records both the criticism and the defence. The movements are charged with being vehicles for the demands of rich or well-to-do agriculturists who were beneficiaries of post-independence agrarian development including the Green Revolution, with little room for the rural poor. The leaders point to a diverse social base among medium and small peasants and to demands such as higher minimum wages for agricultural labour and the inclusion of women's and Dalits' issues by the Shetkari Sangathana. The source's own finding is that apart from the Shetkari Sangathana no other organisation went beyond landowning peasants' issues, and that these organisations showed scant concern for the landless rural poor or rural women - while conceding that they were genuinely broad-based among the peasantry, since smaller cultivators also sell in the market and pay government dues.

✅ **Fact:** the "newness" claim is examined and largely rejected. Similar demands had been made earlier without the rural-versus-urban ideology - a large movement was launched by the Kisan Sabha in Punjab against a betterment levy in **1958**, and the emergence of Charan Singh and the BKD in U.P. in 1967 was widely regarded as the coming of age of a landowning peasantry that had benefited from agrarian change. Nor did the organisations remain outside party politics: the Tamil Nadu organisation became a party; the Karnataka Ryothu Sangha contested elections; Sharad Joshi's Shetkari Sangathana put out a list of forty-eight candidates in the **1984** Lok Sabha elections and openly allied with V.P. Singh from 1987, receiving a Cabinet-level post to formulate a new agricultural policy in 1989; Tikait's support to the BJP became apparent in the wake of the Ayodhya mobilisation in 1990; and the Gujarat Kisan Sangh's links to the BJP were well known.

✅ **Fact:** unity failed visibly. On **2 October 1989** at the Boat Club lawns, Tikait and his men almost dragged Joshi and other leaders off the stage after making them wait two hours before a crowd of lakhs assembled for what was to be a joint rally. Ideologically the movement divided too: Joshi came to favour liberalisation and linking the farmer to the world market; the Karnataka organisation opposed multinationals. The Tamil Nadu movement was destroyed by state repression in **1981** after refusal to repay loans led to forcible confiscation, and its leader Naidu died disappointed in 1984 despite founding the Toilers and Peasants Party in 1982.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the source's criticism of the ideology is analytical rather than moral, and it is worth reproducing: the demands often had justice in them, but the Bharat-versus-India frame is flawed because it pits villagers against town-dwellers in a fratricidal contest instead of focusing on redress - and it leads to positions such as the 1984 proposal that Punjab farmers reduce foodgrain production to hold the country to ransom, mercifully never implemented.

### Comparative treatment: the two families, side by side

| Dimension | Telangana, PEPSU, Naxalbari, Srikakulam | Nasik, Sisauli and the 1980s movements |
|---|---|---|
| Social base | Landless labour, tenants, sharecroppers, tribals | Owner-cultivators, including medium and small, producing for the market |
| Adversary | Landlord, jagirdar, deshmukh, biswedar, jotedar, moneylender | The state, through prices, charges and credit terms |
| Core demand | Land, security of tenure, abolition of forced labour and illegal exactions | Remunerative output prices, cheap inputs, cheap or free power, debt relief |
| Method | Organisation, occupation, and in some phases armed action | Mass blockade, non-payment, electoral bargaining |
| Outcome | Legislation: 1949-53 statutes in Hyderabad and PEPSU; suppression elsewhere | Concessions that were often fiscally unsustainable |
| Relation to the Green Revolution | Predates or coincides with its early phase | Direct product of it |

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Describing the 1980s movements as movements of the privileged ignores the genuine squeeze on cultivators from rising input costs and stagnant procurement prices.

**Reply:** The squeeze was real, and the source says the demands often had justice in them; it also notes that smaller cultivators shared the interest because they too sell and pay dues, which is why the base was broad. What the evidence does not support is the claim that these were movements *of the rural poor*: the landless agricultural labourer's interest in higher food prices is the opposite of the surplus producer's, and only one organisation of the set took up agricultural wages seriously.

**Residual:** Whether a single agrarian platform could ever have united surplus producers and the landless is genuinely doubtful, and the failure of the 2 October 1989 joint rally is the best evidence that leaders on the ground believed it could not.

### UPSC application

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** left-wing extremism questions route to the Internal Security owner, which handles the contemporary response architecture. What this lesson owns is the origin and the sociology: that Naxalbari's mass phase lasted months, that Srikakulam's movement had achieved substantial gains by non-violent organisation before the shift to annihilation, and that the affected populations had not demanded armed struggle. That is the historical half, and it is the half that explains why development and organisation have always figured in the response alongside security.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Merging the two families of agrarian movement; they represent opposed rural interests.
- Dating Naxalbari to the 1970s; it was 1967, and CPI(ML) was formed in 1969.
- Describing Telangana as a movement against the Indian state throughout; its decisive gains came in the anti-Nizam phase, and the armed phase against the Indian Army followed the Nizam's surrender of September 1948.
- Inflating the PEPSU armed force; it never exceeded 30 to 40 people, the largest estimate being 100.
- Calling the 1980s movements "new social movements"; similar demands predate them, and they became entangled with party politics.
- Forgetting the Nasik date of 10 November 1980 and the Sisauli year of 1986.

### Revision notes

1. Telangana: against jagirdars and deshmukhs, forced levy and *veth begar*; decisive phase August 1947 to September 1948; claims of control over 3,000 villages; ceilings declared at 500 then 100 acres.
2. Peasants prioritised ancestral and waste land over landlords' surplus - and kept the first two after the movement declined.
3. Indian Army entered Hyderabad on 13 September 1948; the armed phase against it ended by about 1951 with perhaps 500 dead and about 10,000 jailed.
4. State response: Jagirdari Abolition Regulation 1949; Hyderabad Tenancy and Agricultural Lands Act 1950; over 600,000 protected tenants over a quarter of cultivated area.
5. PEPSU: biswedars over roughly 800 villages, one-sixth of state area; Lal Communist Party 1948; armed force of 30-40; Kishangarh January 1949.
6. PEPSU Tenancy (Temporary Provision) Act January 1952; PEPSU Occupancy Tenants (Vesting of Proprietary Rights) Act 1953 at twelve times land revenue.
7. Naxalbari: United Front sworn in 2 March 1967; peasants' conference sixteen days later; *adhiar* sharecropping under jotedars; suppressed by July 1967; CPI(ML) formed 1969.
8. Srikakulam: Jatapu and Savara tribals; Girijan Sanghams from the 1950s with real gains; armed phase from November 1967, intense November 1968 to February 1969; annihilation line from April 1969; about 48 annihilated by government count; 1,400 arrested by mid-June 1970; leaders killed 10 July 1970.
9. Land-grab movement of 1969-70 across nine states, about 20,000 arrests; Bharatiya Khet Mazdoor Union founded by CPI at Moga in 1968.
10. Nasik, 10 November 1980: two lakh farmers, onion and sugarcane prices, two killed, prices raised; Sisauli 1986: electricity charges rolled back before a mass gathering.
11. Ideology: India versus Bharat, internal colonialism, farmers as net creditors.
12. Assessment: broad-based among the peasantry but confined to landowners' issues except the Shetkari Sangathana; not genuinely "new"; entangled with parties; Boat Club rally failure on 2 October 1989; Tamil Nadu movement destroyed by repression in 1981.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain what the change in agrarian demands between 1946 and 1986 proves about the effects of post-independence agrarian policy, and name the specific error an answer must avoid.

**Model answer:** It proves that policy changed rural class structure rather than rural equality. In the 1940s the demands were land, security of tenure and abolition of forced labour and illegal exactions - Telangana against the forced grain levy and *veth begar*, PEPSU against biswedars claiming proprietorship over roughly 800 villages. In 1967 the demands were still about land and the sharecropper's share, at Naxalbari under the *adhiar* system and at Srikakulam over land restored to tribals. By 1980 the demands were prices and input costs: two lakh farmers blocked highways at Nasik on 10 November 1980 for onion and sugarcane prices, and in 1986 Sisauli forced a rollback of electricity charges. The adversary had changed from the landlord to the state, and the class making the demand had changed from the landless and the tenant to the surplus-producing owner-cultivator created by abolition and consolidated by the Green Revolution. The error to avoid is treating these as one continuous "peasant movement": the landless labourer's interest in cheap food is directly opposed to the surplus producer's interest in high output prices, which is why the attempt at a joint rally on 2 October 1989 collapsed in public.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that continued agrarian agitation proves continued agrarian exploitation. The later agitation was conducted largely by beneficiaries of earlier policy, demanding better terms within the system rather than its overthrow.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

Trace the changing character of agrarian movements in India since independence. (15 marks, 250 words)

#### Model answer

Agrarian movements since 1947 divide into two families whose class content is opposed.

The first family attacked pre-capitalist domination. In Telangana, peasants organised from 1946 against jagirdars and deshmukhs, the forced grain levy and *veth begar*; control was claimed over some 3,000 villages, ceilings were declared at 500 and then 100 acres, and after the Army entered Hyderabad on 13 September 1948 the armed phase against the Indian state cost perhaps 500 lives and 10,000 arrests before withdrawal in 1951. The state answered with the Jagirdari Abolition Regulation of 1949 and the Hyderabad Tenancy Act of 1950, protecting over 600,000 tenants. In PEPSU, muzaras of roughly 800 villages won proprietary rights in 1953 on payment of twelve times the land revenue.

A second wave in 1967 targeted the same structures: Naxalbari's sharecroppers under the *adhiar* system, suppressed within months, and Srikakulam's Jatapu and Savara tribals, whose movement ended with the killing of its leaders on 10 July 1970. CPI(ML) was formed in 1969.

The second family reversed the class content. From Nasik on 10 November 1980 and Sisauli in 1986, surplus-producing farmers demanded remunerative prices, cheaper power and debt relief, articulating an India-versus-Bharat ideology, and negotiated directly with governments and parties.

The trajectory therefore records the success of abolition and of the Green Revolution in creating a landowning peasantry, and their failure to transform the position of the landless - who remained largely outside both families of movement.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the two-family structure stated as the organising claim 4 + three first-family movements with dates and outcomes 5 + the 1980s movements with dates and demands 3 + the class-content reversal explained causally 2 + the landless-remain-outside conclusion 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 13:** The rural class structure has now been traced. The social hierarchy that cut across it - caste - was attacked by a different instrument, the law, and with a different pattern of success and failure.

## Lesson 13 - Caste after 1947: abolition, reservation, assertion and Mandal

Progress: 13/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Article 17 and the statutes, conversion and its limits, Dalit political organisation, the invisible causes of change, and why Backward Classes are a different question

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the survey history's chapter on caste, untouchability and anti-caste politics, read in full, with the canonical basic file's caste bank and the advanced file's criticism of the Mandal data base
CA search: "Scheduled Caste sub-classification creamy layer Supreme Court states implementation 2026 news"
CA found: Reported - following the 2024 decision permitting sub-classification within Scheduled Castes, the Union government told the Supreme Court in August 2026 that the creamy-layer principle developed for Other Backward Classes cannot be transplanted to Scheduled Castes and Tribes, whose protection rests on historical and structural discrimination, and that such a change is for Parliament; no state had implemented an income-based exclusion for Scheduled Castes as of September 2026
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four routes attempted, and what each actually did

```text
 ROUTE 1  LAW                Article 17 abolishes untouchability
                             Untouchability (Offences) Act, 1955
                             Protection of Civil Rights (Amendment) Act, 1976
                             -> removed legal sanction; enforcement remained the problem

 ROUTE 2  RESERVATION        seats in legislatures, educational institutions, government jobs
                             initially for ten years, extended continuously
                             -> real but arithmetically limited: only about 3 per cent of the
                                population reached higher education and government jobs at all

 ROUTE 3  CONVERSION         Ambedkar and about half a million (some say six million) Mahars
                             became Buddhists in 1956
                             -> transferred the problem; village converts added Ambedkar and
                                Buddha to an existing pantheon; Christian and Muslim Dalits
                                faced the same discrimination inside those communities

 ROUTE 4  ASSERTION          Republican Party 1957 -> Dalit Panthers 1972 -> BSP in the 1980s
                             -> changed politics; splits and absorption recurred

 WHAT ACTUALLY MOVED THE POSITION MOST: the invisible processes
    industrialisation, agricultural growth and rural employment, urbanisation,
    primary education and health, anti-poverty and employment guarantee schemes,
    HOUSE SITES in villages, adult franchise, the breakdown of the jajmani system
```

*The visible strategies - conversion and reservation - are the ones students name. The source's judgement is that the invisible ones did more.*

### 1. What the caste order actually did, and how it began to crack

✅ **Fact:** the aspect at issue is hierarchy - high and low, touchable and untouchable - which legitimised unequal access to resources, exploitation and discrimination. The most obnoxious element designated certain groups untouchable and outcaste and used that to deny them ownership of land, entry into temples and access to common resources such as village tanks and wells; no physical contact or acceptance of food or water was permitted. In villages untouchable castes performed the menial work - scavenging, water-carrying, skinning dead animals, leather work, agricultural labour - receiving a fixed share of produce from landowning families under the **jajmani** system.

✅ **Fact:** breaches appeared from the middle and late nineteenth century: commercialisation of agricultural production and agrarian relations, contractual relations, and new employment outside the village in factories, *mandis*, government service and the army, aided by education. Social reform movements such as those of **Jyotiba Phule** in Maharashtra and **Sri Narayana Guru** in Kerala questioned caste inequality. From 1920 Gandhi integrated the abolition of untouchability into the national movement, with campaigns such as the **Vaikom satyagraha of 1924-25** and the **Guruvayur satyagraha of 1931-32**; his effort was to make upper castes recognise the enormity of the injustice and atone for it, and he opposed separate electorates for the Depressed Classes in the Communal Award of 1932 on the ground that separating them from Hindus would remove any pressure on Hindu society to change its attitude.

✅ **Fact:** **B.R. Ambedkar**, a Mahar educated in the United States with a scholarship from the Maharaja of Baroda, emerged as the major leader of the Depressed Classes by the late 1920s. After Gandhi's fast against the Communal Award he agreed to the **Poona Pact** of 1932, under which the Depressed Classes received reserved seats from within the general Hindu category. By 1936 he argued that conversion was necessary and even chose Sikhism, deferring it when the British government would not promise that reservation benefits would continue after conversion. He formed the **Independent Labour Party** in 1936, seeking to combine with peasants and workers, and won a few seats in the 1937 Bombay elections; by the early 1940s, finding that an alliance against the Congress was not advancing, he focused on Scheduled Castes alone and formed the **Scheduled Castes Federation in 1942**, cooperating politically with the colonial government and gaining a seat on the Viceroy's Executive Council.

✅ **Fact:** regional strands developed too - **Ad-Dharm** in Punjab, **Adi-Hindu** in U.P. and the **Namashudras** in Bengal, both the first two allying with pro-British Unionist and Krishak Praja parties respectively; in Bihar **Jagjivan Ram** formed the Khetmajoor Sabha and the Depressed Classes League. The main pre-independence demands were freedom from *begar*, grants of forest or waste land for cultivation, and removal of legal disabilities on owning land, such as those imposed by the Punjab Land Alienation Act of 1900, which did not include Scheduled Castes among agriculturist castes.

### 2. The constitutional and statutory attack

✅ **Fact:** the Constitution extended political rights to all citizens irrespective of religion, caste, sex, language or race, and **Article 17** declared that "untouchability" is abolished and its practice in any form forbidden, with enforcement of any resulting disability an offence punishable by law. Parliament passed the **Untouchability (Offences) Act in 1955**, specifying punishment by fine and cancellation of licences and public grants, and the **Protection of Civil Rights (Amendment) Act in 1976**, providing enhanced and stringent punishment, officers and special courts, and legal aid for victims. The Constitution also reserved seats in legislatures and educational institutions and government jobs for Scheduled Castes, initially for ten years and extended continuously since.

✅ **Fact:** Ambedkar was a party to these initiatives: despite pre-independence differences he was chosen by the Congress as **chairman of the Drafting Committee** and was **law minister** in Nehru's Cabinet. Differences later emerged and he left the government to form the All India Scheduled Castes Federation, whose candidates mostly lost to Congress candidates in reserved seats.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two features of this design explain much of what follows. Reservation was attached to a legal category rather than to a social experience, so its benefits could be captured by the better-placed within the category. And because political reservation operated within the general electorate rather than through separate electorates, representatives of Scheduled Caste origin depended on the votes of all communities - which made co-option easier and radical assertion harder.

### 3. Conversion, and its limits

✅ **Fact:** in **1956** Ambedkar returned to his position that conversion was necessary and led **half a million people, some say six million**, mainly Mahars, into Buddhism; the source suggests he could do this because reservations were not denied to Buddhist converts as they were to Scheduled Castes converting to Christianity or Islam. Some other groups, such as the Jatavs of Agra, followed; many did not. Ambedkar died soon after, in 1956, leaving no second line of leadership; the **Republican Party** was founded in 1957 on the basis of a letter published posthumously, won a few Bombay seats, and then split, with most factions joining or allying with the Congress, which under Y.B. Chavan made special efforts to accommodate them.

✅ **Fact:** sociologists have found that Buddhist converts in villages did not give up their old Hindu gods and goddesses but added photographs of Ambedkar and Buddha to the pantheon, and expressed their new confidence by celebrating Hindu festivals earlier barred to them, such as Gauri and Ganapathi puja, with great public display, and by cooking prohibited religious foods. Upper castes were angered not by the conversion, which they could accommodate, but precisely by this defiance of traditional norms and emulation of Hindu practice. The fate of converts to Christianity - separate Dalit churches or separate places within churches, discrimination including denial of promotion in the church hierarchy and refusal by priests to accept water from their hands - and of low-caste Muslim converts treated similarly by high-caste Ashrafs, shows that conversion "has only transferred the problem of caste-based discrimination".

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the source draws a conclusion that is historiographically pointed and should be reported as such: since Dalits felt equality with caste Hindus only when able to practise the rites and customs previously denied them, "Gandhiji's understanding and strategy of struggle against the Dalit problem, which emphasized gaining religious equality via temple entry, stands validated". That is a contested claim and belongs in an answer as an attributed judgement rather than as a neutral fact.

### 4. Assertion: Panthers, BSP and the limits of both

✅ **Fact:** in the early 1970s the **Dalit Panthers** emerged in Maharashtra as part of a countrywide wave of radical politics, first in creative literature and then in politics, established as a political organisation in **1972**, leaning ideologically on Ambedkar's thought with a base among urban youth and students. They talked of revolution without evidence of a concrete strategy. The agitation to rename Marathwada University after Ambedkar produced **anti-Dalit riots in rural Maharashtra in 1978** in which the main aggressors were middle-caste Maratha-Kunbi non-Brahmin peasants. By the 1980s the Panthers had split over whether to include the non-Dalit poor and non-Buddhist Dalits, over cultural versus economic struggle, and over personalities - Raja Dhale against Namdeo Dhasal - with most factions joining or allying with the Congress, as the Republican Party had twenty years earlier. **Prakash Ambedkar** attempted to unite Dalit organisations in 1990 for the Maharashtra assembly elections, organising a *morcha* of 500,000 in Bombay, before differences resurfaced.

✅ **Fact:** in north India the **Bahujan Samaj Party** emerged in the 1980s under **Kanshi Ram**, later **Mayawati**, declaring electoral power as its basic aim and strategy. Though it initially spoke of Dalits, Backward Castes and minorities coming together as a *bahujan samaj*, in practice it became a Dalit-based party willing to ally with any force - BJP, Congress, Janata, Samajwadi Party - to advance its vote share and gain power; such a deal with the BJP made Mayawati chief minister of U.P. in 1995, and the party dropped V.P. Singh to support Devi Lal and Chandra Shekhar in 1990. Its ideology was marked by a strident and often abusive stance towards upper castes, which proximity to power began to mellow.

✅ **Fact:** non-Dalit parties, groups and non-governmental organisations also contributed, through agricultural-labour unions taking up wages, employment guarantee, right to work, house sites, abolition of child labour and the right to education. Exclusively Dalit organisations multiplied - Ambedkar Sanghams among rural youth, associations of students, teachers, youth and office workers in urban areas, though the latter were more concerned with members' interests and had little link with rural areas or the urban poor.

✅ **Fact:** the source records the uncomfortable fact that despite all this, "the majority of Dalits still vote for the Congress", and argues that this ground reality - rather than theories of co-option or betrayal - explains why Dalit leaders repeatedly moved towards the Congress.

### 5. Why reservation could only do so much, and what really moved

✅ **Fact:** the arithmetic limit is explicit. Given that only about **3 per cent** of the total population received higher education and could access government jobs, the percentage of Scheduled Castes who could possibly benefit from reservation at that level was much smaller, since they were poorer and more rural. Reservation of legislative seats had some effect, as electoral imperatives forced representatives to take up constituents' issues, but the tendency to co-option and personal aggrandisement among representatives of Scheduled Caste origin was no lower than among others.

✅ **Fact:** a further problem emerged - **competition between Scheduled Castes**, such as Mahars and Mangs in Maharashtra, Malas and Madigas in Andhra Pradesh and Chamars and Chuhras in north India. Because the benefits of reservation are inevitably availed by the better-off castes within the category, the disadvantaged began demanding quotas within quotas, and intra-Scheduled Caste hostility became increasingly visible. The source states the logic: once reservation is secured, the only way to improve prospects further is to seek a larger slice of the apportioned cake.

✅ **Fact:** the overall position nevertheless improved considerably, and the source attributes this mainly to processes other than conversion or reservation: industrialisation; agricultural growth raising rural employment; urbanisation; extension of primary education and health facilities; anti-poverty programmes, rural employment guarantee schemes and income-generating schemes such as subsidies and loans for dairying and goat-rearing; literacy campaigns; the campaign against child labour; the **provision of house sites in villages, begun by Indira Gandhi**, which removed a major instrument of coercion by ending the threat of eviction from village land; adult franchise, which made the vote of the poorest and lowest-caste valuable; land distribution where it occurred, removing the stigma of landlessness; and the breakdown of the jajmani system with the delinking of caste from traditional occupation.

✅ **Fact:** the measured result: untouchability in urban areas **virtually disappeared** and in rural areas **declined drastically**, decreasing sharply in prosperous rural areas where employment opportunities for low castes expanded, since employers who must seek out labour cannot flaunt caste status; caste-based discrimination in factories and offices is rare, though prejudices linger.

✅ **Fact:** the source's reading of atrocities is analytically important: they continue, but are usually a reaction to open defiance of upper-caste norms - a lower-caste boy eloping with an upper-caste girl, or lower castes allying with extremist political groups as in Bihar - and so, while condemnable in the strongest terms, are "to be understood as proof of increasing assertion by lower castes".

✅ **Fact:** great inequalities remain in access to education, employment and other opportunities, and the link between caste and literacy is strong. In **1991**, all-India literacy was **64 per cent for men and 39 per cent for women**, but **46 per cent for Scheduled Caste men and only 19 per cent for Scheduled Caste women**; in U.P. the comparable figures were 56/25 and 39/8; in Kerala the gap was much narrower at 94/86 against 85/73. Even the benefits of reservation cannot be used without education, as the general inability to fill reserved quotas at every level shows.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Kerala-U.P. contrast is the most economical evidence in this lesson that outcomes for Scheduled Castes are determined more by universal provision - schooling and health - than by category-specific measures. That is the argument to make when a question asks what strategies work.

### 6. Backward Classes: a different question

✅ **Fact:** the issue of the Backward Classes, which came to a head with the **Mandal report and the anti-Mandal agitation in 1990**, is "quite different from that of the SCs, though efforts are made at the political level to equate or collapse the two". The so-called Backward Castes are intermediate castes, ritually below Brahmins and Kshatriyas and above the untouchables; they suffered certain ritual disabilities but were in no way comparable to Scheduled Castes, since they often had access to land and other economic resources and did not suffer untouchability. The category also contains great internal disparity, with some castes or sections very powerful economically and socially and others quite disadvantaged.

✅ **Fact:** sociologists have shown that Backward Castes such as Ahirs, Yadavas, Kurmis, Vokkaligas, Lingayats and Lodhas gained considerable economic advantage from **post-independence land reform, which gave land rights to ex-tenants of zamindars**; this new strength increased their political clout and representation, and they sought to use it for greater advantage in jobs and education. In rural areas they are "the biggest exploiters of the SCs who are agricultural labourers", so there is little in common between the two categories.

✅ **Fact:** the Mandal report "has been shown by scholars to be based on faulty methodology and a weak data base", and the Mandal judgements have been criticised by sociologists for working on out-dated and ill-informed western notions of caste despite drastic post-independence change. The source's judgement on the politics is blunt: the politics of reservation for Backward Castes "has more to do with sharing the loaves and fishes of office and power than with a struggle for social justice".

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two boundary rules follow. First, the political narrative of Mandal - the 1990 implementation, the protest, the judicial stay and the electoral consequences - is taught in the preceding session on the Rajiv years and the run-up to the millennium, and must not be re-narrated here. Second, the source's criticism of Mandal is a **historiographical position**, not a neutral fact, and should be attributed when used. Contemporary reservation doctrine, sub-classification and creamy-layer questions belong to the Polity and Social Justice owners.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** the caste record after 1947 is one of near-complete legal victory, a limited reservation effect, a transferred rather than solved problem through conversion, a transformed politics through assertion, and a substantial improvement driven mainly by universal processes of education, employment, urbanisation and the vote.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Crediting "invisible processes" over reservation understates the transformative role of quotas in creating a Dalit middle class and a political leadership.

**Reply:** The source does not deny that effect; it locates its scale. Legislative reservation forced representatives to take up constituents' issues, and educational and job reservation created a stratum that produced organisations, literature and leadership. The limit is arithmetic, not ideological: when only about 3 per cent of the population reached higher education and public employment at all, a proportional share of that 3 per cent cannot transform the position of a fifth of the population. The two propositions are consistent - reservation mattered most where the population was small, while mass change required mass instruments.

**Residual:** Whether the invisible processes would have operated at the same speed without reservation and legal abolition cannot be tested, since they occurred together and in the same polity.

### UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** the verified 2026 General Studies Paper I ledger records a 15-mark demand, "Is caste disappearing in urban India? Illustrate your answer with examples", routed to the Indian Society owner.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the historical supply for that answer is exactly the material above: the documented near-disappearance of untouchability in urban areas and its sharp decline in prosperous rural areas; the employment mechanism - employers who must seek labour cannot flaunt status; and the persistence of caste in literacy, in intra-category competition and in political mobilisation. The contemporary evidence and the verdict belong to the Indian Society file; the causal history belongs here. Similar cross-owned demands recur regularly - caste assuming new identities and associational forms, caste as both fluid and static, the relevance of caste in a multi-cultural society - and all of them are strengthened by the post-1947 trajectory rather than by contemporary assertion alone.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Equating Scheduled Castes with Backward Classes; the source treats them as different questions with different histories.
- Presenting reservation as the main cause of improvement; the source attributes more to education, employment, urbanisation, house sites and the vote.
- Treating conversion as a solution; discrimination reproduced itself inside Christianity and Islam and among village Buddhist converts.
- Forgetting that Ambedkar chaired the Drafting Committee and was law minister, and that the Poona Pact of 1932 produced reservation within the general electorate.
- Missing the intra-Scheduled Caste competition that produced demands for quotas within quotas.
- Quoting the 1991 literacy figures without the Scheduled Caste split, which is where the argument lies.
- Re-narrating the 1990 Mandal politics, which the preceding session owns.

### Revision notes

1. The caste order's operative element was hierarchy legitimising unequal access, with untouchability denying land, temple entry and common resources; jajmani fixed shares for menial service.
2. Nineteenth-century cracks: commercialisation, contractual relations, employment outside the village, education; Phule and Narayana Guru.
3. Gandhi integrated untouchability abolition from 1920; Vaikom 1924-25; Guruvayur 1931-32; opposition to separate electorates in 1932; Poona Pact.
4. Ambedkar: Mahar; Independent Labour Party 1936; Scheduled Castes Federation 1942; Viceroy's Executive Council; Drafting Committee chairman; law minister; conversion 1956 with half a million, some say six million; died 1956.
5. Regional strands: Ad-Dharm in Punjab, Adi-Hindu in U.P., Namashudras in Bengal, Jagjivan Ram's Khetmajoor Sabha and Depressed Classes League in Bihar.
6. Article 17; Untouchability (Offences) Act 1955; Protection of Civil Rights (Amendment) Act 1976; reservation initially for ten years, extended continuously.
7. Republican Party 1957; Dalit Panthers 1972; Marathwada renaming agitation and the 1978 anti-Dalit riots with Maratha-Kunbi aggressors; splits by the 1980s.
8. BSP from the 1980s under Kanshi Ram and Mayawati; alliance-neutral strategy; Mayawati chief minister in 1995; BSP supported Devi Lal and Chandra Shekhar in 1990.
9. Reservation's arithmetic limit: only about 3 per cent of the population reached higher education and government jobs at all.
10. Intra-Scheduled Caste competition: Mahar and Mang, Mala and Madiga, Chamar and Chuhra; demands for quotas within quotas.
11. Main causes of improvement: industrialisation, agricultural growth, urbanisation, primary education and health, anti-poverty and employment schemes, house sites begun by Indira Gandhi, adult franchise, land distribution, breakdown of jajmani.
12. Untouchability virtually gone in urban areas and drastically reduced in rural ones; atrocities read as reactions to assertion.
13. 1991 literacy: all-India 64 per cent men and 39 per cent women; Scheduled Caste men 46 and women 19; U.P. 56/25 and 39/8; Kerala 94/86 and 85/73.
14. Backward Classes are intermediate castes with land access and no untouchability; Ahirs, Yadavas, Kurmis, Vokkaligas, Lingayats and Lodhas gained from land reform.
15. The Mandal report is criticised for faulty methodology and a weak data base, and the judgements for out-dated notions of caste; this is an attributed position, not a neutral fact.

### Concept check

**Question:** The source argues that the improvement in the position of Scheduled Castes owes more to "invisible" processes than to the two visible strategies of conversion and reservation. Reconstruct the argument with evidence, and state one limit of it.

**Model answer:** Conversion transferred the problem rather than solving it: about half a million, by some accounts six million, mainly Mahars converted to Buddhism with Ambedkar in 1956, but village converts added Ambedkar and Buddha to an existing pantheon and asserted equality by performing previously forbidden Hindu rites, while Christian converts faced separate churches and denial of promotion and low-caste Muslims were treated similarly by high-caste Ashrafs. Reservation was limited arithmetically, since only about 3 per cent of the population reached higher education and public employment at all, so a share of that could not transform a mass position; it also generated intra-category competition between Mahars and Mangs, Malas and Madigas, Chamars and Chuhras, and demands for quotas within quotas. The invisible processes reached everyone: industrialisation, agricultural growth and rural employment, urbanisation, primary education and health, anti-poverty and employment schemes, literacy campaigns, land distribution where it occurred, adult franchise, the breakdown of the jajmani system, and the provision of house sites in villages begun by Indira Gandhi, which removed the threat of eviction as an instrument of coercion. The measured outcome is that untouchability virtually disappeared in urban areas and declined sharply in prosperous rural ones, where employers seeking labour could not flaunt status. The limit of the argument is that these processes were not independent of the legal and reserved order: abolition removed legal sanction, and reservation produced the leadership and organisations that pressed for the universal provisions.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that the most discussed instrument is the most effective one. Conversion and reservation dominate the literature because they are visible and contested; the evidence points to slower, universal processes as the larger cause of change.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

Trace the transformation of caste relations in India since independence. (15 marks, 250 words)

#### Model answer

Caste after 1947 changed in law immediately, in politics substantially and in social practice slowly and unevenly.

Legally, Article 17 abolished untouchability, the Untouchability (Offences) Act of 1955 attached penalties and the Protection of Civil Rights (Amendment) Act of 1976 added special courts, officers and legal aid. Reservation in legislatures, education and employment, initially for ten years, was extended continuously.

Politically the trajectory ran from Ambedkar - Drafting Committee chairman and law minister, who led about half a million Mahars into Buddhism in 1956 - through the Republican Party of 1957 and the Dalit Panthers of 1972 to the Bahujan Samaj Party of the 1980s, which made electoral power its explicit strategy and took office in Uttar Pradesh in 1995. Assertion also provoked reaction, as the 1978 anti-Dalit riots in rural Maharashtra showed.

Socially, change came mainly from processes not designed for caste at all: industrialisation and urbanisation, agricultural growth and rural employment, primary education and health, anti-poverty and employment schemes, house sites in villages, adult franchise and the breakdown of the jajmani system. Untouchability virtually disappeared in urban areas and declined sharply where rural employment expanded.

Inequality nonetheless persisted where education did. In 1991 literacy was 64 per cent for men and 39 for women nationally, but 46 and 19 for Scheduled Castes, and 39 and 8 in Uttar Pradesh against 85 and 73 in Kerala.

Caste thus lost legal sanction and much ritual force while retaining power as an organising principle of politics and opportunity.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = legal instruments dated 3 + the political trajectory with at least four named organisations or moments 4 + the invisible-processes argument stated as the main social cause 4 + the literacy evidence with the Kerala-U.P. contrast 2 + a verdict distinguishing legal, political and social change 2.**

**Bridge to Lesson 14:** The same pattern - law first, enforcement later, social change slowest - governs the other great hierarchy the Republic legislated against, and there the legislation itself was fiercely contested.

## Lesson 14 - Women after 1947: constitutional equality, the Hindu-law reform and the movement's phases

Progress: 14/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the four Acts, the rights not claimed, the movement from Tebhaga to the autonomous groups, and the health and education record

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the survey history's chapter on Indian women since independence, read in full for the Hindu Code sequence, the movement's phases and the state-level health and education comparisons
CA search: "Women's reservation Nari Shakti Vandan Adhiniyam implementation delimitation census 2026 news"
CA found: Reported - through 2026 the operation of the one-third reservation for women in the Lok Sabha and state assemblies remains tied to a delimitation exercise, with draft bills moved in April 2026 proposing to use 2011 Census data and an expanded House, and the reservation expected to operate from the 2029 general elections; the sequence repeats the pattern by which the local-bodies reservation proposed in 1988 was enacted only in 1993
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the gap between rights granted and rights used

```text
 GRANTED AT ONCE                         USED IMMEDIATELY AND FULLY
 the vote, without qualification of      YES - women are keen voters, acutely conscious
 education, property or income              of the power of the vote, especially rural women
      (won in one stroke what
       suffragettes fought decades for)

 GRANTED BY STATUTE, 1955-56             USED ONLY PARTLY
 Hindu Marriage Act, 1955                divorce increasingly used in urban areas,
 Hindu Succession Act, 1956                though stigma and the difficulty of living
 Hindu Minority and Guardianship Act,       as a single woman remained
   1956                                  INHERITANCE OF PARENTAL PROPERTY: largely NOT claimed
 Hindu Adoptions and Maintenance Act,       -> patrilocal residence is the main reason
   1956                                     -> dowry persists partly as the only share of
                                               parental property a daughter actually receives

 NOT GRANTED TO ALL COMMUNITIES
 the 1985-86 sequence showed the limit: mobilisation, not principle, decided the outcome
```

*The lesson's central analytical fact is that the largest unused right is a property right, and the reason is residence, not law.*

### 1. Why 1947 could move quickly

✅ **Fact:** the improvement of women's position had been at the heart of social reform since the first quarter of the nineteenth century, and the freedom struggle from the 1920s and especially the 1930s drew amply on women's participation. Gandhi's statement to Mridula Sarabhai in the mid-1930s - "I have brought the Indian women out of the kitchen, it is up to you (the women activists) to see that they don't go back" - is recorded as neither an empty boast nor a thoughtless exhortation. By treating women as political beings capable of nationalist feeling and of struggle and sacrifice, the national movement resolved many doctrinal debates about women's role in the public sphere: women who could march in processions, defy laws and go to jail unescorted could also aspire to jobs, the vote and inheritance. The image shifted from a recipient of justice in the nineteenth century, to a supporter of nationalist men in the early twentieth, to a comrade by the 1930s and 1940s. The **All India Women's Conference** was founded in **1926**.

✅ **Fact:** the Constitution promised complete equality, and women received the vote alongside men without any qualification of education, property or income - won at one stroke what suffragettes in many western countries had fought long and hard for.

### 2. The Hindu Code: the fight and the four Acts

✅ **Fact:** in the early 1950s Nehru initiated the enactment of the **Hindu Code Bill**, a measure demanded by women since the 1930s. A committee under **B.N. Rau**, the constitutional expert who prepared the first draft of the Constitution, had submitted a draft code in **1944**; another committee chaired by **B.R. Ambedkar**, law minister after independence, submitted a bill that raised the age of consent and marriage, upheld monogamy, gave women rights to divorce, maintenance and inheritance, and treated dowry as *stridhan*, women's property.

✅ **Fact:** strong opposition from conservative sections and hesitation among senior Congress leaders **including President Rajendra Prasad** led to postponement, despite strong support from a majority of Congressmen, women activists and social reformers. Sections of the bill were ultimately passed as **four separate Acts**: the **Hindu Marriage Act**, the **Hindu Succession Act**, the **Hindu Minority and Guardianship Act** and the **Hindu Adoptions and Maintenance Act**. The first is dated **1955** and the other three **1956**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the "four Acts, not one code" point is the single most reliable factual trap in this lesson and appears repeatedly in examination material. It is also analytically significant: fragmentation was the price of passage, and it meant that the coherent code Ambedkar's committee had drafted was never enacted as such.

✅ **Fact:** the source's comparative judgement on the limits of that reform is careful. The extension of legal rights to Hindu women "was not sufficient but it was a big step forward", as is shown by the stiff opposition encountered in attempts to extend legal rights to other religious communities. In **1985**, about forty years after Hindu law was reformed, the Supreme Court granted maintenance to a divorced Muslim woman in the Shah Bano case; there was a furore among conservative sections and sufficient pressure on the Rajiv Gandhi government for it to introduce a bill negating the judgement. The source's formulation of the asymmetry is exact: "while the Opposition brought lakhs into the streets, the supporters of Shah Bano could muster only hundreds", and while Nehru faced opposition he could also muster considerable support, because among Hindus the process of social reform had gone much further.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the political narrative of the 1986 reversal belongs to the preceding session. What belongs here is the structural point: the enforceability of a reform depends on the depth of prior social reform in the community concerned, and the thirty-year interval between the two episodes is the evidence.

### 3. The rights not claimed

✅ **Fact:** the right to vote was taken very seriously, and women are keen voters acutely conscious of the power of the vote, particularly rural women. Other rights remained on paper. With regard to inheritance of parental property, legal rights are by and large **not claimed**: it remains common in most parts of the country, rural and urban, for women to forgo their rights, and the custom of **patrilocal residence** is very largely responsible. This is also one reason women have refused to give up dowry, because it is their only chance of getting a share of parental property. The legal right to divorce has been increasingly used in urban areas, though stigma persists and the difficulties of setting up as a single woman remain immense.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the clearest instance in the whole topic of the gap between a right and its use, and the cause is neither ignorance nor legal defect. A woman who will live in her husband's village cannot easily cultivate or manage a share of land in her father's, and claiming it may cost her the relationship that is her insurance. The reform's failure is therefore located in residence patterns, which no statute addressed.

### 4. The movement's phases

**Phase one - consolidation, 1947 to the late 1960s.**

✅ **Fact:** with different political forces going their own ways after independence, the women's movement diversified. Many leaders became involved in government-initiated and institutional welfare activity, including rehabilitation and recovery of women lost or abandoned in the Partition migration and riots, working women's hostels and vocational centres. In **1954** Communist women left the All India Women's Conference to form the **National Federation of Indian Women**, a party forum rather than a broad platform. The relative absence of visible struggles in the 1950s and 1960s produced the view that there was no women's movement until the 1970s, which the source rejects as a failure to recognise that phases of consolidation and quiet constructive work are integral parts of a movement.

✅ **Fact:** women also raised their issues inside other movements. In the **Tebhaga** movement in Bengal in 1946-47 they organised separately as the **Nari Bahini**, running shelters and maintaining communications, while Communist women mobilised rural women on rights to finance and property and village-level **Mahila Atma Raksha Samitis** took up domestic violence. In **Telangana** between 1946 and 1950 women's participation was significant and the leadership addressed wife-beating, though no women's organisations emerged; women were discouraged from joining the guerrilla force and felt incompletely accepted when they did, and Communist women elsewhere complained of being encouraged to marry comrades and edged into the "women's front" rather than integrated into leadership.

**Phase two - mass participation and new issues, 1970s to mid-1980s.**

| Movement | Date and place | What it established |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Anti-price-rise movement | 1973-75, urban Maharashtra, organised by Communist and Socialist women | Thousands of housewives in public rallies; those who could not leave home joined by beating *thalis* with rolling pins; spread to Gujarat into the Nav Nirman movement |
| ✅ SEWA | Founded as a women's wing of the Gandhian Textile Labour Association in Gujarat, later independent | Organised unorganised-sector women - vendors, hawkers, home-based workers - into a union combining collective bargaining with training, credit and technical help; spread to Indore, Bhopal, Delhi and Lucknow under Ela Bhatt |
| ✅ Shahada | 1972, Dhulia district, Maharashtra, led first by Gandhian Sarvodaya workers and later Maoist activists | Bhil tribal women prominent in a drought-relief and land movement that became a militant anti-liquor campaign, breaking liquor pots and publicly punishing men who beat their wives |
| ✅ Uttarakhand anti-liquor | Early 1960s, under Gandhian influence including Sarla Behn, Mira Behn and Sunderlal Bahuguna | Women picketing liquor vendors and demanding prohibition; anti-liquor movements recurred, most recently in Andhra Pradesh in the mid-1990s |
| ✅ Chipko | From 1974, Uttarakhand | Named from women hugging trees to prevent felling; first major environmental movement, and the origin of the argument that environmental issues are women's issues because deterioration falls hardest on those who fetch fuel, fodder and water |
| ✅ Chattisgarh Mines Shramik Sangh | 1977 | Militant women against mechanisation seen as harming women's employment; the Mahila Mukti Morcha developed as a new platform |
| ✅ Bodh Gaya | 1979, Bihar, Chhatra Yuva Sangharsh Vahini | Raised the demand that land be registered in women's names as well - an idea later adopted in some states for government-distributed land and tree *pattas* |
| ✅ Bhopal Gas Peedit Mahila Udyog Sangathan | After 1984 | Led the effort to secure justice for gas-leak victims |
| ✅ Samagra Mahila Aghadi | Mid-1980s, women's wing of the Shetkari Sangathana | Over one lakh women at its November 1986 session; stand against the brutalisation of politics; decided to field all-women panels for panchayat and zilla parishad elections |

✅ **Fact:** a distinct stream of **"autonomous" women's groups** mushroomed in urban centres from the mid-1970s, many formed by women active in or influenced by the Naxalite movement, whose decline triggered debate on gender relations and women's place in political organisations. Among the earliest were the **Progressive Women's Organization** at Osmania University, Hyderabad, in **1974**, and the **Purogami Stree Sangathana** in Pune and **Stree Mukti Sangathana** in Bombay in **1975**. The United Nations declaration of **1975** as International Women's Year contributed to a flurry of activity, with March 8 celebrated as International Women's Day in Maharashtra for the first time and a women's conference in Pune in October attended by women from Maoist groups, the Socialist and Republican parties, the CPM and the Lal Nishan Party.

✅ **Fact:** after the Emergency, a Delhi group launched **Manushi**, a journal that documented and analysed the movement under **Madhu Kishwar**. Women in the Janata Party, mostly Socialists, formed the **Mahila Dakshata Samiti** and played a major role in the campaign against dowry, in which the Delhi-based **Stri Sangharsh** was also active. The dowry campaign ran from **1979** through street rallies, plays, demonstrations outside the homes of victims and demands for legal reform; the **Janwadi Mahila Samiti** and the **All India Democratic Women's Association**, set up in 1981, conducted door-to-door campaigns. A bill to amend the **Dowry Prohibition Act of 1961** went to a Joint Select Committee, and amendments strengthening the law were passed in **1984**, with minor additions later. The movement declined afterwards amid a feeling that victories had not meant much, given the persistence of dowry and the difficulty of securing convictions.

✅ **Fact:** the other major campaign concerned rape, especially custodial rape. The **Rameeza Bee** case in Hyderabad in 1978, the **Mathura** case in Maharashtra and the **Maya Tyagi** case in western U.P. in 1980 brought the issue to public attention; a bill was introduced in 1980 and passed in **1983**, treating custodial rape as a more heinous offence and shifting the burden of proof from the victim to the accused, which made a "sea change" in the possibility of convictions. The campaign also exposed sharp divisions within the movement, caused as much by contests over turf as by ideology and strategy, and the government's prompt response left many activists feeling their agenda had been appropriated - revealing, the source notes, "the inherent weakness in a strategy that does not have room for absorbing reformist gains".

✅ **Fact:** the Shah Bano protests of 1985-86 lacked the same enthusiasm or unity, because the issue was complicated by an overall communal atmosphere in which Muslim identity became entangled with women's rights and Hindu communalists' enthusiasm for Muslim women's rights left activists confused. The agitation against the **Roop Kanwar** death at **Deorala** in Rajasthan in 1987 followed similar lines, with Hindu communal groups portraying it as an attack on tradition and putting women on the streets to defend sati; some of the most effective opposition came from **Arya Samajists such as Swami Agnivesh**, who toured rural Rajasthan and Haryana and challenged the head priests of the Puri and Benares temples to debate the claim of scriptural sanction, while Gandhians in Orissa organised a rally of 10,000 women to *gherao* the head priest of the Puri temple, who could not prove the claim; opposition also came from the anti-caste movement in Maharashtra and from rural women in Rajasthan.

**Phase three - institutionalisation, from the 1980s.**

✅ **Fact:** among autonomous groups there was a clear shift from mass campaigns to women's centres for legal aid, counselling, documentation, research and publication, partly because mass campaigns focused on legal reform had not solved the problems they addressed. Groups such as **Saheli** in Delhi emphasised women's joys as well as problems and encouraged expression through music, dance and art; others produced magazines, monitored media for derogatory advertising and film content, raised health issues, or campaigned against foeticide, for the girl child, and for water and housing for slum women. **Anveshi** in Hyderabad became a platform for theoretical study and the **Centre for Women's Development Studies** in Delhi promoted research, documentation and later a journal; university-based centres multiplied in the 1990s and Women's Studies entered curricula.

✅ **Fact:** the source's assessment of this phase is balanced: what appeared to some activists as a watering down was more likely a diffusion of the movement's ideas into wider society, accompanied by some dilution of ideological sharpness. It also records the movement's weaknesses - lack of unity on goals, strategies and methods; sectarianism; a tendency to react to immediate crises rather than build a consensus agenda; allegations that some sections were swayed by foreign funding into donor-driven issues; convoluted theoretical debates; and a persistent gap between urban educated groups and rural or poor urban women's concerns.

✅ **Fact:** government policy was nevertheless affected. A **National Perspective Plan for Women** came in **1988**, detailing plans for women's health, education and political participation. The **Panchayati Raj bill was introduced in 1989** - though passed only in **1993** - instituting **one-third** of panchayat seats reserved for women. The **Scheme for Development of Women and Children in Rural Areas** sponsored Mahila Mandals in rural areas, and the **Mahila Kosh** extended credit to them. Women panchayat members and village pradhans began to receive special training, and a serious move to reserve one-third of parliamentary seats for women had been under way for some time, generating considerable debate.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the 1988-to-1993 interval between proposal and enactment for local-body reservation is the exact structural parallel to the later delay in operating parliamentary reservation, and it is the historically grounded point a candidate can make about the difference between legislating a right and bringing it into effect.

### 5. The measured record: health, education and the regional contrast

✅ **Fact:** the aggregate picture is severe. Female literacy in Barmer, the worst district of Rajasthan, was **8 per cent**, lower than Burkina Faso's 10 per cent; the infant mortality rate in Ganjam, India's worst district in this respect, was **164 per thousand live births**, worse than Mali's 161; the fertility rate in U.P. was **5.1**; the female-to-male ratio in Haryana was **865 women per 1,000 men**, lower than that of any country in the world; among elderly widows, mortality figures are generally **86 per cent higher** than for married women of the same age. Half of rural females aged 12-14 had never been enrolled in any school - above two-thirds in U.P., Madhya Pradesh and Bihar and as high as **82 per cent in Rajasthan**. The average number of years of schooling for persons aged 25 and above was **2.4** in India, but **1.2 for females** against 3.5 for males. Half of all Indian females aged 15-19 were illiterate, against under 10 per cent in China. Overall female literacy was **39 per cent in 1990-91**, in a system where single-teacher schools were one-third of all schools in 1986, two-thirds of teachers were found absent during inspections, there were fifty-eight children per teacher at the 6-10 age level, and India ranked 82nd of 116 countries in the share of public expenditure on education in gross national product.
