✅ **Fact:** the contrast within India is the analytical key. **Kerala's** adult female literacy was **86 per cent in 1990-91** against 94 for men - higher than China's 68 and 86 and higher than any individual Chinese province; rural female literacy in the 10-14 age group reached 98 per cent by 1987-88; total fertility was **1.8** in 1992, below replacement; infant mortality was **17 per thousand** against 31 in China and 79 in India as a whole; and the female-male ratio improved from 1,004 to 1,036 between 1901 and 1991 while the all-India figure fell from 972 to 927.

✅ **Fact:** **Himachal Pradesh** is the decisive case because it had no such historical advantage. Crude literacy rates there in 1961 were 9 per cent for females and 21 for males, below all-India averages; by 1987-88 literacy in the 10-14 age group was 81 per cent for rural females and 97 per cent in urban areas, where women had outstripped men; in girls attending school in urban areas Himachal did better than Kerala, 95 against 94 per cent. Its female-male ratio rose from 884 to 976 between 1901 and 1991, the biggest increase in India at +92. **Tamil Nadu** showed comparable gains: fertility of 2.2 in 1991, infant mortality of 58 per thousand in 1990-92 - the third lowest in the country - female literacy in the 10-14 group of 85.6 per cent urban and 70.8 rural, and about 97 per cent of children aged 12-23 months vaccinated by 1992-93, the highest in India.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the source's conclusions from this variation are directly usable. History and tradition matter, and the south has historically done better than the north - but a strong commitment in public policy can bring rapid change, as Himachal Pradesh shows. Economic prosperity does not automatically produce gender justice: Punjab and still more Haryana are prosperous and perform poorly. Female literacy and education are "unambiguous winners", with links to every other indicator; and unequal access to health facilities and improved living standards is the major cause of the decline in the all-India female-male ratio from 972 to 927 between 1901 and 1991 - not because women's survival chances fell absolutely, but because women gained less than men from improvements.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** the Indian state legislated equality for women far ahead of social practice, and where outcomes actually improved, the decisive instruments were universal schooling and accessible primary health care rather than legal reform alone.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Presenting inheritance rights as "not claimed" makes women's non-use of the law sound like a choice, when it reflects coercion within families.

**Reply:** The source's explanation is structural rather than voluntarist: patrilocal residence is named as the principal cause, and dowry is described as persisting because it is the only share of parental property a daughter actually receives. That is a description of constraint, not of preference. The examination point is that a law conferring a right on a person who must live elsewhere, and whose security depends on the goodwill of those she would sue, is not self-executing.

**Residual:** How far the 1955-56 Acts changed practice cannot be measured directly in this period, because the relevant behaviour - who inherits, who divorces, who is maintained - was not systematically recorded.

### UPSC application

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** questions on women's movements and on women's reservation route to the Indian Society, Social Justice and Polity owners. This lesson's distinctive supply is chronological and causal: the Rau draft of 1944 and the Ambedkar committee; the four Acts of 1955-56 and their fragmentation; the phases of the movement from Tebhaga to the autonomous groups; the 1983 rape-law amendment shifting the burden of proof; the Dowry Prohibition Act amendments of 1984; the 1988 National Perspective Plan; and the 1989-to-1993 passage of local-body reservation. The 2018 General Studies Paper I demand on the women's movement and women of lower social strata is routed to Indian Society, and this material supplies its historical spine - including the honest finding of a persistent gap between urban educated groups and rural or poor urban women.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Saying the Hindu Code Bill was enacted as a single code; it became four separate Acts.
- Dating all four Acts to 1955; the Marriage Act is 1955 and the other three 1956.
- Claiming the 1955-56 reforms created complete equality; they substantially expanded rights within Hindu personal law and did not create full legal or social equality.
- Treating the post-1947 decades as a movement vacuum; the 1950s and 1960s were a phase of consolidation and institutional work.
- Attributing Chipko solely to environmentalism; its women's dimension is part of the record.
- Giving the all-India female literacy figure without the state contrasts, which carry the argument.
- Explaining the falling female-male ratio as an absolute decline in women's survival; it reflects women gaining less than men from improvements.

### Revision notes

1. All India Women's Conference founded 1926; the national movement transformed the image of women from recipient of justice to comrade.
2. The Constitution gave women the vote without qualification of education, property or income.
3. B.N. Rau committee draft code 1944; Ambedkar committee bill; opposition including President Rajendra Prasad; enacted as four Acts - Hindu Marriage Act 1955, and the Hindu Succession, Hindu Minority and Guardianship and Hindu Adoptions and Maintenance Acts 1956.
4. Shah Bano in 1985 showed the limits of extending reform to other communities; the asymmetry of mobilisation, lakhs against hundreds, is the structural point.
5. The vote is used; inheritance rights are largely not claimed because of patrilocal residence; dowry persists partly as the only share of parental property received.
6. Phase one, consolidation: Partition rehabilitation, hostels, vocational centres; National Federation of Indian Women formed in 1954 when Communist women left the AIWC.
7. Women in other movements: Tebhaga's Nari Bahini and Mahila Atma Raksha Samitis; Telangana participation without separate organisations.
8. Phase two: anti-price-rise movement 1973-75; SEWA under Ela Bhatt; Shahada 1972 anti-liquor; Uttarakhand anti-liquor from the early 1960s; Chipko from 1974; Chattisgarh Mines Shramik Sangh 1977; Bodh Gaya 1979 land-in-women's-names demand; Bhopal Gas Peedit Mahila Udyog Sangathan; Samagra Mahila Aghadi with over a lakh women in November 1986.
9. Autonomous groups: Progressive Women's Organization 1974; Purogami Stree Sangathana and Stree Mukti Sangathana 1975; International Women's Year 1975; Manushi under Madhu Kishwar.
10. Dowry campaign from 1979; Dowry Prohibition Act 1961 amended in 1984; rape campaign after Rameeza Bee 1978, Mathura and Maya Tyagi 1980, producing the 1983 amendment on custodial rape and burden of proof.
11. Shah Bano and Deorala 1987 protests were divided by the communal context; Arya Samajists and Gandhians led effective anti-sati mobilisation.
12. Phase three: legal-aid and research centres, Saheli, Anveshi, the Centre for Women's Development Studies, Women's Studies in universities.
13. Policy outcomes: National Perspective Plan for Women 1988; Panchayati Raj bill 1989, passed 1993, with one-third of panchayat seats reserved for women; DWACRA; Mahila Kosh.
14. Record: Barmer female literacy 8 per cent; Ganjam infant mortality 164; U.P. fertility 5.1; Haryana sex ratio 865; all-India female literacy 39 per cent in 1990-91; average schooling 1.2 years for females.
15. Kerala: female literacy 86 per cent, fertility 1.8, infant mortality 17, ratio rising from 1,004 to 1,036. Himachal Pradesh: from 9 per cent female literacy in 1961 to 81 per cent rural in the 10-14 group by 1987-88, ratio up 92 points. Tamil Nadu: fertility 2.2, infant mortality 58, vaccination about 97 per cent.
16. Conclusions: policy commitment can override historical disadvantage; prosperity does not guarantee gender justice; female education is the decisive variable.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does the comparison between Kerala, Himachal Pradesh, Haryana and Punjab matter more for a Mains answer than the all-India averages on women's status?

**Model answer:** Because the averages describe a problem while the comparison identifies a cause. Kerala could be dismissed as a historical special case - early educational investment in Travancore and Cochin and matrilineal custom - and its outcomes are exceptional: adult female literacy of 86 per cent in 1990-91, total fertility of 1.8 in 1992, infant mortality of 17 per thousand and a female-male ratio rising from 1,004 to 1,036 between 1901 and 1991 while the all-India figure fell from 972 to 927. Himachal Pradesh removes the special-case defence, because it began below the all-India average, with 9 per cent female literacy in 1961, and reached 81 per cent rural and 97 per cent urban literacy in the 10-14 age group by 1987-88, with the largest improvement in the country in its sex ratio at 92 points; Tamil Nadu shows a similar trajectory. Punjab and Haryana complete the argument in the other direction: both are prosperous and perform poorly, Haryana's ratio of 865 being lower than that of any country in the world. Together the four cases establish that public policy in schooling and health, not income and not history alone, determines gender outcomes - which is a causal claim an examiner can reward, unlike a recital of national averages.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that economic growth delivers gender justice automatically. The prosperous states in this comparison are among the worst performers, and the best performers are distinguished by universal provision rather than by income.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

"The Indian state legislated for women's equality long before Indian society was prepared to deliver it." Examine with reference to the period since 1947. (15 marks, 250 words)

#### Model answer

The proposition is well supported, and its mechanism can be specified.

The legislation came early and was genuinely contested. Women received the unqualified vote in the Constitution. The Hindu Code Bill, drafted by the Rau committee in 1944 and reframed by Ambedkar's committee, met opposition reaching to President Rajendra Prasad and was passed only in fragments - the Hindu Marriage Act of 1955 and the Hindu Succession, Hindu Minority and Guardianship and Hindu Adoptions and Maintenance Acts of 1956.

Delivery lagged structurally. The vote was used immediately and remains so, particularly by rural women. Inheritance rights were largely not claimed, because patrilocal residence makes a share in a father's village unusable and dowry survives as the only share of parental property a daughter actually receives. The 1985 Shah Bano sequence showed that extension to other communities depended on mobilisation rather than principle, since opponents brought lakhs to the streets and supporters hundreds.

Where outcomes improved, the instruments were universal rather than legal. Kerala reached 86 per cent adult female literacy; Himachal Pradesh rose from 9 per cent in 1961 to 81 per cent rural literacy in the 10-14 group by 1987-88; prosperous Haryana's sex ratio of 865 shows that income alone does not deliver.

The movement supplied the pressure - the 1983 rape-law amendment, the 1984 dowry amendments, the 1988 National Perspective Plan and local-body reservation proposed in 1989 and enacted in 1993.

Law ran ahead of society, and schooling and health, not statutes alone, closed part of the distance.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the legislative sequence dated exactly, including the four-Act point 4 + the non-claiming of inheritance explained by residence 3 + the Shah Bano asymmetry as evidence of the social limit 3 + the state comparison with at least three figures 3 + the movement's legislative achievements 2.**

**Bridge to Lesson 15:** Caste and gender were hierarchies the Republic attacked. The third great social division was one it believed it had defeated in 1947 - and which returned as an ideology rather than as a riot.

## Lesson 15 - Communalism as ideology: revival, social base and state response

Progress: 15/18 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the definitions, the phases and riot data, the petty-bourgeois analysis, the opportunism of secular parties and the structure of the Ayodhya issue

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the survey history's chapter on the revival and growth of communalism, read in full, with the canonical advanced file's insistence that communalism be treated as an ideology and that Nehru's and the historians' strong formulations be attributed
CA search: "India communal harmony secularism debate 2026 Places of Worship Act Supreme Court news"
CA found: Reported - in 2026 the Supreme Court is hearing challenges to the Places of Worship (Special Provisions) Act, 1991, which freezes the religious character of places of worship as they stood on 15 August 1947 with the Ayodhya site excepted; an interim order has restrained fresh suits and the proceedings in pending ones until validity is decided, and a verdict on the Sambhal survey plea was reserved in September 2026
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: ideology, violence and the phases

```text
   DEFINITIONS
   SECULARISM   = separation of religion from state and politics; religion as private;
                  the state must not discriminate on grounds of religion or caste
   COMMUNALISM  = the BELIEF that Indian society is divided into religious communities
                  whose economic, political, social and cultural interests diverge and are
                  even hostile, BECAUSE of religious difference
                  -> a belief system, a way of viewing society, economy and polity
   RELATION     communal ideology can exist WITHOUT violence
                communal violence cannot exist WITHOUT communal ideology
                => ideology is the disease; violence is the symptom

   PHASES OF INTENSITY (riots and deaths as recorded)
   1950s        marginal: communal parties poll poorly in 1952, 1957, 1962
                late 1950s riots: killed 7 (1958) -> 41 (1959) -> 108 (1961)
                Jabalpur 1961 shocks the nation -> NATIONAL INTEGRATION COUNCIL
   1962         China war: national unity; communal sentiment retreats - briefly
   mid-1960s    upswing: Jan Sangh 14 seats (1962) -> 35 (1967); coalition ministries in
                north Indian states; riots 1,070 (1964), 520 (1969), 521 (1970);
                killed 1,919 / 673 / 298
   1971-77      respite: under 250 riots a year, under 1,000 killed; Jan Sangh down to 22;
                Bangladesh war blows to both Hindu and Muslim communalism
   1975-77      Emergency: leaders and activists jailed -> violence and propaganda fall sharply
   1977-79      Janata period: increase, with RSS and Jan Sangh influence inside the party
   from 1978    ENDEMIC: widespread, pre-planned, organised, longer, funded and armed
```

*The phase table is the answer to "why did communalism revive?" - it shows that intensity tracks the political opportunity available to communal organisations, not any change in religion.*

### 1. Definitions first, because the argument depends on them

✅ **Fact:** secularism basically means separation of religion from the state and politics and its treatment as a private, personal affair, and requires that the state not discriminate against a citizen on grounds of religion or caste. Communalism is an ideology based on the belief that Indian society is divided into religious communities whose economic, political, social and cultural interests diverge and are even hostile to each other **because of their religious differences**. It is above all a belief system through which society, economy and polity are viewed and explained, and around which effort is made to organise politics. The source states that as an ideology it is akin to racialism, anti-Semitism and fascism, and "in fact, it can be considered the Indian form of fascism".

✅ **Fact:** the relation between ideology and violence is asymmetrical. Communal violence usually occurs when communal thinking reaches a certain intensity and the atmosphere is vitiated by fear, suspicion and hatred. Communal ideology can prevail without violence; communal violence cannot exist without communal ideology. Ideology and politics are the disease, violence only the external symptom - which is why awareness typically registers only when violence breaks out, and why those who organise violence aim not at genocide but at creating a situation that communalises the masses.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is a strong, attributable historiographical position and must be presented as such. The phrase "the Indian version of fascism" is associated with Nehru and carried forward in this historian's judgement; a candidate should write "the nationalist-left reading treats communalism as an ideology akin to fascism" rather than presenting it as a neutral definition. Equally, the distinction between ideology and violence is the analytical move that earns marks, because most answers treat riots as the phenomenon to be explained.

### 2. Why it was marginal first, and why that bred complacency

✅ **Fact:** it was one of the great triumphs of the national movement that, despite Partition and the barbaric riots accompanying it, the Indian people accepted secularism as a basic value, enshrined it in the Constitution and set out to build a secular state and society. The legacy of the freedom struggle, Gandhi's martyrdom, Nehru's total commitment and the active support of Patel, Maulana Azad, Rajagopalachari and others made secularism dominant in the 1950s, and communal parties polled poorly in **1952, 1957 and 1962**.

✅ **Fact:** consequently people became complacent, believing that economic development and the spread of education, science and technology would automatically weaken communal thinking. It was not realised that communalism has passive and active phases depending on circumstances, and would not disappear without active struggle. Meanwhile communal ideologues continued their work and organisations - the RSS, Jan Sangh, Jamaat-e-Islami, Muslim League, Akali Dal and various Christian communal groups in Kerala - continued to function.

### 3. The phases, with their numbers

✅ **Fact:** the late 1950s saw a series of riots: persons killed rose from **7 in 1958 to 41 in 1959 and 108 in 1961**; the **Jabalpur riot of 1961** shook the nation and Nehru reacted by immediately forming the **National Integration Council**. Chinese aggression in 1962 aroused national unity and communal sentiment retreated, but the interlude was short-lived.

✅ **Fact:** from the mid-1960s the disruptive forces were on the upswing. The Jan Sangh increased its parliamentary strength from **14 in 1962 to 35 in the 1967 general elections**, participated in coalition ministries in several north Indian states and attracted considerable rural support in U.P., Madhya Pradesh and Rajasthan. Riots numbered **1,070 in 1964, 520 in 1969 and 521 in 1970**, with **1,919, 673 and 298 killed** respectively. There was respite from 1971 to 1977, with riots not exceeding 250 in any year and the killed under 1,000, as Indira Gandhi consolidated power in 1971 and the Jan Sangh's strength fell from 35 to 22; the Bangladesh war at the end of 1971 dealt a major blow to both Hindu and Muslim communalism. Communalism and communal violence began to increase again **from 1978 and have been endemic since**.

✅ **Fact:** two control episodes are recorded. During the Emergency from 1975 to 1977, when the major leaders and most activists of the RSS, Jan Sangh and Jamaat-e-Islami were arrested, communal violence and the level of communal propaganda **came down drastically**, since few were left to organise riots or promote hatred. During the Janata government there was an increase, because of the strong influence of the RSS and Jan Sangh within the party and government; the momentum was such that Indira Gandhi's return in 1980 failed to check it.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** these two episodes are the closest thing to a natural experiment in the record, and they support the source's core claim. If communal violence were a spontaneous eruption of religious feeling, removing a few thousand organisers would not have reduced it sharply; and if it were driven by economic distress alone, it would not have risen during a period of political accommodation of communal organisations. Organisation, not religiosity, is the operative variable.

✅ **Fact:** the character of violence also changed: it spread to almost all parts of the country including areas earlier believed immune - Kerala, Tamil Nadu, Andhra Pradesh, West Bengal and Orissa - reached villages, involved all religious groups, and became increasingly pre-planned, well-organised and of longer duration, with rioters amply supplied with funds, firearms and other destructive materials.

### 4. The social base: why the petty bourgeoisie

✅ **Fact:** communalism has a concrete social base and reflects socio-economic and political conditions, but in a **distorted** manner: what it declares to be the social reality is not the social reality, what it declares to be the causes of discontent are not the causes, and what it offers as solutions are not solutions. It is therefore no answer to any of the problems that generate it, and it undermines the real struggle to change social conditions.

✅ **Fact:** the specific mechanism the source identifies is the position of the middle classes or petty bourgeoisie. Independence and the first three Plans opened a wide range of middle-class opportunities - Indianisation and expansion of the officer ranks of the armed forces and private firms, expansion of the administrative apparatus, growth of banking, trading and industrial companies, school and college education and other social services, and the training and recruitment of engineers, doctors and scientists. **This initial push was exhausted by the mid-1960s.** The pattern and rate of economic development then failed to generate large-scale employment or to sustain the expansion of social services, returning the petty bourgeoisie to job scarcity, competition and discontent - while the spread of education and rapid population growth led millions of peasant and working-class youth to aspire to the same positions. Changes in agrarian relations simultaneously produced new strata of rich and middle peasants and capitalist farmers - a rural bourgeoisie and petty bourgeoisie - providing fertile ground for communal and casteist ideologies.

✅ **Fact:** the source adds a non-economic dimension. Indians have been losing an old world without gaining a new one: traditional institutions and support systems of caste, joint family, village and urban neighbourhood have broken down rapidly, while the new solidarities of class, trade unions, kisan sabhas, youth organisations, social clubs, political parties and voluntary associations have made tardy progress. Many therefore turn to communal organisations as an alternative focus of unity and solidarity; and the disappearance of older values under the pressure of the profit motive, competition and careerism has left "a moral and cultural vacuum which is highly conducive to ideologies based on fear and hate". A further factor is the inevitable exhaustion of the political idealism generated by the national movement, without a new unifying, anti-divisive vision to replace it.

✅ **Fact:** the source enters a warning that a careful answer should reproduce: great care must be exercised in social analysis of communalism, which should rest on serious empirical and theoretical research; it is not easy to assign communal motives to classes other than the petty bourgeoisie, and there is "so far no evidence that the capitalist class in India backs communalism", though this cannot be asserted for the future.

### 5. Immediate causes, state laxity and political opportunism

✅ **Fact:** the immediate causes of riots are conjunctural - local, specific and accidental, such as a minor religious dispute, the teasing of a girl, or a violent quarrel between two individuals of different religions - and they become operative only where there has been **prior communalisation** of the area. They are sparks lighting a fire for which the ground has already been prepared.

✅ **Fact:** additional factors are recorded: the involvement of the urban poor and lumpen elements, whose numbers grew with lopsided development and large-scale migration into towns, living in overcrowded areas without health, education, sanitation or drinking water, whose social anger finds expression in spontaneous violence and loot when a riot provides the opportunity; and criminal gangs engaged in smuggling, illicit liquor, gambling, drug pushing and kidnapping using riots to settle scores.

✅ **Fact:** state laxity is treated as a major factor. The state alone possesses the instruments to counter communal violence, and immediate, effective action is the only viable way of dealing with it; yet the administration has seldom acted firmly, decisively, in time and with the full force of the law. Action has rarely been taken even under existing laws against instigators of communal hatred, despite the intensive inflammatory propaganda that invariably precedes violence. Communalists and communal ideology have made serious inroads into state apparatuses, and communalised sections of the police have often worsened matters by inaction and partisanship.

✅ **Fact:** political opportunism by secular parties is identified as another major factor from the 1960s, with a specific list: the Congress allied with the Muslim League in Kerala in the early 1960s; Communist parties allied with the League in Kerala and the Akali Dal in Punjab in the late 1960s, justifying it by arguing that minority communalism was understandable and less dangerous; Socialists and other secular parties entered seat adjustments and then coalition governments with the Jan Sangh in several north Indian states in 1967; Jayaprakash Narayan permitted the RSS, Jan Sangh and Jamaat-e-Islami to become the backbone of his movement in 1974-75; the Jan Sangh became part of the Janata Party in 1977; and in the November 1989 elections the Janata Dal formed an indirect electoral alliance with the BJP and then a government with its support, sanctioned indirectly by the Communist parties. The consequence is described as making communal parties respectable and legitimising communalism, whittling down one of the major contributions of the national movement and the Nehru era - "making communalism a dirty word even when failing to root it out".

✅ **Fact:** the source applies the same standard to concessions: the reversal of the Supreme Court's judgment in the Shah Bano case and the opening of the disputed Ayodhya structure in 1986, and V.P. Singh's declaration of the Prophet's birthday as a holiday in 1990, are described as concessions to Muslim and Hindu communalisms which did not lessen communal tensions but aggravated them.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the political narrative of those decisions belongs to the preceding session on the Rajiv years. What this lesson contributes is the analytical proposition: symmetrical concession to competing communal claims does not neutralise them; it validates the premise both share, namely that religious communities are the relevant political units.

✅ **Fact:** two further distinctions are recorded. A sharp distinction must be made between **communal parties**, structured around communal ideology, and parties that are basically secular but opportunistic - the latter fact being "a major obstacle in the burgeoning forth of communalism". And there is no difference in kind between majority and minority communalism, which are variants of the same ideology and equally dangerous, though minority communalisms can end in separatism while majority communalism can take the form of fascism, and different communalisms feed and strengthen each other.

### 6. The Ayodhya issue: structure, not narrative

✅ **Fact:** the source explains why Hindu communalism needed a religious issue. Muslim communalism had flourished in the 1940s on the cry of Islam in danger, while Hindu communalism remained weak because Hinduism is not an organised religion with a single sacred book or a hierarchical priestly class, Hindus have neither one God nor one set of beliefs, there are no strict rules determining who is a Hindu, there is a long tradition of religious tolerance, and it was not easy to convince a large religious majority that its religion was in danger. From the late 1970s Hindu communal politics therefore searched for a religious issue, and found one in the Babri Masjid-Ram Janmabhoomi dispute.

✅ **Fact:** the chronology, stated as the source records it: a mosque was built by a governor of Babur at Ayodhya in the early sixteenth century; some Hindus claimed in the nineteenth century that it stood on the birthplace of Ram where a temple had existed; the issue took a serious turn only in **December 1949**, when a communal-minded district magistrate permitted a few Hindus to enter the mosque and install idols - an action condemned by Patel as home minister and by Nehru, though the U.P. government felt it could not reverse it and instead **locked the structure**, barring both communities, which was accepted as a temporary solution pending the court dispute. Quiet lasted until **1983**, when the Vishwa Hindu Parishad began a campaign; on **1 February 1986** the district judge reopened the structure, gave possession to Hindu priests and permitted worship, arousing passions and producing riots in which **sixty-five persons were killed in U.P. towns alone**. Powerful Hindu and Muslim communal groups were then ranged against each other, and the issue was allowed to fester while both communalisms consolidated.

✅ **Fact:** the source's judgement on the nature of the dispute is explicit: though it appears religious on the surface, in reality communalists are not interested in religion but only in the manipulation and exploitation of religion and religious identity for political ends; religious differences are not the root cause of communalism; communalism is not the same as religious-mindedness; and it is the intrusion of religion into politics and the affairs of the state that is undesirable, as Gandhi put it in 1942 - "Religion is a personal matter which should have no place in politics."

⚠️ **Boundary note.** The political sequence from the 1989 campaign through the 1990 mobilisation and the 1992 demolition is taught in the preceding session; it is not re-narrated here. What belongs to this lesson is the structural analysis - why a religious issue was needed, how a local dispute was converted into a national mobilisation, and why concession aggravated rather than reduced tension. Constitutional and litigation questions belong to Polity.

### 7. The conclusion the source draws, with its qualification

✅ **Fact:** despite the growth of communalism and communal organisations, India "still has a basically healthy secular society". Communalism is perhaps the most serious challenge facing Indian society and polity, but it is not yet the dominant mode of thought; even where communalists have won elections, voters have largely expressed discontent with the existing state of political and economic affairs rather than imbibed communal ideology; the Indian people remain basically secular and believers in communal ideology constitute a fringe. Even where riots have occurred there is no permanent divide, and in no part of the country is "an aggressive majority arranged against a beleaguered minority". Popular consciousness has been a major barrier to communalism's spread in rural areas and much of urban India, which explains why an ideology beginning in the last quarter of the nineteenth century has still failed to strike deep roots in large parts of the country.

✅ **Fact:** the crucial structural safeguard identified is the absence of **active state support**: the Indian state has been basically secular and opposed to communalism, and though it has lacked political will to deal firmly with communal violence, it has not promoted communal ideology through textbooks, mass media or administrative measures. The comparative evidence adduced is that communal and communal-type movements cannot prevail without state support or at least the neutrality and passivity of state power - the colonial period, Pakistan and Bangladesh, and the fascist movements of Italy, Germany, Japan and Spain where they succeeded, against France and the United States where they failed.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** communalism revived not because religion changed but because organisation, political opportunity and a social base converged, while the state's response weakened - and it has been contained, so far, because the state has not adopted the ideology.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Treating communalism as "the Indian form of fascism" is a political judgement dressed as analysis, and using riot statistics as a measure of ideology confuses the symptom the source itself says is secondary.

**Reply:** Both criticisms have force and the honest response is to relabel rather than abandon. The fascism comparison is an attributed judgement of a particular historiographical tradition and should be presented as such. The riot data are used here not as a measure of ideology but as an indicator of the organisational and political opportunity available to communal organisations - which is why the Emergency and Janata episodes are informative: the same society produced very different violence levels under different political conditions.

**Residual:** The source's own petty-bourgeois explanation is offered with a warning about the limits of the evidence, and the disclaimer about the capitalist class is explicitly provisional. The social-base question is therefore genuinely unsettled, and a candidate should say so rather than assert a class theory of communalism as established.

### UPSC application

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** communalism questions route primarily to the Indian Society owner, which handles contemporary manifestations, and to Polity for constitutional secularism. What this lesson supplies is the historical structure: the definition of communalism as ideology rather than violence, the phase data, the organisational variable, the opportunism of secular parties, and the safeguard of a state that has not adopted the ideology. A 2023 General Studies Paper I demand on the post-liberal economy, ethnic identity and communalism routes to Indian Society; the argument this lesson supplies to it is that the social base lies in frustrated mobility among the petty bourgeoisie, which is a claim about economic change rather than about religion.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Defining communalism as communal violence; the source treats violence as a symptom.
- Presenting "the Indian form of fascism" as a neutral definition; it is an attributed judgement.
- Explaining revival by religious revival; the explanatory variables are organisation, political opportunity and blocked middle-class mobility.
- Omitting the Emergency and Janata episodes, which are the most informative evidence on the organisational variable.
- Treating minority communalism as less dangerous; the source treats them as variants of one ideology with different terminal forms.
- Re-narrating the Ayodhya politics of 1989-92, which the preceding session owns.
- Ignoring the source's own methodological warning about assigning communal motives to classes.

### Revision notes

1. Secularism: separation of religion from state and politics, no discrimination on grounds of religion or caste.
2. Communalism: the belief that religious communities have divergent and hostile interests because of religious difference - a belief system, not a riot.
3. Ideology can exist without violence; violence cannot exist without ideology; the purpose of organised violence is to communalise, not to exterminate.
4. Communal parties polled poorly in 1952, 1957 and 1962; complacency followed.
5. Deaths in riots: 7 (1958), 41 (1959), 108 (1961); Jabalpur 1961 led to the National Integration Council.
6. Jan Sangh: 14 seats (1962), 35 (1967), 22 (1971); riots 1,070 (1964), 520 (1969), 521 (1970); killed 1,919, 673, 298.
7. Respite 1971-77; Emergency arrests sharply reduced violence and propaganda; the Janata period saw an increase; endemic from 1978.
8. Violence became widespread, pre-planned, organised, longer, funded and armed, and reached areas earlier thought immune.
9. Social base: exhaustion of middle-class opportunity by the mid-1960s; blocked mobility; new rural bourgeois strata; the breakdown of old solidarities without new ones; exhaustion of national-movement idealism.
10. Immediate causes are conjunctural sparks that work only where prior communalisation exists; urban poor and criminal gangs participate.
11. State laxity: failure to act on inflammatory propaganda, inroads into state apparatuses, communalised sections of the police.
12. Opportunism: Congress-League in Kerala in the early 1960s; Communists with the League and Akali Dal in the late 1960s; Socialists with the Jan Sangh in 1967; the RSS and Jamaat in the 1974-75 movement; Jan Sangh in the Janata Party in 1977; Janata Dal and BJP in 1989.
13. Concessions aggravate: Shah Bano reversal, the 1986 opening, and the 1990 holiday declaration.
14. Ayodhya structure: mosque by a governor of Babur in the early sixteenth century; idols installed December 1949, condemned by Patel and Nehru, structure locked; VHP campaign from 1983; district judge reopened it on 1 February 1986, with sixty-five killed in U.P. towns.
15. Safeguard: the Indian state has not promoted communal ideology; comparative evidence shows such movements cannot prevail without state support or passivity.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using the evidence of the Emergency and the Janata period, explain why the source treats organisation rather than religiosity as the operative variable in communal violence.

**Model answer:** During the Emergency of 1975-77 the major leaders and most activists of the RSS, Jan Sangh and Jamaat-e-Islami were arrested, and communal violence and the level of communal propaganda came down drastically, because few were left to organise riots or promote hatred. In the Janata period that followed, violence increased, because of the strong influence of the RSS and Jan Sangh within the party and government - and the momentum was such that Indira Gandhi's return in 1980 did not check it. The same population, with the same religious beliefs, produced very different levels of violence within four years, and the variable that changed was the presence and political position of the organisations. That is consistent with the wider phase record: riots rose as the Jan Sangh grew from 14 seats in 1962 to 35 in 1967 and entered state ministries, and fell in 1971-77 when its strength dropped to 22. It is also consistent with the account of causation, in which local incidents act only as sparks where prior communalisation has prepared the ground.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating riots as spontaneous eruptions of religious feeling. The source's evidence shows that violence became increasingly pre-planned, organised, prolonged, funded and armed, which is a description of capacity rather than of sentiment.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

"Communalism is an ideology, not merely a law-and-order problem." Discuss with post-1947 evidence. (15 marks, 250 words)

#### Model answer

The proposition is the organising claim of the historiography and is supported by the record.

Defined as a belief system - that religious communities have divergent and hostile interests because of religious difference - communalism can exist without violence, while violence cannot exist without it. Violence is therefore a symptom, and organised violence aims at communalising a population rather than at destroying it.

Post-1947 evidence supports this. Communal parties polled poorly in 1952, 1957 and 1962 while communal ideologues continued to work, so ideology survived electoral marginality. Riot intensity then tracked organisational strength: deaths rose from 7 in 1958 to 108 in 1961; riots numbered 1,070 in 1964 as the Jan Sangh grew from 14 seats in 1962 to 35 in 1967 and entered state ministries; violence fell in 1971-77; it dropped sharply during the Emergency when organisers were jailed, and rose in the Janata period when the same organisations gained influence.

The ideological reading also explains what a law-and-order reading cannot. Riots are preceded by intensive inflammatory propaganda against which action is rarely taken; conjunctural sparks operate only where an area has already been communalised; and symmetrical political concessions to competing communal claims, as in 1986 and 1990, aggravated rather than reduced tension, because they validated the shared premise that religious communities are the political units.

The qualification is methodological: the social-base analysis, resting on blocked middle-class mobility after the mid-1960s, is offered by its author with an explicit warning about the limits of the evidence.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the definitional move made precisely 3 + the asymmetry between ideology and violence 2 + phase data with at least four figures 4 + the Emergency and Janata contrast as decisive evidence 3 + the explanatory advantage over a law-and-order reading 2 + the methodological qualification 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 16:** Economy, land, caste, gender and communalism have each shown the same shape - decisive state action against politically weak targets, hesitation elsewhere. The next lesson asks what kind of state produces that shape.

## Lesson 16 - The post-colonial state: what kind of state was this?

Progress: 16/18 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the national-movement legacy, the rejection of the dependency model, democracy and development, and why redistribution stopped where it did

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the survey history's overview chapter on the post-colonial Indian state and the political economy of development, read in full, with the canonical advanced file's thesis on growth, democracy and equity
CA search: "India consumption expenditure survey 2025-26 poverty rate estimate news"
CA found: Reported - in 2026 the national poverty estimate is placed at roughly 4-5 per cent on consumption-survey-based lines while an international comparison puts it near 5.3 per cent at one threshold and near 23.9 per cent at a higher lower-middle-income threshold, an open divergence in measurement that mirrors the long-run difficulty of evaluating the Indian state's distributive record
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four legacies and their consequences

```text
 LEGACY 1  MULTI-CLASS POPULAR MOVEMENT -> a popular-democratic, sovereign national state
           class balance OPEN-ENDED: capitalists, upper peasantry, middle classes,
           organised workers, rural and urban poor all contend inside it
           NOT contenders: feudal landlords (weakened, then marginalised by land reform)
                           international capital (never inside the ruling coalition)

 LEGACY 2  ANTI-IMPERIALISM -> sovereignty defended; bargaining with foreign capital occurs
           BETWEEN an independent state and foreign capital, not INSIDE the ruling coalition
           -> the "triple alliance" model of dependent development does not fit India

 LEGACY 3  DEMOCRACY -> industrial transformation attempted WITHOUT the coercion that
           financed every earlier industrialisation
           enclosures (Britain) | forced collectivisation (USSR) | high land tax (Japan)
           slavery (USA) | suppression of unions (East Asia) | colonial extraction
           (Britain took 85% of its 1801 gross domestic capital formation as unilateral
            transfers from Asian and West Indian colonies)

 LEGACY 4  EQUITY AND PRO-POOR ORIENTATION -> every Plan treats poverty removal as a key
           objective; even right-wing formations must swear by the poor
           BUT the agency to mobilise the poor was never found
```

*The fourth legacy is where the topic's central failure lives, and the source's explanation is about agency, not about intention.*

### 1. Government is not the state

✅ **Fact:** the source insists on a distinction that most answers collapse. A government may be headed by a staunch socialist, may get Parliament and the Constitution to declare socialism an objective and may pass the most radical laws for the poor, the landless, oppressed castes, tribals and bonded labour - and the **state** may still be better described as bourgeois than socialist, because the power balance in the other state apparatuses and in society may differ sharply from that in the government's leadership, determining how laws, the Constitution and institutions are interpreted, implemented and used.

✅ **Fact:** Gandhi is credited with understanding this: in seeking to overthrow the colonial state he did not focus only on the colonial government but worked to corrode the power of the colonial state by countering its influence in the education system, the media, the bureaucracy, the police and above all among the people.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this distinction is the analytical key to the entire topic. Every failure examined in Lessons 7 to 14 occurred at the point where central intention met local apparatus - revenue officials colluding with landlords, police partisanship in riots, cooperative boards captured by dominant families, schools without teachers. A state is not what its statutes say; it is what its apparatuses do.

### 2. Why India was not a dependent or neo-colonial state

✅ **Fact:** the model of a ruling coalition formed by a "triple alliance" between international capital, the state and local capital, central to accounts of dependent development in Latin America, did not apply to India. A foreign bourgeoisie did not form part of the ruling coalition or the Indian state after independence; bargaining with international capital occurred **between an independent state with an entirely indigenous ruling-class coalition and international capital**, which the source calls an important difference in terms of autonomy.

✅ **Fact:** the Indian variant of the dependency thesis - that the state was dominated by big business and landlords increasingly collaborating with foreign finance capital as subordinate partners, so that the post-independence state was neo-colonial - has been challenged, and the source states that overwhelming contrary evidence has pushed such views to the fringes. The specific evidence: the political and economic independence of the Indian capitalist class, which imbibed the movement's anti-imperialist ethos and was at the forefront of developing an economic critique of imperialism from the 1920s; its participation in the Nehruvian consensus; the reduction of foreign capital's role to negligible levels under Indira Gandhi; the corporatist rather than revolutionary direction of the unionised working class, which never posed the kind of threat that historically drove a bourgeoisie into alliance with imperialism; and the marginalisation of feudal landlords, already weakened during the national movement and then completed by land reform except in a few pockets.

✅ **Fact:** the source records the recurring pattern of the critique: the dependency reading resurfaced at the 1966 devaluation, at the early-1980s International Monetary Fund loan - a loan eventually not fully drawn, which helped reduce oil-import dependence through indigenous production - and again after 1991, when reform was described as "a reversal in the direction of policy since decolonisation".

### 3. Democracy and development: the argument, and its evidence

✅ **Fact:** the third legacy is democracy as a fundamental value, described as a product of the people's long struggle rather than a gift of the elite or of the British, and therefore firmly rooted. The claim that India has a fully thriving democracy rather than a merely formal one is supported by high participation and by the continuing entry of new groups and classes into democratic institutions.

✅ **Fact:** the substantive argument is that democracy **shaped** development rather than merely coexisting with it:

- ✅ the initial phase of "primitive accumulation" occurred everywhere else without full democratic rights, through enclosures in Britain, forced collectivisation in the Soviet Union, high land tax in Japan, slavery in the United States, total suppression of trade-union rights in East Asia, and colonial surplus extraction - Britain receiving as unilateral transfers from colonies in Asia and the West Indies **85 per cent of its gross domestic capital formation in 1801**;
- ✅ democracy ensured that India's transition was not financed on the backs of workers and peasants: the working class advanced through collective bargaining, and there was by and large a **net transfer of resources to agriculture** after independence rather than extraction from it;
- ✅ a free press - with 8,600 daily newspapers and 33,000 periodicals at the time of writing - kept governments on their toes and helped avert scarcity and famine, in contrast with China, where the world learnt only many years later of an estimated **16 to 23 million famine deaths between 1959 and 1961**;
- ✅ democracy made an inflationary strategy unviable: the trend rate of inflation did not exceed **8 per cent a year between 1956 and 1990** despite two oil shocks and several droughts;
- ✅ even during post-1991 stabilisation, anti-poverty measures were expanded and the initial rise in poverty was quickly reversed, because in the genuine dilemma between fiscal prudence and egalitarian commitment - which, as Amartya Sen points out, is a choice between two goods rather than between good and bad - democracy ensures that the second is not ignored.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the strongest single argument available for a Mains answer on India's development record, because it reframes slowness as a cost consciously accepted rather than as a failure. It must nevertheless be stated as an argument with a price attached: the same democratic constraint that prevented coercive extraction also prevented the enforcement of ceilings against the rural propertied.

### 4. The measured social record

| Indicator | Then | Later |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Rural population below the poverty line | 58.75 per cent in 1970-71 | 37.3 per cent in 1993-94 |
| ✅ Total population below the poverty line | 56.25 per cent | 36 per cent |
| ✅ Average life expectancy | 32 years in 1950-51 | over 63 years by 1993-94 |
| ✅ Per capita income | - | 2.5 times higher in 1996-97 than in 1950-51, with population up more than 158 per cent |
| ✅ Literacy | 18.3 per cent in 1951 | 62 per cent in 1997 |
| ✅ Infant mortality | 146 per thousand in 1951 | 71 per thousand in 1997 |
| ✅ Famine | Recurrent in colonial times | Made a thing of the past by food self-sufficiency and public action |

✅ **Fact:** and yet more than three hundred million people remained below the poverty line and nearly half the population was illiterate. The source attributes the persistence partly to relatively slower growth - with Indonesia and China cited as cases where high growth produced dramatic poverty reduction - and partly to the nature of the state and the failure to alter its class balance in favour of the poor through popular mobilisation.

### 5. The agency problem: why redistribution stopped

✅ **Fact:** the Nehru-Mahalanobis strategy of growth with equity assumed from the beginning that **popular mobilisation from below** would be necessary to implement radical measures such as land reform, cooperativisation and universal education. The problem was locating an agency to perform that task.

✅ **Fact:** the Congress, with independence, was transformed from a party of struggle and movement into a party of governance, and efforts to make its workers perform the former role proved essentially unsuccessful. Gandhi had anticipated this and unsuccessfully called for the disbanding of the Congress at independence and the forming of a separate organisation to struggle for people's causes, distinct from the one that governed. Nehru tried to fill the void by creating a developmental bureaucracy from the village worker upward, and "unwittingly created a byzantine institution whose main purpose increasingly appeared to be that of multiplying and feeding itself".

✅ **Fact:** the task was political and the bureaucracy could not substitute for it. Nehru expected the left to perform it and tried repeatedly, unsuccessfully, to gain its support. The left initially characterised him as "the running dog of imperialism" and, after abandoning that position, still refused to cooperate because it saw such work as reformist, strengthening a bourgeois state, while its own role was to sharpen contradictions and prepare for its overthrow. It therefore abandoned the space provided by an open-ended democratic structure, dismissing it as bourgeois democracy, and did little either to alter the class balance in the bureaucracy, media, judiciary and education system or to mobilise the poor to increase their say within the existing state.

✅ **Fact:** the consequence recorded is twofold: a capitalist developmental perspective with an inadequate pro-poor and welfare orientation prevailed over state apparatuses and society; and the democratic space increasingly came to be occupied by casteist and communal tendencies, which hurt the poor even as they mobilised them.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the most distinctive argument in the whole topic and it is a nationalist-left self-criticism rather than a conservative one. Its logic can be stated in one sentence that an examiner will remember: **a state open enough to be shifted in favour of the poor was never actually shifted, because the forces that wanted to shift it refused to work inside it.** Present it as an attributed interpretation, and note the obvious counter-position - that a left operating inside such a state might have been absorbed rather than transformative.

✅ **Fact:** the source adds that simple democratic arithmetic nevertheless secured the poor several concessions, since all political formations must seek their votes; that scattered non-governmental organisations have provided some space for idealistic youth but with limited results in the absence of wider political intervention; and that the Panchayati Raj amendments offer much promise depending on how far progressive forces occupy the democratic space available at the grassroots.

### 6. Competitive populism, and the "strong state" argument

✅ **Fact:** the source identifies a major political development threatening a viable developmental path: the very success of Indian democracy led to growing demands on the state by all classes including the poor, and from the late 1970s all political formations began to indulge in **competitive populism**, distributing largesse through subsidies - on food, fertiliser, diesel, exports and electricity, often reducing consumer costs to zero - which proliferated to unsustainable levels and pushed the country towards default.

✅ **Fact:** the prescription offered is a "strong state", defined carefully as strong **not** in counterposition to democracy, decentralisation and empowerment, but in its capacity to suppress forces operating outside democratic limits - terrorists, separatist insurgencies, fanatical and violent casteist or communal forces; to discipline capital that does not perform competitively, as Japan and other East Asian states did, and equally to discipline sections of labour performing below accepted productivity standards; to guide the economy on a path of rapid modernisation without populism while keeping social justice central; and to participate in globalisation in a way that increases rather than diminishes sovereignty.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice that this is a *developmental-state* argument arrived at from a democratic-nationalist premise, not an authoritarian one, and that it directly answers the puzzle of Lessons 5 and 9: the Indian state was strong enough to plan and too weak to enforce, which is a different failing from being either too interventionist or too passive.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** the post-independence Indian state was a multi-class, popular-democratic and sovereign formation whose class balance was open to change and was never changed, so it delivered growth and democracy while under-delivering on equity.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Blaming the left for the state's failure to redistribute exonerates the governments that actually held power for four decades.

**Reply:** The argument does not exonerate them; it explains a structural gap. Governments legislated - abolition, tenancy statutes, ceilings, untouchability offences, Hindu-law reform - and the legislation failed at the point of local implementation, where the state apparatus was staffed and influenced by the very classes being regulated. Altering that required sustained mobilisation of beneficiaries, which governments in office are structurally poor at conducting against their own supporters. The evidence that mobilisation was the missing variable is positive, not merely inferential: Kerala's conferment of titles with peasant-organisation participation and West Bengal's Operation Barga, both of which achieved more than comparable states with similar statutes.

**Residual:** Whether a left working inside the state would have shifted its class balance or been absorbed by it cannot be determined. The two Indian cases where the left did work inside state institutions produced real implementation gains and also, as Operation Barga's stall showed, ran into objective limits that no political will could remove.

### UPSC application

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this lesson is where a candidate acquires the vocabulary for the highest-value sentences in any question about India's post-independence development. Three formulations are worth internalising: that India attempted industrialisation without the coercion that financed every previous industrialisation; that a free press and popular sovereignty prevented famine and inflationary extraction; and that the state's class balance was open to change and was never changed. Present each as an argued position with its evidence, and pair each with its cost.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Equating government with state; the source's whole analysis depends on separating them.
- Describing post-1947 India as neo-colonial or dependent; foreign capital was never inside the ruling coalition and its share of factory-sector value added was about a tenth in 1981-82.
- Using the democracy-and-development argument as praise without stating its cost in slower redistribution.
- Quoting poverty figures without their dates; the rural ratio moves from 58.75 per cent in 1970-71 to 37.3 per cent in 1993-94.
- Presenting the "strong state" argument as authoritarian; it is explicitly defined against that reading.
- Treating competitive populism as a feature of one party; the source treats it as systemic from the late 1970s.

### Revision notes

1. The national movement was multi-class and popular, producing a sovereign, popular-democratic national state with an open-ended class balance.
2. Feudal landlords and international capital were not contenders inside that state.
3. Government is not the state; the class balance in other apparatuses determines how laws are implemented.
4. The "triple alliance" model of dependent development does not fit India; bargaining occurred between an independent state and foreign capital.
5. Democracy shaped development: no enclosures, collectivisation, land-tax extraction, slavery, union suppression or colonial transfer - Britain drew 85 per cent of its 1801 gross domestic capital formation from colonial transfers.
6. Net transfer of resources was to agriculture rather than from it; a free press with 8,600 dailies and 33,000 periodicals helped avert famine, unlike China's 16-23 million famine deaths in 1959-61.
7. Inflation's trend rate stayed under 8 per cent a year, 1956-90.
8. Poverty: rural 58.75 per cent (1970-71) to 37.3 per cent (1993-94); total 56.25 to 36 per cent; life expectancy 32 to over 63; literacy 18.3 per cent (1951) to 62 per cent (1997); infant mortality 146 to 71.
9. Over three hundred million still below the poverty line and nearly half the population illiterate.
10. The agency problem: Congress became a party of governance; Gandhi's disbandment proposal; Nehru's developmental bureaucracy; the left's refusal to work inside the state.
11. Consequence: a capitalist developmental perspective with weak pro-poor orientation prevailed, and democratic space was occupied by caste and communal mobilisation.
12. Competitive populism from the late 1970s; unsustainable subsidies; the "strong state" prescription defined against authoritarianism.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain the source's claim that India's state was "open-ended" in class terms, and show how that claim explains both the achievements and the failures traced in this session.

**Model answer:** Open-ended means that no class was permanently installed as the ruling class: the state that emerged from a multi-class popular movement was an arena in which capitalists, the upper peasantry, the middle classes, organised workers and the rural and urban poor contended for influence, while feudal landlords had been weakened and then marginalised by land reform and international capital was never inside the coalition at all. The achievements follow from that composition. Because no foreign bourgeoisie sat inside the ruling coalition, bargaining with foreign capital occurred between an independent state and outsiders, which preserved autonomy; because the state rested on popular sovereignty and a free press, development could not be financed by coercive extraction from peasants and workers, so famine was averted and inflation's trend rate stayed below 8 per cent a year from 1956 to 1990; and because equity was a movement legacy, every Plan treated poverty removal as an objective and poverty fell from 58.75 per cent of the rural population in 1970-71 to 37.3 per cent in 1993-94. The failures follow from the same openness. An arena can be shifted only by organised force applied inside it, and the poor were never mobilised to shift it: the Congress became a party of governance, the developmental bureaucracy could not substitute for political work and the left declined to operate within a structure it dismissed as bourgeois. So ceilings failed, tenancy protection failed where beneficiaries were unorganised, and the democratic space was occupied by caste and communal mobilisation instead.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading "open-ended" as neutrality. The state was not neutral in practice, because its apparatuses were staffed and influenced by dominant rural and urban groups; openness describes what could have been changed, not what was.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

"The developmental state in India delivered growth and democracy but under-delivered on equity." Critically examine. (20 marks, 250 words)

#### Model answer

The proposition is well founded on all three counts, and the reason for the third lies in the design of the second.

Growth was delivered. National income grew about 4 per cent a year to the mid-1960s, four times the late colonial rate; savings rose from 5.5 to 10.5 per cent of national income by 1965-66; capital goods output rose tenfold between 1951 and 1969, cutting imported equipment from 43 per cent of fixed investment in equipment in 1960 to 9 per cent in 1974; food self-sufficiency followed the new agricultural strategy.

Democracy was delivered and shaped development. Unlike every earlier industrialisation - enclosures, collectivisation, land tax, slavery, union suppression and colonial transfers that gave Britain 85 per cent of its 1801 capital formation - India could not extract coercively. A free press helped avert famine, against China's 16 to 23 million famine deaths in 1959-61; inflation stayed below 8 per cent a year from 1956 to 1990.

Equity under-delivered. Ceilings released about two per cent of cultivated area; tenancy remained largely oral, with 82 per cent insecure by the 1961 Census; literacy was 62 per cent as late as 1997 and infant mortality 71 per thousand; over three hundred million remained poor.

The explanation is agency. Growth with equity presumed mobilisation from below, but the Congress became a party of governance and the left refused to work inside the state.

The verdict is causal, not merely descriptive: the same democratic constraint that prevented coercive growth also prevented coercive redistribution.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = growth evidence with four dated figures 4 + the democracy-shaped-development argument with the comparative list 5 + equity shortfall quantified across land and human development 5 + the agency explanation stated as the causal core 4 + a verdict that links the second and third findings 2.**

**Bridge to Lesson 17:** If the state's apparatuses determined what its laws achieved, the condition of those apparatuses is the next question - and by the 1970s the word being used for it was not strength but disarray.

## Lesson 17 - Institutions of governance: decay alongside democratic deepening

Progress: 17/18 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: Parliament, Cabinet and the Prime Minister's Office, judiciary, bureaucracy, police, armed forces, federalism and the local tier

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the survey history's chapter on disarray in institutions of governance, read for the crisis-of-governability argument and the institution-by-institution assessment, with the canonical advanced file's summary of the same material
CA search: "Naxalism Left Wing Extremism India 2026 government claims end deadline affected districts"
CA found: Reported - the Union home ministry informed states in 2026 that no district remains classified as affected by left-wing extremism after a deadline of 31 March 2026, with legacy districts retained for monitoring; the reduction from about 126 affected districts in 2013-14 is attributed to a combined security, development and rehabilitation approach, which is the state-capacity question of this lesson in contemporary form
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the paradox stated precisely

```text
                 WHAT DECAYED                          WHAT HELD OR IMPROVED
  PARLIAMENT   proceedings degenerate from the      elections free, regular and decisive
               late 1960s; procedures ignored;      seven central governments have fallen
               Question Hour often suspended;       since 1977 on losing a Lok Sabha majority
               denunciation replaces debate
  CABINET      declining as a policy institution     the judiciary holds its ground:
               since 1969; bypassed by the prime     independence, fundamental rights,
               minister; PMO becomes a parallel      public interest litigation
               executive from Shastri onwards
  BUREAUCRACY  rigid, status-quoist, resistant to    political neutrality survives: Communist
               empowerment of the poor; "functional" governments in West Bengal and Kerala
               partisanship; no accountability       did not complain of sabotage
  POLICE       worst institutional condition;        armed forces: disciplined, professional,
               colonial attitude to the poor;        non-political, heterogeneous by region,
               National Police Commission report     caste and religion, under civilian control
               of 1979 never implemented
  FEDERALISM   Article 356 misused in the 1970s      states retain wide autonomy; Sarkaria
                                                     Commission 1980; misuse later checked
  LOCAL TIER   late-1950s panchayat experiment       the new Panchayati Raj offers a fresh
               downgraded and captured by the        attempt with constitutional backing
               landed elite
```

*Two columns, one period. The mistake to avoid is concluding from the left column that Indian democracy failed; the right column is why it did not.*

### 1. The achievement before the criticism

✅ **Fact:** among the most significant features of India's political development has been the commitment of its leaders to democracy, national unity and economic development, together with their ability to establish the necessary political institutions - of both the state and civil society - and to root them in Indian society. These institutions have been sustained despite rapid social change, with new social groups regularly entering the political arena and asserting their rights, and the repeated peaceful and constitutional succession of governments at the Centre has been a sign of inner strength.

✅ **Fact:** for about twenty-five years before the account was written, however, the political system had been under strain and losing vitality, with a deterioration in political institutions unable to respond adequately to economic development and social change, growing political awakening and rising expectations, the refusal of the oppressed to accept their condition, and growing class and caste conflict for a larger share of power and of the gains of development.

✅ **Fact:** the resulting **"crisis of governability"** takes multiple forms: unstable governments, frequent elections and changing electoral moods, inability to reconcile contending demands, weakening law and order, growing civil discord sometimes reaching insurgency, communal violence, increasing recourse to violent and extra-constitutional agitation, growing corruption and, above all, the failure of governments at the Centre and in the states to implement policies or provide effective governance.

✅ **Fact:** the qualification is immediate and equally important: it would be wrong to suggest that the political system or its institutions have been crumbling or that India has undergone a crisis of the state. The institutions have proved resilient, have managed to function even if inadequately, and have retained legitimacy - partly through longevity and much more through greater popular participation, especially in elections. The source's diagnosis is that besides the skewed socio-economic structure, the major culprit has been the **quality of political leadership**: more than a crisis of the state, there has been a crisis of leadership, functioning without strategic design, ideology or thought-out tactics, relying instead on ad hocism, gimmickry, populism, personal appeal and money, on opportunistic coalitions of disparate parties, on caste and communal coalitions, or on centralisation through coteries.

### 2. Parliament

✅ **Fact:** Nehru worked incessantly to instal respect for Parliament, attending its sittings regularly however busy, treating opposition parties with respect as an integral part of the democratic process and often letting them influence and even change government policies, while the opposition abided by parliamentary rules. The system functioned well in the Nehru and immediate post-Nehru years.

✅ **Fact:** proceedings began to degenerate in the **late 1960s**. Procedures have been routinely ignored; sessions marked by shouting, abuse and rowdy behaviour even towards the prime minister; walk-outs, unruly scenes, demonstrations inside the chambers and *dharnas* have progressively replaced reasoned argument; the Question Hour, once the pride of Parliament, has degenerated and is often suspended; and in recent years Parliament has sometimes been unable to transact business for days.

✅ **Fact:** the pattern also changed. A government with a majority formulates and implements policies irrespective of the opposition's views, and the opposition opposes irrespective of merit; legislatures seldom witness confrontation between well-worked-out alternatives; the worth of a government decision is often tested in the streets and the media rather than in the House. Defections, which became common in the states after 1967 and reached the Centre in the toppling of the Janata government in 1977, were checked by the anti-defection law of 1985, though defections and the break-up of alliances later became common again with defectors remaining within the ambit but not the spirit of the law.

⚠️ **Boundary note.** The design of the Fifty-second Amendment and the Tenth Schedule is taught in the preceding session, and present-day anti-defection doctrine belongs to Polity. What belongs here is the institutional pattern: a legislature that lost deliberative quality while retaining decisive constitutional power.

✅ **Fact:** the offsetting fact is exact and examinable: Parliament has not become ineffectual; a government can hold power only while it retains the confidence of the House, and **since 1977 seven governments at the Centre have fallen because of losing a majority in the Lok Sabha**.

### 3. Cabinet and the Prime Minister's Office

✅ **Fact:** the Cabinet's role and significance as a policy and decision-making institution has been declining **since 1969**, with the beginning of Indira Gandhi's government; it has most often been bypassed and ignored by the prime minister, especially in policy-making, and cabinet ministers owing office to prime ministerial pleasure have accepted this, dissenting at most on minor issues. There has hardly been a reversal of prime ministerial dominance under the weaker political personalities who followed.

✅ **Fact:** two causes are identified. The first is increasing centralisation of power in both government and party in the hands of prime ministers, itself a consequence of ruling parties' reliance on them to win elections. The second is the emergence of the **Prime Minister's Secretariat, the PMO**, as an independent and virtually parallel executive that encroaches on the powers and functions of individual ministries and the Cabinet - gathering information, giving advice, initiating policies including economic and foreign policy, overseeing implementation and taking a hand in appointments and promotions of high officials. Its domineering role, **starting with Shastri and Indira Gandhi**, continued through the Janata period to the BJP-led government headed by Vajpayee.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the assessment given is that concentration of power in one individual is unhealthy, and that a strong Cabinet also enables a multiplicity of interests, regions and cultural zones to share power and participate effectively in decision-making - which is a federal and representational argument, not merely an administrative one.

### 4. Judiciary: the institution that held

✅ **Fact:** one institution has held its ground in all essentials. The high judiciary, especially the Supreme Court, has fully used its right and obligation to enforce and interpret the Constitution, set high standards of independence from the executive and legislature, and been in the forefront of the defence of fundamental rights, enjoying high legitimacy and respect.

✅ **Fact:** the principal criticism is its socially conservative and status-quoist character, which made it insensitive to social issues and movements and led it to stand in the way of radical socio-economic legislation in the name of individual rights - for years interpreting the right to property so as to negate land reforms and bank nationalisation, and tending to ignore the Directive Principles. These rulings were largely rectified through the Constitution's easy amendment procedure, used repeatedly by Nehru and Indira Gandhi "to bend the stick the other way".

✅ **Fact:** in later years the Court became more sensitive to social issues from the rights of women, workers and minorities to ecology, human rights, social justice and discrimination, with **public interest litigation** - under which even a postcard from a victimised citizen to the Chief Justice is treated as a writ petition - as the emblem of its activism; this has not given the poor easy access to higher courts but has opened a window previously shut. The two most negative features identified are **inordinate delay**, with High Court backlogs amounting to several lakhs of cases, and the **high cost** of justice, which limits access to the well-off. In recent years the Court has also been accused of "judicial despotism" for arrogating powers vested in the executive or legislature.

✅ **Fact:** two other constitutional institutions are assessed positively. Presidents have functioned with dignity, without controversy and within widely accepted interpretations of their powers; and Election Commissions have on the whole fulfilled with credit their obligation to hold free and fair elections involving millions of voters, nearly a million polling booths and thousands of candidates.

### 5. Bureaucracy and police: the core of the crisis

✅ **Fact:** the most important institutional crisis is the quality of public administration and the bureaucracy, and its deterioration "lies at the core of the 'crisis of governability'", including the breakdown of law and order and growth of crime in several states and large cities, because even the best legislation and policy is nullified during implementation.

✅ **Fact:** the bureaucracy is characterised as rigid, basically conservative, pro-status quo and resistant to social change, especially the empowerment of the poor or redistributive measures, favouring dominant social groups and influential persons particularly in rural areas; with a non-performance character and file-pushing procedures, unsuited to economic development or to involving people in it; and with hardly any mechanism for enforcing discipline, punishing inefficiency, checking corruption or rewarding merit and honesty. Its worst feature appears in dealings with common people, where government servants and especially policemen are generally discourteous, domineering, unhelpful, corrupt, inefficient and arbitrary, with accountability to the citizen not even raisable - which the source connects to anti-incumbency voting, as people use their votes in a desperate quest for a friendly, honest and minimally efficient administration.

✅ **Fact:** two qualifications are recorded. The tradition of **political neutrality** still holds, with bureaucrats implementing the policies of the government in power irrespective of their own opinions - and the source notes that Communist governments in West Bengal and Kerala did not complain of the higher bureaucracy obstructing or sabotaging their policies on political, ideological or class grounds.
