**Prompt as commonly written:** "Ceiling laws achieved nothing."

**Correct formulation:** "Ceiling laws released about two per cent of cultivated area - 2.4 million acres declared surplus by 1970 and about five million acres distributed to some 4.7 million beneficiaries by mid-1992, of whom over half were Scheduled Caste or Scheduled Tribe by 1985 - but they also discouraged accumulation beyond the ceiling and so prevented the dispossession of small and marginal holders that a competitive land market would probably have produced."

**Why the error fails:** it judges a policy solely against its stated objective and misses a structural effect that explains why India did not experience the consolidation of holdings that accompanied commercialisation elsewhere.

### Drill 3 - the Green Revolution's distributive verdict

**Prompt as commonly written:** "The Green Revolution widened inequality."

**Correct formulation:** "The output verdict is settled and the distributive verdict is not. Production rose 35 per cent between 1967-68 and 1970-71 and imports fell from 10.3 to 3.6 million tonnes between 1966 and 1970. On distribution, one position documents diffusion across three phases and broad scale-neutrality, citing Raj Krishna's 1979 finding that farmers with five acres or less used 32 per cent of fertiliser and 33 per cent of credit on 21 per cent of area; the other documents unequal access to irrigation and credit, regional lag and ecological cost. Both agree that insecure tenants lost as rents rose towards 70 per cent of gross produce in parts of Punjab, and that relative inequality increased while the poor gained absolutely."

**Why the error fails:** it states a contested conclusion as settled and, in examination answers, frequently attributes the inequality thesis to a source that argues the opposite - a misattribution an examiner can detect.

### Drill 4 - the cause of 1991

**Prompt as commonly written:** "The 1991 crisis was caused by the Gulf War and by liberalisation."

**Correct formulation:** "The Gulf crisis of August 1990 was the trigger; the cause was a decade of borrowing to finance a public investment-savings gap that reached about 9 per cent of GDP by 1989-90, with the consolidated fiscal deficit rising from 4.1 per cent in 1974-75 to 10.4 per cent in 1991, external debt from $23.5 billion in 1980-81 to $83.8 billion in 1990-91, the concessional share falling from over 80 per cent to about 40 per cent and the debt-service ratio rising from 10.2 to 35 per cent. Import liberalisation is ruled out, because the trade balance improved in the late 1980s with exports growing about 14 per cent a year in dollar terms."

**Why the error fails:** a shock of a few months cannot explain a decade-long widening of a structural gap, and the trade data contradict the liberalisation claim directly.

### Drill 5 - reform as correction

**Prompt as commonly written:** "In 1991 India abandoned the Nehruvian model and self-reliance."

**Correct formulation:** "In 1991 the instruments changed and the goals did not. Growth, self-reliance and poverty removal remained the declared objectives, while licensing was dismantled, the MRTP Act abolished, the exchange rate linked to the market with about a 20 per cent devaluation, trade and financial markets opened and foreign direct investment welcomed. Self-reliance was redefined from insulation to competitiveness - a change made possible because insulation had already delivered its purpose, with imported equipment down to 9 per cent of fixed investment in equipment by 1974 and foreign firms accounting for about a tenth of factory-sector value added in 1981-82."

**Why the error fails:** it confuses a change of means with a change of ends, and ignores that the instruments abandoned had already achieved what they were built for.

### Drill 6 - the two families of agrarian movement

**Prompt as commonly written:** "Peasant movements continued after independence, from Telangana to the farmers' agitations of the 1980s."

**Correct formulation:** "Two families of movement with opposed class content followed one another. Telangana, the PEPSU muzaras, Naxalbari and Srikakulam mobilised the landless, tenants, sharecroppers and tribals against landlords, jagirdars, deshmukhs, biswedars and jotedars for land, security and the end of forced labour. The movements led from Nasik on 10 November 1980 and Sisauli in 1986 mobilised surplus-producing owner-cultivators against the state for higher output prices, cheaper power and debt relief. Their attempt at a joint rally at the Boat Club on 2 October 1989 collapsed publicly."

**Why the error fails:** it implies a continuous agrarian struggle when the later movements were conducted largely by beneficiaries of the earlier reforms, whose interest in high food prices is opposed to the landless labourer's.

### Drill 7 - communalism as ideology

**Prompt as commonly written:** "Communal riots increased after the 1960s because of growing religious intolerance."

**Correct formulation:** "Communalism is a belief system - that religious communities have divergent and hostile interests because of religious difference - and violence is its symptom. Its intensity tracked organisational strength and political opportunity: riot deaths rose from 7 in 1958 to 108 in 1961; riots numbered 1,070 in 1964 as the Jan Sangh grew from 14 seats in 1962 to 35 in 1967; violence fell during the Emergency when organisers were jailed and rose in the Janata period when the same organisations gained influence; and it became endemic from 1978."

**Why the error fails:** it explains a political phenomenon by a religious one, and cannot account for the sharp fall and rise of violence within four years in the same society.

### Drill 8 - the graded verdict

**Prompt as commonly written:** "India's post-independence record was mixed, with both achievements and failures."

**Correct formulation:** "India transformed its economy substantially, its politics profoundly and its social hierarchies least of all; the state acted decisively wherever the losing party was politically weak - against intermediaries, against colonial economic dependence, against famine - and hesitated wherever the loser was its own base, which is why ceilings, tenancy enforcement and local delivery failed. The third shortfall is why the first two produced movements rather than contentment."

**Why the error fails:** "mixed" states no ruling and can be written without knowing anything about the period; the corrected version is a thesis with predictive content that the body of the answer can support.

# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

## Master chronology

```text
1946   All India Kisan Sabha demands a 25-acre ceiling
       Kaira District Cooperative Milk Producers' Union registered (December)
       Telangana movement gathers; Tebhaga in Bengal
1947   AICC Economic Programme Committee under Nehru proposes transfer after five years
       AICC committee first proposes a ceiling on holdings (November)
1948   Industrial Policy Resolution; Atomic Energy Commission
       Indian Army enters Hyderabad (13 September); PEPSU formed (July)
1949   Zamindari abolition bills introduced in several provinces
       Kumarappa Committee report (July); Jagirdari Abolition Regulation, Hyderabad
       Idols installed in the Ayodhya structure (December); structure locked
1950   Planning Commission established (15 March)
       Hyderabad Tenancy and Agricultural Lands Act; Constitution in force
1951   First Plan begins; IDRA enacted; First Amendment
       Bhoodan's first donation at Pochampalli (18 April)
1952   PEPSU Tenancy (Temporary Provision) Act (January)
       Communal parties poll poorly in the first general election
1953   PEPSU Occupancy Tenants (Vesting of Proprietary Rights) Act
       JP joins the Bhoodan movement; AICC at Agra urges ceiling action
1954   Parliament accepts the "socialist pattern of society" (December)
       RBI recommends crop loans; NFIW formed
1955   Avadi session; Fourth Amendment; Untouchability (Offences) Act
       Hindu Marriage Act; Amul brand adopted; Gramdan begins (end of year)
       Jammu and Kashmir completes ceiling distribution (by mid-1955)
1956   IPR 1956; Second Plan; zamindari abolition essentially complete
       Hindu Succession, Hindu Minority and Guardianship, Hindu Adoptions Acts
       PL-480 imports begin; Ambedkar's conversion and death
1957   NDC standing committee sets ceiling deadlines; Republican Party founded
1958   Ranga's letter signed by 100 Congress MPs (December)
1959   Nagpur Congress (January): ceilings and cooperative joint farming
       Ranga resigns (February); Nehru's assurance; Tibet (March)
       Swatantra Party founded (June); AICC cooperative training abandoned
1960-61 Most states pass ceiling legislation by end-1961
       Jabalpur riot (1961); National Integration Council
1962   China war; Jan Sangh holds 14 seats
1964   NDC Land Reform Implementation Committee (June); Shastri writes to chief ministers
       on dairying; 1,070 communal riots
1965   Agricultural Prices Commission; NDDB created; war and drought
1966   Devaluation; food imports exceed ten million tonnes; three annual Plans begin
1967   Congress setback; Jan Sangh at 35 seats; Naxalbari (March-July)
       Srikakulam armed phase begins (November)
1968   Bharatiya Khet Mazdoor Union founded at Moga
1969   Bank nationalisation; MRTP Act; Congress split; CPI(ML) formed
       RBI records tenants and labourers receiving 4-6 per cent of credit
1970   Land-grab movement and about 20,000 arrests; Indira Gandhi's ceiling conference (September)
       West Bengal extends limited protection to bargadars (July)
1971   Central Land Reforms Committee recommendations (August); Bangladesh war
1972   Chief ministers' conference approves new ceiling guidelines (July)
       Dalit Panthers founded; insurance nationalised; Shahada movement
1973   FERA; coal nationalisation; P.S. Appu Task Force report
       Anti-price-rise movement begins
1974   Thirty-fourth Amendment places ceiling laws in the Ninth Schedule (August)
       Chipko movement; GCMMF formed at Anand
1975-77 Emergency: communal violence and propaganda fall sharply
1977   Janata government; Left Front takes office in West Bengal (June)
       Chattisgarh Mines Shramik Sangh
1978   Operation Barga launched (July); communalism becomes endemic
       Anti-Dalit riots in rural Maharashtra
1979   National Police Commission report; Bodh Gaya land-in-women's-names demand
       The budget described as the watershed of fiscal relaxation
1980   Nasik roko agitation (10 November); Sarkaria Commission appointed
1983   VHP campaign on the Ayodhya issue begins
1984   Dowry Prohibition Act amendments; Bhopal gas disaster
1985   March 1985 ceiling position: 7.2 million acres surplus, 4.3 million distributed
       Shah Bano judgment
1986   Ayodhya structure reopened by court order (1 February); Sisauli agitation
       Samagra Mahila Aghadi session (November)
1988   National Perspective Plan for Women
1989   Panchayati Raj bill introduced; Boat Club rally failure (2 October)
1990   National Front loan waiver; Mandal implementation and agitation
1991   Balance-of-payments crisis; gold sale (March); reserves at two weeks' cover (July)
       Rao government and the reform programme begin (June)
1992   Mid-1992 ceiling position: 7.3 million acres surplus, about 5 million distributed
1993   Panchayati Raj amendments enacted with one-third reservation for women
1995   Operation Flood reaches 69,875 societies and 8.9 million members
1997   Fifth Pay Commission acceptance; over 800 reported custodial deaths
1999   Reserves at $30.4 billion (January); Bihar dissolves its State Bhoodan Committee (June)
```

## Master comparison: the four land-reform components

| Component | Target class | Political weight of the target | Instrument | Outcome | Decisive evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Abolition of intermediaries | Zamindars, jagirdars | Weak; isolated during the national movement | State Acts plus First and Fourth Amendments | Largely succeeded by 1956 | About 20 million tenants became owners; Rs 1,642 million of Rs 6,700 million paid by 1961 |
| Tenancy reform | Resuming landowners, including new owner-cultivators | Strong locally; embedded in revenue administration | State tenancy Acts with floors and ceilings on resumption | Partly succeeded for the covered; failed on coverage | 1961 Census: 82 per cent of tenancies insecure; Hyderabad cohort: 45.4 per cent protected, 12.4 per cent owners |
| Ceilings | Holders above the limit, and by extension all substantial cultivators | Strong enough to form a political party | State ceiling Acts; 1972 guidelines; Ninth Schedule 1974 | Largely failed at transfer; succeeded at prevention | About 2 per cent of cultivated area distributed; 32.25 million acres wilfully dispersed |
| Bhoodan and Gramdan | Donors, voluntarily | Not coerced at all | Movement, padayatra, trusteeship | Could not substitute | Over 4 million acres donated by March 1956; about 872,000 acres distributed by early 1961 |

## Master comparison: cooperation by type

| Type | What the member surrenders | Verification | Outcome | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Joint farming | Managerial control of land | None available to the member | Failed | Bogus societies evading land laws; unsuccessful state pilot farms |
| Service and credit | Nothing; a transaction is pooled | Loan received, input supplied | Substantial success with elite capture | Credit share 3.3 per cent (1951-52) to nearly 30 per cent (1981); landless still about 4 per cent |
| Dairy on the Anand pattern | Nothing; milk supplied daily | Daily payment against daily supply | Outstanding success | Milk output growth 0.7 to over 4 per cent a year; 8.9 million members by 1995 |

## Causal chain: from design to crisis to correction

```text
capital-scarce economy + democratic commitment + export pessimism
        -> heavy and capital-goods industry in the public sector
        -> licensing and controls needed to direct private investment (IDRA 1951)
        -> 1956-57 exchange crisis adds import and foreign-exchange controls
        -> growth ~4% and a capital-goods base to the mid-1960s
        -> agriculture lags; PL-480 dependence from 1956
        -> 1965-66 droughts + wars + aid leverage
        -> devaluation judged a failure; economic nationalism deepens controls
        -> food security achieved by technology (1967 onwards)
        -> efficiency falls: ICOR up, exports stagnate, PSU returns ~2.5%
        -> fiscal relaxation from the late 1970s; competitive populism
        -> borrowing shifts from concessional to commercial
        -> August 1990 shock -> reserves collapse -> July 1991 at the edge of default
        -> instruments changed, goals retained
```

## Causal chain: from abolition to the farmers' movements

```text
abolition removes intermediaries
   -> about 20 million occupancy tenants become owners
   -> the new owner class is politically organised and locally dominant
        -> tenancy enforcement fails where it must act against them
        -> ceilings are delayed 14 years and then evaded
   -> redistribution stalls; area expansion nears its limit
   -> technology is substituted: HYV package from the mid-1960s
        -> output and marketed surplus rise; food sovereignty achieved
        -> the same owner class becomes a surplus-producing political class
             -> demands shift from LAND to PRICES, POWER TARIFFS and DEBT RELIEF
             -> Nasik 1980, Sisauli 1986
             -> lower ceilings become politically impossible
```

## Argument map: why the state succeeded and failed in the same decades

```text
CLAIM      the state acted decisively where the losing party was politically weak
             and hesitated where the loser was its own base
EVIDENCE 1 abolition succeeded; ceilings did not - same state, same decade
EVIDENCE 2 Jammu and Kashmir implemented ceilings fully by mid-1955
EVIDENCE 3 Kerala and West Bengal outperformed on tenancy with the same statutes
EVIDENCE 4 1991 reform withdrew rules easily and withdrew benefits not at all
EVIDENCE 5 legal equality for caste and gender was granted early; enforcement lagged
MECHANISM  laws are executed by local apparatuses staffed and influenced by the
             classes being regulated; the remedy is organisation of beneficiaries
LIMIT      objective structure can bind even where will exists - Operation Barga
             stalled because residual landlords were themselves small cultivators
VERDICT    a state strong enough to plan and too weak to redistribute
```

## Argument map: the Green Revolution debate

```text
SETTLED    output, yields, marketed surplus, the end of import dependence,
           and three documented phases of regional diffusion
CONTESTED  the distributive verdict
  POSITION A  diffusion and scale-neutrality
              Raj Krishna 1979 input-intensity data; labour demand in Punjab;
              small holdings' share of holdings and area stable or rising
  POSITION B  unequal access to irrigation and credit; regional lag;
              labour displacement in some settings; ecological cost
  CONCEDED BY BOTH  insecure tenants lost; rents rose towards 70 per cent in parts of
              Punjab; relative inequality increased while the poor gained absolutely;
              groundwater, fertiliser and pesticide damage and yield plateaus are real
ANSWER RULE state the settled findings as fact, present both distributive positions
            with their evidence, record the conceded points, return a graded verdict
```

## Answer-writing spine for this topic

```text
1  Decode the directive: assess / examine / trace / comment / critically examine
2  Identify the strand or strands: economy | land and agriculture | society | state
3  Open with a thesis, not a description
4  Disaggregate before judging - always, in this topic
5  Attach dates and denominators to every figure
6  Name contested numbers as contested (tenancy area, poverty line, distribution)
7  Supply at least one regional contrast
8  Present criticism with its strongest reply
9  Mark the boundary where another subject owns the material
10 Close with a graded verdict that follows from the body
```

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## 1. The synthesis frame

- Four strands: the economic model; land and agriculture; the social order; state capacity.
- Organising thesis: the state acted decisively where the losing class was politically weak and hesitated where the loser was its own base.
- Boundaries: chronology to the earlier sessions of this period; present-day policy to Economy, Indian Society, Social Justice, Governance and Polity.
- Evidence discipline: several central numbers here are contested or unreliable at source - tenancy area, the Green Revolution's distributive effect, the poverty ratio - and must be quoted with their attribution and their warning.

## 2. The planned mixed economy: design

- Nationalist roots of state direction: Ranade and Naoroji; Karachi Resolution 1931; N.R. Sarkar for FICCI in 1934; National Planning Committee 1938 with twenty-nine volumes; Bombay Plan 1945 locating dependence in the absence of capital-goods industry.
- Advantages at independence: Indian entrepreneurs holding about 75 per cent of the industrial-goods market and dominance in banking and insurance; large conglomerates; a broad consensus on self-reliance, import substitution, prevention of foreign domination, land reform, growth with equity, positive discrimination and a central state role.
- The defining commitment: industrial transformation inside democracy and civil liberties, unattempted elsewhere.
- Sequence: IPR 1948 deferring nationalisation ten years; Planning Commission 15 March 1950; First Plan 1951-56 as consolidation; "socialist pattern" accepted by Parliament December 1954 and elaborated at Avadi 1955; IPR 1956 and Second Plan 1956-61 expanding the public sector without nationalising existing industry; Third Plan 1961-66; three public-sector steel plants inside the Second Plan.
- Mahalanobis logic: invest -> import or build machines -> export pessimism -> build capital goods at home in the public sector; consumer goods and employment from small and cottage industry, community projects and cooperatives.
- Control regime: IDRA 1951; the 1956-57 exchange crisis adding import and foreign-exchange controls; the bureaucracy-politician nexus and rent-earning business resisting dismantling.

## 3. Achievements to the mid-1960s

- Growth about 4 per cent a year, 1951 to 1964-65 - four times the late colonial rate; comparable with Japan's early decades on K.N. Raj's comparison.
- Savings and investment 5.5 per cent of national income (1950-51) to 10.5 and 14 per cent (1965-66); sterling balances of about Rs 16 billion helping bridge the gap; investment nearly five times the 1951-52 level nominally and over three times in real terms.
- Industry 7.1 per cent a year, 1951-65; Plan industrial expenditure 70 per cent then 80 per cent on metals, machinery and chemicals; 1951-69 capital goods up tenfold, intermediates quadrupled, consumer goods up 70 per cent.
- Machine tools 89.8 per cent imported in 1950; imported equipment 43 per cent of fixed investment in equipment in 1960 and 9 per cent in 1974.
- Public sector's share of corporate paid-up capital: 3.4 per cent (1951), 30 per cent (1961), about 50 per cent (early 1970s), 75 per cent (1978); not in partnership with foreign capital.
- Aid: 0.4 per cent of net national product in the First Plan, 2.25 and 3.17 per cent in the Second and Third; 6:1 aid to private investment, 1948-61; over 71 per cent of First Plan aid for wheat, about 98 per cent of Second and Third Plan aid for industry, transport, communication and power.
- Infrastructure and science: Plan expenditure about 26 per cent on transport and communication, 19.9 per cent on social services, 10.6 per cent on power; 1950-51 to 1965-66 electricity capacity 4.5 times, villages electrified 14 times, hospital beds 2.5 times, school enrolment nearly 3 times, technical-education capacity 6 and 8.5 times, against population growth of a little over one-third; Atomic Energy Commission 1948; CSIR laboratories; R&D Rs 10 million (1949) to Rs 4.5 billion (1977); technical manpower 190,000 to 2.32 million.
- Shortfalls: agriculture over 3 per cent a year yet short of demand, forcing PL-480 from 1956; employment assumptions failed; literacy lagged; efficiency costs already accumulating.

## 4. Crisis, inward turn and achievement, 1965-1980

- 1965-66 droughts: output down 17 per cent, food output down 20 per cent; inflation 12 per cent a year 1965-68, food prices near 20 per cent; fiscal deficit 7.3 per cent of GDP in 1966-67; reserves about $340 million.
- Aid suspended after the 1965 war; PL-480 renewal withheld; conditions were liberalisation, devaluation and a new agricultural strategy.
- Devaluation nominally 36.5 per cent, effectively much less; condemned amid a second drought before effects could appear; three annual Plans 1966-69; Fourth Plan from April 1969.
- Adjustment fell on capital expenditure, down about 50 per cent in real terms 1966-67 to 1970-71; industrial growth 7.8 per cent (1951-66) to 4.99 per cent (1966-74).
- Economic nationalism: bank nationalisation and MRTP 1969; insurance 1972; coal 1973; FERA 1973; failed wheat-trade takeover 1973; sick-unit takeovers from the mid-1970s.
- Achievements: foodgrain output up 35 per cent (1967-68 to 1970-71); imports 10.3 to 3.6 million tonnes (1966-70); availability to 128.8 million tonnes (1984); stocks over 30 million tonnes; deficit 3.8 per cent of GDP (1969-70); reserves about $7.3 billion (1978-79); aid down to 0.35 per cent of net national product (1972-73); debt service 10.2 per cent (1980-81); oil self-sufficiency target of 29 million tonnes met by the Sixth Plan; bank branches 8,262 (1969) to 60,601 (1992), over 60 per cent of expansion rural.
- Foreign capital marginal: about 10 per cent of factory-sector value added (1981-82); 86.5 per cent of technology agreements without equity (1977-80); foreign banks 8.9 per cent of deposits (1970).
- Droughts of 1987-88 absorbed without a rise in rural poverty - a first since independence.

## 5. The road to 1991

- Inefficiency: licensing, MRTP, small-scale reservation, no exit, man-days lost index 100 (1961) to 891.6 (1980); ICOR 2.0 (First Plan) to 3.6 (Third) to about 5.76 (1971-76), near 4 in the 1990s; public-sector return about 2.5 per cent in the 1980s, far lower excluding 14 petroleum enterprises holding 77 per cent of 1989-90 profits.
- Missed opening: world trade in manufactures up tenfold against output up fourfold; Third World share of industrial exports 5 per cent (1970) to double by 1983; India's share of world exports 2.4 per cent (1948) to 0.42 per cent (1980) to 0.6 per cent (1994); manufactured exports half China's, a third of Brazil's and a quarter of South Korea's in 1980-81; Korea's OECD manufactured exports $41 billion against India's $9 billion in 1990.
- Fiscal drift: relaxation from about 1975, the 1979 budget as watershed; food subsidies doubled 1975-77; fertiliser subsidy tenfold 1976-80; 1990 loan waiver over Rs 100 billion; deficits 4.1 (1974-75), 6.5 (1979-80), 9.7 (1984-85) and 10.4 per cent of GDP (1991); public investment-savings gap 3.6 to 9 per cent of GDP; domestic debt 31.8 to 54.6 per cent of GDP.
- 1980s growth about 6 per cent, but debt-led and spending-led; current account from a $1.5 billion surplus (1977-78) to a $9.9 billion deficit (1990-91); external debt $23.5 billion to $83.8 billion; concessional share above 80 per cent to about 40 per cent; debt service 10.2 to 35 per cent.
- Crisis: reserves $2.24 billion (1990-91); Kuwait invasion August 1990; rating downgrade and NRI withdrawals; 20 tonnes of gold sold March 1991; about two weeks' cover by July 1991.

## 6. Reform since 1991

- Rao minority government from June 1991 with Manmohan Singh; antecedents in the mid-1960s attempt, 1970s "reform by stealth", Indira Gandhi's deregulation after 1980 and Rajiv Gandhi's partial measures abandoned in the political crisis.
- Package: fiscal correction; market-linked exchange rate with about 20 per cent devaluation; trade liberalisation; delicensing; abolition of MRTP; public-sector reform; capital-market and financial reform; opening to foreign investment.
- Results: GDP 0.8 per cent (1991-92) to 5.3, 6.2 and over 7.5 per cent average; Eighth Plan 6.94 per cent against the Seventh's 6; savings over 23 per cent (1991-97); industrial production 12.8 per cent (1995-96); capital goods about 25 per cent (1994-95); central fiscal deficit 8.3 per cent (1990-91) to about 6 per cent average, primary deficit 4.3 to 0.6 per cent; exports about 20 per cent a year (1993-96); current-account deficit 3.2 to 0.4 per cent and then 1.6 per cent; reserves $30.4 billion (January 1999); external debt 41 to 28.7 per cent of GDP; debt service 35.3 to 19.5 per cent; FDI $129 million to $2.1 billion.
- Poverty: 51.3 per cent (1977-78), 38.9 per cent (1987-88), about 36 per cent (1993-94); the 1992-93 rise attributed mainly to the 1991-92 drought; social-service and rural-development spending from 7.8 to nearly 10 per cent of central expenditure; real agricultural wages recovering past pre-reform levels by 1993-94; additional employment about 6.3 million a year (1992-95) against 4.8 million in the 1980s; inflation 17 per cent (August 1991) to under 5 per cent (February 1996).
- Unfinished: public savings; subsidies rising (food Rs 28.5 billion to Rs 61.14 billion; fertiliser Rs 32.01 billion to Rs 62.35 billion); state utilities; labour reform and exit.
- Slowdown from 1997; Fifth Pay Commission selective acceptance; East Asian crisis; post-1998 sanctions.
- Framing: correction of excessive dirigisme, not repudiation - goals retained, instruments changed; a cross-party consensus reminiscent of the Nehruvian one.

## 7. Land reform, component by component

- Two phases: institutional to the early 1960s; technological from the mid- or late 1960s; overlapping.
- Abolition: bills from 1949 on the U.P. committee model under G.B. Pant; compensation non-justiciable; First Amendment 1951 and Fourth 1955 after judicial obstruction including in Patna; Acts in most provinces by 1956; about twenty million tenants became owners; compensation inversely graded - about twenty times net annual income for the smallest, two to four times for the largest - and paid over up to forty years; Kashmir paid none; Rs 1,642 million of Rs 6,700 million paid by 1961; the "personal cultivation" loophole against the Kumarappa Committee's 1949 stipulation; mass eviction of small tenants; beneficiaries were occupancy and upper tenants.
- Tenancy: three objectives - security, fair rent of one-fourth to one-sixth of gross produce, ownership purchase at a multiple of annual rent; resumption capped at three times the family holding in the First Plan; floors of half the holding in several states, five acres in Bihar and two and a half in West Bengal; small-owner protection captured by large owners, recorded by P.S. Appu's 1973 Task Force and the Third Plan; Bombay protected tenants 1.7 to 1.3 million (1948-51); Hyderabad down about 57 per cent (1951-55); Hyderabad cohort by 1954 - 45.4 per cent protected, 12.4 per cent owners, 2.6 per cent legally evicted, 22.1 per cent illegally, 17.5 per cent "voluntary"; Fourth Plan recommendation on surrenders; concealment as "farm servants" and conversion to sharecropping; West Bengal bargadars protected only from July 1970; 1961 Census 82 per cent insecure; 1971 Census claims of 91.1 per cent owner-operation and Bihar at 99.6 per cent against 36.65 per cent in 1961; Charan Singh's record drive; Kerala's late-1960s conferment; Operation Barga from July 1978, 0.4 million recorded before, about 1.4 million by November 1990 of an estimated 2.4 million, crop division 1:3, orientation camps, stalled by small residual holdings and bargadar rotation; market rents about 50 per cent and up to 70 per cent in parts of Punjab against legal 20-25 per cent; ownership purchase about 0.77 million of 1.3 million in Gujarat and about 1.1 million of 2.6 million in Maharashtra; Thorner's 1968 judgement.
- Ceilings: 1946 Kisan Sabha demand; November 1947 AICC committee; Kumarappa Committee July 1949; First Plan deferral; Nagpur January 1959; Ranga's resignation and the Swatantra Party June 1959; legislation only by end-1961; defects - high limits, individual unit, escalators to 140 per cent and unlimited in Andhra Pradesh, exemptions including "efficiently managed farms" and reportedly 26 categories in Tamil Nadu, and delay enabling dispersal; by 1970 no acre declared surplus in Bihar, Mysore, Kerala, Orissa and Rajasthan, 1,400 acres declared in Andhra Pradesh, Jammu and Kashmir alone fully implemented with about 230,000 acres by mid-1955 without compensation, 2.4 million acres nationally or about 0.3 per cent of cultivated land; second wave - NDC committee June 1964, land-grab movement 1970 with about 20,000 arrests, Indira Gandhi's September 1970 conference, Central Land Reforms Committee August 1971, July 1972 guidelines at 10-18, 27 and 54 acres with a family-of-five unit and SC/ST priority, Thirty-fourth Amendment August 1974 and the Ninth Schedule; by March 1985, 7.2 million acres surplus and 4.3 million distributed to about 3.3 million beneficiaries, 54.6 per cent SC/ST receiving 43.6 per cent of area; by mid-1992, 7.3 million surplus, about 5 million distributed, about 4.7 million beneficiaries, about 2 per cent of cultivated area, with 32.25 million acres wilfully dispersed and 1.6 million acres under litigation; West Bengal under 3 per cent of cultivated area supplying about a quarter of all surplus; Hanumantha Rao on prevention of concentration; by 1976-77 about 97 per cent of holdings below 25 acres and 87 per cent below 10.
- Bhoodan and Gramdan: Vinoba Bhave; Sarvodaya Samaj; target 50 million of 300 million acres; first donation 18 April 1951 at Pochampalli; 200 villages and 12,200 acres within three months; over 4 million acres by March 1956; about 654,000 acres to 200,000 families by end-1957 and about 872,000 acres by early 1961; Gramdan from end-1955 with over 4,500 villages by end-1960, 1,946 in Orissa; JP joined 1953; Bihar dissolved its State Bhoodan Committee June 1999; assessed as a movement-based attempt with unrealised trusteeship potential and a moral ambience acknowledged even by critics.

## 8. Cooperation

- Joint farming: Kumarappa Committee compulsion proposal; Second Plan target; China delegations of mid-1956 on a false comparison, China's 1954-74 agricultural growth being 2 per cent against India's 2.5; Nagpur January 1959; opposition from Rajagopalachari, Ranga and Charan Singh; Nehru's February 1959 assurance; Tibet and the border ending the China model; service-cooperative target implying 6,000 societies a month; AICC training abandoned June 1959; Third Plan's ten pilot projects per district; Thorner's survey of 117 leading cooperatives, December 1958 to May 1959, finding bogus societies and unsuccessful state pilot farms.
- Service cooperatives: elite capture and bureaucratisation; RBI's 1954 crop-loan recommendation refused; RBI 1969 recording tenants and labourers at 4-6 per cent of credit; PACS lending Rs 23 crore (1951-52), nearly Rs 200 crore through 212,000 societies (1960-61), about Rs 4,900 crore (1992-93); cooperative share of cultivator credit 3.3 per cent to nearly 30 per cent by 1981; non-institutional sources from 92.7 per cent (1951) to under 37 per cent (1981); overdues 20 to 38 per cent (1960-70), 45 per cent by the mid-1970s, Bihar 77 per cent; the 1990 write-off up to Rs 10,000; Ladejinsky's 1972 judgement.
- Dairy: Kaira union registered December 1946 at 250 litres a day; Tribhuvandas Patel chairman from January 1947 for over twenty-five years; Kurien chief executive 1950-73; Amul brand and the milk-powder plant 1955; cheese and baby-food plant 1960; cattle-feed plant 1964; GCMMF 1974; IRMA; by 2000, 1,015 societies and 574,000 members handling nearly a million litres a day with turnover about Rs 487 crore; NDDB created 1965 at Shastri's initiative and located at Anand; Operation Flood reaching 69,875 societies in 170 milksheds with 8.9 million members by 1995, about 6.3 per cent of production and 22 per cent of marketed milk; milk-output growth from 0.7 to over 4 per cent a year; payments Rs 2.1 billion (1972) to Rs 34 billion (1995) in constant rupees; about 60 per cent of beneficiaries marginal, small or landless; about one-third of producers landless; about 6,000 women's societies; self-selection as the transferable lesson; spread to fruit, vegetables, oilseeds, salt and trees, with the Dhara brand.
- Institutional-phase overview: land reform achieved within democracy without collectivisation, expropriation or occupation; semi-feudal landlordism and illegal cesses largely ended; land revenue's real burden fell from often half the net income from agriculture to below one per cent; institutional credit Rs 0.23 billion (1950-51) to Rs 7.75 billion (1972-73); Thorner's judgement that more change occurred in twenty-one years than in the preceding two hundred; and his verdict that socialism in the countryside was no more achieved than the British achieved capitalism.

## 9. The Green Revolution

- Nehru-era foundations: 31 per cent of First Plan outlay and 20-24 per cent thereafter on agriculture and irrigation; Bhakra-Nangal, agricultural universities, research laboratories and fertiliser plants; IADP in fifteen districts during the Third Plan; G.S. Bhalla's formulation on foundations.
- Conjuncture: population growth about 2.2 per cent; stagnating yields; PL-480 from 1956 at nearly three million tonnes rising past four and a half million by 1963; wars of 1962 and 1965; droughts of 1965-66; imports over ten million tonnes in 1966; forced procurement rejected as incompatible with democracy; Shastri, C. Subramaniam and Indira Gandhi; the Bell Mission "leaning on open doors".
- Package: HYV seeds including Mexican dwarf wheat; fertiliser, pesticides, machinery, soil testing, education and credit; about 32 million acres, roughly 10 per cent of cultivated area; Agricultural Prices Commission 1965; institutional finance doubled 1968-73; irrigated-area growth from about 1 to about 2.5 million hectares a year; pump sets 421,000 to 2.4 million; tube wells 90,000 to 460,000; tractors 31,000 to 140,000; fertiliser 306,000 to 2,350,000 tonnes.
- Output: foodgrain up 35 per cent (1967-68 to 1970-71); food production 89 to 112 million tonnes (1964-65 to 1971-72), about 10 per cent per capita; imports 10.3 to 3.6 million tonnes; availability 73.5 to 99.5 and then 110.25 and 128.8 million tonnes; stocks over 30 million tonnes and exports by the 1980s; an estimated 8-10 million tonnes of wheat imports avoided annually at $600-800 million.
- Source of growth: area 51 per cent and yield 49 per cent (1949-50 to 1964-65); yield about 80 per cent (1967-68 to 1989-90); the marketed surplus as the decisive political outcome.
- Diffusion: phase one 2.08 per cent with Punjab at 6.63; phase two 2.38 per cent with rice spreading south and east; phase three 3.4 per cent with West Bengal at 5.39 and the south overtaking the north-west; inter-state variation falling.
- Distribution: Raj Krishna 1979 on input intensity; 98 per cent of holdings under 25 acres in 1980-81 on 77.2 per cent of area; Ladejinsky February 1969 on labour demand; Punjab agricultural labourers trebling 1961-81 and urban employment up about 50 per cent 1971-81; insecure tenants as the identified losers; employment elasticity declining; relative inequality rising with absolute gains; targeted programmes RWP, SFDA, MFAL, CSRE and Maharashtra's EGS, with employment generated rising from about 20 million person-days in the mid-1960s to 850 million in 1988-89.
- Ecology: fertiliser and pesticide damage; groundwater depletion; yield plateaus; but ecological degradation is greatest where yield-increasing technology is slow, and Swaminathan's estimate that pre-Green Revolution yields would require 80 million extra hectares, about 66 per cent more cultivable area.
- Politics: no "Red Revolution"; a beneficiary peasantry demanding prices and subsidies; below-cost and in places free power.

## 10. Agrarian struggles

- Telangana: jagirdars and deshmukhs; forced levy and *veth begar*; Andhra Mahasabha; decisive phase August 1947 to September 1948; claims of 3,000 villages; ceilings declared at 500 then 100 acres; peasant preference for ancestral and waste land; Army entry 13 September 1948; armed phase to about 1951 with perhaps 500 dead and about 10,000 jailed; Jagirdari Abolition Regulation 1949; Hyderabad Tenancy Act 1950 with over 600,000 protected tenants on over a quarter of cultivated area.
- PEPSU: biswedars over roughly 800 villages, one-sixth of the state; movement from 1939, confrontation from 1945; Praja Mandal and Brish Bhan; Lal Communist Party 1948; armed force of 30-40, at most 100; Kishangarh January 1949; PEPSU Tenancy (Temporary Provision) Act January 1952; Vesting of Proprietary Rights Act 1953 at twelve times land revenue.
- Naxalbari: United Front 2 March 1967; Konar's announcement; conference sixteen days later; Charu Mazumdar, Kanu Sanyal, Jangal Santhal; *adhiar* sharecropping; suppressed by July 1967; CPI(ML) 1969.
- Srikakulam: Jatapu and Savara; Girijan Sanghams from the 1950s with real gains; armed phase from November 1967; intense November 1968 to February 1969; annihilation line from April 1969; about 48 annihilated by government count; 1,400 arrested by mid-June 1970; leaders killed 10 July 1970.
- Land-grab movement 1969-70 across nine states with about 20,000 arrests; Bharatiya Khet Mazdoor Union at Moga, 1968.
- "New" farmers' movements: Nasik 10 November 1980, two lakh farmers, onion and sugarcane prices, two killed; Sisauli 1986 on electricity charges; organisations across Tamil Nadu, Karnataka, Punjab, U.P., Gujarat and Maharashtra; India-versus-Bharat ideology and the net-creditor claim; criticism as landowners' movements with the Shetkari Sangathana as the partial exception; not genuinely new; party entanglement from 1984 and 1987; Boat Club failure 2 October 1989; Tamil Nadu movement destroyed by repression in 1981.

## 11. Caste

- Hierarchy, untouchability and jajmani; nineteenth-century cracks through commercialisation, contractual relations, outside employment and education; Phule and Narayana Guru.
- Gandhi from 1920; Vaikom 1924-25; Guruvayur 1931-32; opposition to separate electorates in 1932; Poona Pact.
- Ambedkar: Mahar; Independent Labour Party 1936; Scheduled Castes Federation 1942; Viceroy's Executive Council; Drafting Committee chairman; law minister; conversion 1956 with half a million, some say six million; death 1956; Republican Party 1957.
- Regional strands: Ad-Dharm, Adi-Hindu, Namashudras, Jagjivan Ram's organisations; pre-independence demands against *begar*, for waste land and against disabilities such as those under the Punjab Land Alienation Act, 1900.
- Article 17; Untouchability (Offences) Act 1955; Protection of Civil Rights (Amendment) Act 1976; reservation extended continuously.
- Assertion: Dalit Panthers 1972; Marathwada agitation and the 1978 anti-Dalit riots; splits by the 1980s; BSP under Kanshi Ram and Mayawati, chief minister in 1995; Prakash Ambedkar's 1990 morcha of 500,000; the majority of Dalits nonetheless voting Congress.
- Limits: reservation bounded by about 3 per cent of the population reaching higher education and public employment; intra-Scheduled Caste competition and quotas within quotas; conversion transferring discrimination into Christianity and Islam; village Buddhist converts adding to an existing pantheon.
- The larger causes: industrialisation, agricultural growth and rural employment, urbanisation, primary education and health, anti-poverty and employment schemes, house sites begun by Indira Gandhi, adult franchise, land distribution and the breakdown of jajmani.
- Outcomes: untouchability virtually gone in urban areas and sharply reduced in rural ones; atrocities read as reactions to assertion; 1991 literacy at 64/39 nationally, 46/19 for Scheduled Castes, 39/8 in U.P. and 85/73 in Kerala.
- Backward Classes as a distinct question: intermediate castes with land access and no untouchability; Ahirs, Yadavas, Kurmis, Vokkaligas, Lingayats and Lodhas gaining from land reform; Mandal criticised for methodology and data base, and the judgements for outdated notions of caste - both attributed positions.

## 12. Women

- AIWC 1926; the movement's transformation of the image of women; the unqualified vote.
- Rau committee draft 1944; Ambedkar committee bill; opposition including President Rajendra Prasad; enactment as four Acts - Marriage 1955; Succession, Minority and Guardianship, and Adoptions and Maintenance 1956.
- Shah Bano 1985 and the asymmetry of mobilisation as evidence of the social limit of legal reform.
- Rights used and unused: the vote used intensively; inheritance largely unclaimed because of patrilocal residence; dowry surviving as the only share received; divorce increasingly used in urban areas against stigma.
- Movement phases: consolidation to the late 1960s with Partition rehabilitation, hostels and vocational centres, and the NFIW split of 1954; participation in Tebhaga through the Nari Bahini and Mahila Atma Raksha Samitis and in Telangana; mass phase from the 1970s - anti-price-rise 1973-75, SEWA under Ela Bhatt, Shahada 1972, Uttarakhand anti-liquor, Chipko from 1974, Chattisgarh 1977, Bodh Gaya 1979, Bhopal, and the Samagra Mahila Aghadi of November 1986; autonomous groups from 1974-75, International Women's Year 1975, Manushi; dowry campaign from 1979 and amendments in 1984; rape campaign after Rameeza Bee 1978, Mathura and Maya Tyagi 1980 producing the 1983 amendment; Shah Bano and Deorala 1987 divided by the communal context; institutionalisation from the 1980s through centres, research and Women's Studies.
- Policy: National Perspective Plan for Women 1988; Panchayati Raj bill 1989 passed 1993 with one-third reservation; DWACRA; Mahila Kosh.
- Record: Barmer female literacy 8 per cent; Ganjam infant mortality 164; U.P. fertility 5.1; Haryana sex ratio 865; average female schooling 1.2 years; all-India female literacy 39 per cent in 1990-91; Kerala at 86 per cent literacy, 1.8 fertility, 17 infant mortality and a ratio rising to 1,036; Himachal Pradesh from 9 per cent in 1961 to 81 per cent rural in the 10-14 group and the largest ratio gain in India; Tamil Nadu at 2.2 fertility, 58 infant mortality and about 97 per cent vaccination; national ratio falling from 972 to 927 because women gained less than men.

## 13. Communalism

- Definitions of secularism and communalism; ideology as the disease and violence as the symptom; the attributed characterisation as the Indian form of fascism.
- Phases: marginality in the 1950s with poor polling in 1952, 1957 and 1962; deaths 7, 41 and 108 in 1958, 1959 and 1961; Jabalpur 1961 and the National Integration Council; the 1962 interlude; Jan Sangh 14 to 35 to 22 seats; riots 1,070, 520 and 521 in 1964, 1969 and 1970 with 1,919, 673 and 298 killed; respite 1971-77; sharp decline during the Emergency; increase under the Janata government; endemic from 1978; violence becoming organised, prolonged, funded and geographically general.
- Social base: exhaustion of middle-class opportunity by the mid-1960s; blocked mobility; new rural bourgeois strata; the collapse of old solidarities without new ones; a moral and cultural vacuum; the exhaustion of national-movement idealism - offered with an explicit methodological warning and with no evidence of capitalist-class backing.
- Immediate causes as sparks on prepared ground; participation of the urban poor and criminal gangs; state laxity and inroads into state apparatuses; communalised sections of the police.
- Political opportunism: Congress-League in Kerala; Communists with the League and the Akali Dal; Socialists with the Jan Sangh in 1967; the 1974-75 movement; Janata 1977; Janata Dal and BJP in 1989; concessions in 1986 and 1990 aggravating rather than reducing tension.
- The Ayodhya issue's structure: why Hindu communalism needed a religious issue; the early-sixteenth-century mosque; idols in December 1949 condemned by Patel and Nehru and the structure locked; the VHP campaign from 1983; the district judge's order of 1 February 1986 with sixty-five killed in U.P. towns; the political sequence thereafter belonging to the preceding session.
- The safeguard: the Indian state has not promoted communal ideology, and comparative evidence indicates such movements cannot prevail without state support or passivity.

## 14. The post-colonial state

- Legacies: a multi-class popular movement producing a sovereign, popular-democratic national state with an open-ended class balance; anti-imperialism; democracy; equity.
- Non-contenders: feudal landlords, marginalised by land reform; international capital, never inside the ruling coalition - hence the rejection of the triple-alliance model.
- Government is not the state; implementation depends on the class balance in the other apparatuses.
- Democracy shaped development: no enclosures, collectivisation, land-tax extraction, slavery, union suppression or colonial transfer, against Britain's 85 per cent of 1801 gross domestic capital formation from colonies; net transfer to agriculture; a free press with 8,600 dailies and 33,000 periodicals against China's 16-23 million famine deaths in 1959-61; inflation's trend rate under 8 per cent a year, 1956-90; anti-poverty protection during post-1991 stabilisation.
- Record: rural poverty 58.75 per cent (1970-71) to 37.3 per cent (1993-94); total 56.25 to 36 per cent; life expectancy 32 to over 63; per capita income 2.5 times with population up over 158 per cent; literacy 18.3 per cent (1951) to 62 per cent (1997); infant mortality 146 to 71; famine ended.
- The agency problem: Congress becoming a party of governance; Gandhi's rejected disbandment proposal; the developmental bureaucracy; the left's refusal; the consequent prevalence of a capitalist developmental perspective and the occupation of democratic space by caste and communal mobilisation.
- Competitive populism from the late 1970s; the "strong state" prescription defined against authoritarianism.

## 15. Institutions

- Crisis of governability: instability, unreconciled demands, weakening order, insurgency, communal violence, extra-constitutional agitation, corruption and implementation failure - a crisis of leadership and administration, not of the state.
- Parliament: degeneration from the late 1960s; Question Hour often suspended; defections after 1967, checked in 1985, later circumvented; seven central governments falling since 1977 on losing a Lok Sabha majority.
- Cabinet: decline from 1969; the PMO as a parallel executive from Shastri and Indira Gandhi to Vajpayee.
- Judiciary: independence sustained; earlier property-rights conservatism corrected by amendment; public interest litigation; delay and cost as the worst features; the "judicial despotism" charge.
- President and Election Commission assessed positively.
- Bureaucracy: rigid, status-quoist, inordinately expanded, functionally partisan; political neutrality surviving, as West Bengal and Kerala attest.
- Police: the worst institutional condition; colonial attitude to the poor; communal bias; over 800 reported custodial deaths in 1997; the 1979 National Police Commission report never implemented.
- Armed forces: professional, non-political, heterogeneous, under civilian control, deliberately small until 1962.
- Political parties: described as the weakest link - inter- and intra-party instability, factionalism, proliferation, shifting loyalties, absent internal democracy, hand-to-mouth functioning and appeals to caste, religion and regional chauvinism; Congress organisation reduced to an increasingly ineffective electoral machine; opposition parties forming opportunistic alliances from 1967 onward, with Kerala and West Bengal as rare exceptions.
- Corruption: limited in the colonial period by the state's narrow functions; expanded with the permit-licence-quota regime, wartime shortages and then the state's enlarged developmental and regulatory role; signals in the Nehru era not acted on strategically; election funding from the late 1960s as a major source; communal and caste riots initiating hooligans into politics, producing booth-capturing and criminalisation in some states.
- Federalism: central influence rising with planning and single-party dominance and then declining; Article 356 misuse in the 1970s later checked; the Sarkaria Commission of 1980 on strong Centre and strong states; the late-1950s panchayat experiment stifled and captured.

## 16. The millennium balance sheet

- Achievements: national unity strengthened; democracy sustained and deepened; civil liberties entrenched; social transformation shown to be possible democratically; growing political awareness and autonomous voter choice; economic stagnation broken, the colonial structure ended, autonomy preserved, agriculture more than three and a half times larger since 1950, industry more than seventeen times, national income more than sevenfold and per capita income two and a half times; famine ended; reform carried through without collapse.
- Failures: poverty above three hundred million; near-half illiteracy; inequality; corruption; casteism; communalism endemic and named the chief threat to unity; institutional decay; competitive populism; the non-mobilisation of the poor; unresolved forms of protest.
- Qualifiers: regional variation in agriculture, land reform and human development; contested statistics; contested readings - nationalist-left, market-liberal, subaltern and Marxist, and critical agrarian and environmental.
- The poverty series as the source records it: about 59 per cent of the population below the poverty line in 1970-71, 51.3 per cent in 1977-78, 44.5 per cent in 1983 and 36 per cent in 1993-94, with over 300 million still below it - a number equal to India's population at independence - and wide state variation, from about 63 per cent in Bihar to about 20 per cent in Punjab and Haryana in 1993-94; the reduction attributed largely to employment-generating anti-poverty programmes begun in the mid-1970s, with Maharashtra's Employment Guarantee Scheme singled out.
- Consumption and living-standard indicators: no major famine since Bengal in 1943, and very few deaths from hunger or disease even in the worst drought of the century in 1987-88; households unable to obtain two square meals a day falling to 19 per cent in 1983 and under 5 per cent in 1994; per capita foodgrain availability rising from 394.9 grams a day in 1951 to 468.8 in 1971 and 507.7 in 1995, about 28 per cent, after a fall of over 24 per cent between 1901 and 1941; cloth availability 9 metres a head in 1950 to 29.3 metres in 1995; milk production from 17 to 68.3 million tonnes between 1950 and 1996; per capita income growth of 2.8 per cent a year from 1975 to 1995, comparing favourably with Britain, France, Germany and the United States at comparable stages.
- Closing formulation: India transformed its economy substantially, its politics profoundly and its social hierarchies least of all, and the third shortfall is why the first two produced movements rather than contentment.

## Book evidence and its controls

1. Bipan Chandra, Mridula Mukherjee and Aditya Mukherjee, *India After Independence, 1947-2000*, locally held searchable PDF at `books\India After Independence-1947-2000 By Bipan Chandra.pdf`. **Chapters 25 to 38 were extracted and read in full**, covering PDF pages 423 to 627: Chapter 25, Indian Economy 1947-1965; Chapter 26, Indian Economy 1965-1991; Chapter 27, Economic Reforms Since 1991; Chapter 28, Land Reforms - Zamindari Abolition and Tenancy Reforms; Chapter 29, Land Reforms - Ceiling and the Bhoodan Movement; Chapter 30, Land Reforms - Cooperatives and an Overview; Chapter 31, Agriculture Growth and the Green Revolution; Chapter 32, Agrarian Struggles Since Independence; Chapter 33, Revival and Growth of Communalism; Chapter 34, Caste, Untouchability, Anti-caste Politics and Strategies; Chapter 35, Indian Women Since Independence; Chapter 36, The Post-Colonial Indian State and the Political Economy of Development; Chapter 37, Disarray in Institutions of Governance; Chapter 38, On the Eve of the New Millennium.
2. Controls applied to that text:
   - several statistical tables in the extract are optically garbled, so no figure has been taken from a table image; every number used here comes from the running prose, and where prose and a garbled table could differ, the prose is used;
   - the growth rate of about 4 per cent is carried with its stated boundary of 1951 to 1964-65 and is not extended to later decades;
   - tenancy-area estimates are reproduced with the source's own warning about oral and unrecorded tenancy, and the 1971 Census conclusions are reported as the source characterises them rather than as data;
   - the Green Revolution distribution argument is reported as the source's position and is not blended with the critical literature, which is separately named;
   - the characterisation of communalism as the Indian form of fascism, the validation of the temple-entry strategy, the criticism of the Mandal data base and the assessment of the left's refusal to work inside the state are all marked as attributed judgements rather than neutral facts;
   - no casualty total is asserted beyond the figures the text itself carries, and the Telangana, Srikakulam and riot figures are reproduced with their stated provenance and with the rebel-versus-government divergence noted where the source notes it;
   - the political narrative of 1985-1992 contained in these chapters is not reproduced, because the preceding session owns it; only the structural analysis is used.
3. No other book was used for this topic. The colonial-economy and national-movement volumes held locally were not required, because the period taught here begins in 1947 and its evidence base is the post-independence survey.

The 2026 General Studies Paper I could not be text-extracted and its two cross-owned demands are carried from the audited routing ledger with that limitation stated.

## Evidence and limitation controls

- No content from excluded derived or generated package trees was read, cited or used, in accordance with the permanent live-session source exclusion.
- The learner-v2 artifacts for this topic were not opened. The file named as a complete topic package in the knowledge tree was verified by SHA-256 to be **byte-identical** to the excluded learner-v2 learning session, and was therefore treated as the same excluded artifact and not read.
- Every quantitative claim is carried with its period and, where the source qualifies its reliability, with that qualification attached.
- Contested items are named as contested and are listed in the coverage matrix's contested-evidence register rather than silently resolved.
- Attributed historiographical judgements are marked as attributed: the fascism characterisation of communalism, the validation of the temple-entry strategy, the criticism of the Mandal data base, the assessment of the left's role and the diffusion argument on the Green Revolution.
- The 2018 Prelims demand on the founders of a 1948 labour organisation is recorded as an open evidence gap; no founder, date or attribution is invented to close it.
- No answer, key, elimination or corrective mapping is supplied for any objective question, including the one directly owned demand whose official key is not held locally.
- Boundaries are marked where they are reached: political chronology to the earlier sessions of this period; present-day economic policy to Economy; contemporary caste and gender data and reservation doctrine to Indian Society and Social Justice; constitutional and institutional doctrine to Polity and Governance.
- Current-affairs items are dated, attributed to the reporting that produced them, and used only as analogues for historical mechanisms.
